Left to the clearance alone the room settles a few pixels above the true bottom, which is invisible until a message arrives: the pre-paint follow in `ChatRoom` requires `distance ≤ growth + 1` and those pixels fail it, so the follow falls through to the post-paint `pinToBottom()` and the conversation lurches by the residual on the frame after the bubble is drawn

**Media messages** (photos **and** videos in one bubble; `media.mime` is the discriminator):

- Rendered **without a bubble**; tapping opens the fullscreen viewer at that index and swipes with **native scroll snapping**, not a gesture handler. **The swipe reaches the whole conversation, not the bubble it opened on** — see 트랙 below
- Attachments sent together are **one bubble** (§ 6.) — branch on array length alone: 1 keeps its own `media.width` / `height` ratio, 2+ use a fixed square-cell grid whose height follows from the cell layout
- A video tile shows its poster, a play glyph, and its running time from `media.duration_ms`
- The `+` opens a `BottomSheet` with `사진/영상` → album and `파일` → everything else (§ 9.1.), on two inputs rather than one — an album input that accepts everything costs iOS its photo picker entirely and opens Files. A `카메라` row was dropped: `capture` and `multiple` cannot be combined, and iOS already offers the camera inside the picker. The same attachments also arrive by dropping them on the room, which is desktop-only, and by pasting them into it, which is not (§ 9.2.)
- Picked attachments stage in a tray with a per-item remove control and an edit control on every photo and video tile — a photo crops and filters, a video trims. **A file tile carries neither** (§ 9.1.): it shows its name in the same 64px square and has nothing to edit. The trimmer is § 12.1.'s, with **no `maxDurationMs`**: an attachment has no length cap, so both handles move and trimming is an edit rather than a requirement
- **Both editors turn the picture in 90° steps** (회전, `RotateCw`, in the top bar). `MediaEditor` and `VideoCropper` keep their own count rather than reading `getTransforms()`, which folds a JPEG's EXIF orientation in; `react-advanced-cropper` reports the crop against the image as turned, so `applyEdit` rotates the source on the output canvas with a context transform (never a full-size intermediate — `EDITED_MAX_EDGE`) and `cropVideo` hands the count to `mediabunny`'s `rotate`, which `Conversion` applies before `process` sees the frame. The turn survives a ratio chip's remount through `onReady`
- **`useAttachmentEditing` owns both editors and the id rule**, for chat and the library alike. A trimmed clip is re-read into a full draft (its poster, dimensions and duration all belong to the new file) but **keeps the old draft's id**, since `useMediaSelection.replace` matches on it and a fresh id would append a second tile instead of replacing the one being edited. That is a fact about `replace`, not about either screen, and it lived in two copies until they could drift
- **The trimmer stays mounted until the re-read lands, and a send is held for as long as it does.** `toMediaDraft` on a trimmed clip is a full decode bounded only by `DECODE_TIMEOUT`; dismissed first, the tray shows the untrimmed tile and an upload started in that window ships the original, with the late `replace` addressing a draft `takeAll` has already removed
- **The trimmer reads the duration off the element, not only off the draft.** `toVideoDraft` reports `null` for a fragmented MP4 and some `.mov`, and a free-range trim that fell back to a literal cut every such clip down to that literal — data loss with no message. 완료 is held until the element has resolved one
- Decoding is serial, so the tray opens on a **placeholder tile** rather than staying empty until the last file lands — a § 9.1. file stages immediately, since there is nothing to decode
- `heic`/`heif` stay on the allow-list — iOS hands the original over when it declines to transcode, and the thumbnail is produced by decoding it in an `<img>`, so an engine without HEIC support rejects the pick instead of storing something it could never render
- Sending shows real upload progress per bubble; a failure offers **재전송 / 취소**, and a retry re-uploads only what had not landed. Progress commits only when the **whole-percent** figure changes — `upload.onprogress` fires per network chunk and every commit re-renders the virtualized list
- Attachments upload through § 13.4.'s bounded pool. The **byte budget**, not the concurrency count, governs here. The `media` array is assembled **by index**, so pick order survives however the uploads interleaved. A failure **stops the queue**: nothing further launches, in-flight tasks settle, then the error is rethrown — so the retry is left exactly the remainder. A slot the retry skips is weighed as zero, or its bytes would be reserved for an upload that never runs
- **A media bubble arms the same hold every other bubble does** (§ 8.11.), and suppresses iOS's own callout. Two menus over one photo is not a choice the user can read; the app's sheet wins the hold and carries 공유, and the OS keeps the § 7.10. viewer

**The viewer's 트랙 — it spans the conversation, not the bubble:**

- **`GET /api/chat/media` is the track**, in three shapes: `?around={mediaId}` is the window the viewer opens on — every photo and video in the conversation, in send order, up to `CHAT_MEDIA_TRACK_SPAN` either side of the slide the reader tapped — and `?before={mediaId}` / `?after={mediaId}` are the pages that extend it at either edge. Exactly one of the three, or **400**: they name different windows and the query prefers `around`, so a request carrying two would have its cursor silently ignored. 파일 and 음성 are excluded, and they have to be named rather than inherited — § 6. keeps one bubble to one kind so a bubble's own cells never mix, but this track crosses bubbles and neither has a `_thumb` object or a box (§ 9.1., § 9.3.), so either one reaching a slide is a swipe into nothing
- **All three name a `media` id, never a cursor pair.** The order is a join row's `(message_id, sort_order)` and the client holds neither column — so the id is resolved to a position server-side, by the one lookup `?around=` already needed. It is also what makes a withdrawal cost the paging nothing: the edge anchor is read off the track's own first and last slide **at the moment of asking**, and § 8.13.'s narrowing has already removed a withdrawn slide from it, so the next ask measures from the surviving neighbour
- **Ordered by `(message_id, sort_order)`, never by the media id.** Every attachment of one multi-photo send shares the transaction timestamp (§ 6.), so a timestamp cannot separate them — and `sort_order` is the order the sender picked, which is the order the bubble draws them in. Any other ordering makes a swipe through one bubble disagree with the bubble it came from
- **A row hidden from 보관함 is still in the track.** § 10.'s delete takes a photo out of the library and deliberately leaves the bubble carrying it, so this reads the conversation rather than `isInLibrary` — which is also why it is a query of its own and not a `kind` on `GET /api/archive`
- **The window pages at both edges, and that is what lets the span be small.** `CHAT_MEDIA_TRACK_SPAN` is **30** a side rather than 200, because reaching the end of the window is no longer a dead end: once the reader is within `CHAT_MEDIA_PAGE_MARGIN` slides of either edge, the next stretch is fetched and merged. `MediaViewer` mounts a slide element per cell, so the DOM cost is per slide **in the track** rather than per slide on screen — at 200 a side, every open of every photo mounted four hundred slides for a reader who would swipe three, and at 500 a thousand slides made the snap track a thousand viewport-widths re-diffed on every crossing. Now that reach is only paid for by a reader who travels it, and a reader who swipes hundreds of photos in one open ends up where the old span started. There is still a ceiling on one _request_ — the query is otherwise unbounded and a long history would answer in megabytes — but the track only ends where the conversation does
  - **Only the older edge is a prepend, and it is the whole of the difficulty.** Slides inserted at the front move every slide after them, so the reader's `scrollLeft` stops naming the photo they were on; a newer page lands past them and moves nothing. § 10.'s grid solved this shape first, and the two halves of its answer are reused here
  - **The page is fetched during the swipe and inserted only once the track is still**, through § 8.3.'s `useSettledCommit`, which moved into `shared` for § 10. and has this as its third caller. WebKit hands the scroll offset to the compositor for the length of a gesture, so a correction landing mid-flick is deferred to a scroll that has already moved on. The wait is cheap here in a way it is not on a list: a `snap-mandatory` track goes still between swipe gestures, so the settle arrives about once per slide
  - **The correction is the offset the reader's own slide resolves to, where § 10.'s grid has to use a size delta.** Every slide is exactly one track width and `MediaViewer` already derives the reader's logical position from the held slide's `id` during render (below) — so after a page of _n_ lands in front of them, that position **is** `index + n` and `scrollLeft = index × width` is exact. § 10. could not do this: its prepend regroups the lines it merges into and leaves no row to re-find, which is why it compares total sizes. An absolute offset is the safer of the two — it cannot double-apply on top of a correction the engine did make, and a delta can
  - **It is applied before paint**, in a layout effect. Passive, the frame between the commit and the correction paints whichever slide the unmoved offset now points at, which is a page-width back
  - **The newer page is held under the same gate even though it needs no correction**, because the correction is keyed on the track's length and cannot tell an append from a prepend. Ungated it would be a length change under a moving finger, which is the thing that must not happen at all
  - **Paging is 채팅's alone and reaches `MediaViewer` as a prop.** The library's viewer swipes the loaded library and the § 10. grid behind it does that paging; a second pager on top would fetch rows the grid is already fetching and insert them into a snapshot the grid never sees. `useViewerTrack` owns **what** is in the track and the component owns **when** a page may land, since only it can see its own scroller — the same split `useArchiveMedia` and `ArchiveGrid` have
  - **A held page and a reopen do not commute**, so the track carries § 8.6.1.'s generation token exactly as § 10.'s shelf does. Closing the viewer or opening another photo bumps it; a page in flight for the old open is discarded when it lands, and one already held is dropped, because it is a stretch of a track that no longer exists. **A held page and a withdrawal do not commute either**: the page can carry slides of the very message being withdrawn, so § 8.13.'s narrowing filters the held pages as well as the loaded track — committed unexamined they would arrive back into it a moment later, offering 메시지 삭제 over a message that is already gone. Filtering what is **held** is only half of it, though: a page — or the opening window — can have been asked for before the withdrawal and answer after it, which no filter over `heldRef` can reach. So the narrowing also **remembers the message id**, and both fetches filter what they get through that set before storing it. The generation token cannot stand in for it: a withdrawal is not a reopen, so bumping it would discard a page fetched for the track the reader is still holding, and `isLoadingRef` is only cleared for the generation that set it — the edge that was mid-request would page no further for the rest of the open
  - **Paging is disarmed until the window lands**, and both edges start closed. Before it the track is the bubble's own attachments, nine cells at most (§ 6.), so the viewer reports the reader as near both edges at once — and a **draft** has no stored row for an anchor to name at all. Whether an edge may have more is read off the window's own shape rather than reported by the server, the way § 10. reads its upward edge: the anchor's distance from each end **is** how many rows came back that way, and a side that filled `CHAT_MEDIA_TRACK_SPAN` is the same "there may be more" test every later page is judged by. Which is why the window's newer half asks for one row more than the span — it spends a row on the anchor itself, and without that it would report the newer edge exhausted on every open
  - **The ask is keyed on the slide the reader is on, never on a scroll frame**, so it cannot page itself into a loop the way § 8.3. warns a render-scoped effect does: the position moves once per crossing, and the commit that answers moves the edge away again
- **The viewer opens on the bubble's own attachments and swaps the window in when it arrives**, so the tap costs no round trip and the photo is on screen immediately. The swap is applied only if it is still the **open** it was requested for — a fetch outlives a close, and applied blindly it would replace the _next_ open's track and throw the reader to an unrelated slide. That is the generation token above, and it replaced a comparison against the slide the viewer still held: the reader swiping to a sibling before the window lands is not that failure at all, since a bubble's attachments are contiguous in the track and `MAX_MEDIA_PER_MESSAGE` is far inside the span, so the slide they moved to is in the window too and the reconciliation below lands them on it. Refusing there instead cost them the window — and, now, paging for the rest of the open. A failure is silent: the bubble's own cells are a working viewer, and a toast would report the loss of something never asked for
- **`MediaViewer` reconciles the offset by the held slide's `id`, not by the track's length.** That is what tells a swap or a page from a delete: both are a different array of a different length whose current slide is still on screen, and read as a delete either would fling the reader hundreds of photos away. Found → the reader stays on the same photo and only the slides around it appeared; gone → slides were removed under them, and the reader is **re-pointed at the id of the surviving neighbour** rather than left on an offset
  - **The neighbour is walked out of the track as the previous render drew it**, which is the only array the removed slide still has a position in, so the viewer keeps one snapshot of it. Counting survivors off the reader's old DOM offset is correct for a single slide and wrong for every larger removal: 메시지 삭제 takes the whole bubble, 2 to 9 slides (§ 6.), and an offset left where it stood skips every one of them that sat after the slide on screen — four photos, on a bubble of nine with the reader on the fifth. The walk goes forward first and backward only at the end of the track, which is `DESIGN.md § 7.10.`'s "the next photo is already under them"
  - **It is settled during the render, not in an effect.** The offset is what the slides, the chrome and the neighbour preloads are all keyed on, so it has to be right on the render that draws them — and a `setState` in an effect is the cascading render the React Compiler refuses. That leaves the delete with nothing in the offset to recognise it by afterwards, so the render that lost the held slide **records** that it did: § 18. #6.'s zoom reset is keyed on that flag, a commit later, rather than on the position
  - **The caller therefore hands `cells` back narrowed and nothing else.** Neither 채팅's track nor 보관함's snapshot clamps its own `index`: that field is `initialIndex`, read once at mount, and the reconciliation above is the only thing that moves a reader afterwards. One question is answered in one place
- **`initialIndex` is the offset the viewer _opened_ at and is asserted once, at mount.** Every later move belongs to the reconciliation above. It used to be re-asserted whenever the prop changed, which was masked only by effect ordering — both were passive and the mount scroll was declared first, so the reconciliation ran second and won. The offset correction is a layout effect now, so a re-run would land _after_ it and scroll the reader to a position they left long ago: the window swap rewrites that number, and so does § 8.13.'s narrowing. 보관함 already relied on the same rule from the other side (§ 10.)
- **A step is a swipe by another input, and there are two of them: `ArrowLeft` / `ArrowRight`, and the pair of arrows `DESIGN.md § 7.10.` draws at the sides of the slide.** Both do one thing — scroll the track by one slide width — so `handleScroll` names the slide they arrive at and everything above follows without knowing which input moved the reader. Neither is gated on the pointer: `AGENTS.md § 4.2.` allows a per-device control set for `useIsIos` alone, so the arrows ride the chrome and appear on every device
  - **The keys reach the viewer through `useModalOverlay`, not through a listener of its own.** That hook already answers "is anything open over me" for `Escape` and the `Tab` cycle, and it is the same question here: the § 8.11. share dialog, the § 7.4. bundle modal and the § 12.1. background sheet all open **over** the viewer, and a second copy of the predicate would swipe the track behind whichever one is up. It forwards every key it does not own and `preventDefault`s nothing itself
  - **A step measures from the step already in flight, never from the live `scrollLeft`.** Held down, an arrow key repeats several times faster than a smooth scroll completes, and mid-animation the track's own offset still rounds to the slide being left — so every repeat would re-issue the step already running and the viewer would sit on the next photo for as long as the key was down. The pending offset is dropped three ways, and **arrival is only one of them**: a step that is cut short never reaches the offset it named, so it is dropped on `pointerdown` and `wheel` as well — the two inputs that interrupt a smooth scroll, and the two that never fire while one runs on its own. Left to arrival alone, a reader who pressed → and swiped back mid-animation had the next press measured from a slide they had turned away from and travelled there instead of one across. The offset correction above drops it too, its commit having moved the slide that offset named
- **The bubble the reader is inside is drawn as a filmstrip in the bottom chrome** (`DESIGN.md § 7.10.`), and the strip is a scrubber: its centre is a notch, and the thumbnail passing under it becomes the slide on screen **while the drag is still under way** — instantly, since a smooth scroll per frame lags the finger and queues behind the crossings after it. The row is dragged rather than scrolled (`useInertialStrip`), which is what makes a crossing this component reported distinguishable from one the viewer sends back — as a scroller it could only guess, and the wrong guess looped the strip against the track until the tab was killed. A tap on a thumbnail is the same step by another input — it names its destination where an arrow counts one across, and reaches the track through the same pending-offset path, so `handleScroll` still names the slide it arrives at. It is the same contiguous run the position counts, so a bubble of one draws nothing. The active thumbnail is **centred** on the open and eased back to the centre on every crossing made elsewhere, and a mouse drags the row by the same pointer events a finger does
- **Every per-slide control resolves through the bubble that owns the slide**, not through the one the viewer was opened from. 메시지 삭제 is offered on **my own** messages only (§ 8.13.) and is therefore withheld per slide, which is a control changing under a swipe rather than one absent for the whole open
- **원본 저장 offers the rest of the bubble when there is a rest of it.** A bubble carrying several attachments (§ 6.) answers the tap with the § 7.4. modal — 이 사진만 / 모두 저장 — and a single attachment saves on the one tap it always did, since a question with one answer is not worth asking. **The bundle is the bubble, never the track**: the sender picked those together and § 6. sent them as one row, which is the only grouping the reader ever saw, where "모두" over a conversation-wide track would be hundreds of files. **Every sentence about 모두 is named by the _bundle's_ kind, and only 이 사진만 by the slide's** — § 6. lets one bubble carry photos and videos together, so a slide-named heading offered to save 동영상 and then saved the photos beside it, which misdescribes the action rather than merely labelling it. `toMediaKind` calls a mixed set `photo`, the same rule the § 10. shelf follows when it counts a selection carrying both. The count's particle comes from `josa` (`AGENTS.md § 0.4.`) and its unit from `toMediaCountUnit` — 장 for photos and for a mixed bubble, 개 for one that is all video. The bundle **outlives the modal's dismissal and openness is a separate flag**, for the reason § 10.'s delete confirmation records: all three sentences are derived from it, so clearing it on either answer fades the modal out with no title, no description and a blank 이 사진만. The **question** is 채팅's alone — a flat library has no bundle to offer — but the **handler** is now passed by both viewers, because 보관함 had to stop reaching the object through a bare `<a href>` (below)
- **Every 원본 저장 probes the route before it navigates, and 보관함's anchor is gone for it.** § 18. #1.'s 삭제 is not scoped to the uploader and nothing publishes it — there is no channel for `media`, and the library tab holds no § 8.4. stream — so a screen the other participant deleted from goes on showing rows whose objects are gone, until it is reloaded. Reaching one through an anchor navigated a standalone PWA to a bare JSON 404 with no way back, which is the same failure `toMediaDownloadUrl` carries its `download=1` to avoid cross-origin. `downloadMedia` asks first with `redirect: "manual"`, where a 302 surfaces as an opaque response and a 404 as an ordinary one — so no byte of the object is fetched to find out — and says `원본을 찾지 못했어요` instead of leaving. A **thrown** `fetch` answers yes: that is the network failing rather than the object being gone, and refusing there would report a deletion that never happened
  - **The stale tile itself is left alone, deliberately.** Making 보관함 live would want a `media` trigger, a channel and an SSE connection on a tab that has none, and it would buy a thumbnail disappearing a few seconds sooner. What it must not do is fail _silently_ or destructively, which is what the probe answers. The slide beside it already degrades on its own — every image in the viewer is a `PreloadImage`, so an object that 404s retries once and then draws its `ImageOff` box rather than a broken element
- **A bubble paged out of the loaded window has no bundle to offer**, which the conversation-wide track makes ordinary — the reader can swipe to a photo whose message is no longer in memory. That slide saves alone rather than claiming a size it cannot see
- **A withdrawal narrows the track instead of dismissing it** (§ 8.13.), and so does confirming 메시지 삭제 from inside the viewer. Both used to close it, which was right while the track was the one bubble being deleted — there was nothing left to look at. Spanning the conversation, closing would throw the reader out of a photo stream over one bubble leaving it; the slides go and the next photo is already under them (`DESIGN.md § 7.10.`). It closes only when the last slide goes

**The fullscreen viewer:**

- Pinch zoom — § 18. #6. `usePinchZoom` in `shared/lib`: **1x–4x, double tap toggling 2x**, panning while zoomed, rubber-banding past either bound and settling back on release. **The gesture surface and the transformed element must stay two elements** — `getBoundingClientRect` reports the transformed box, so a surface that scaled with the photo would measure its own zoom back into the pan bounds and tighten the clamp every frame. For the same reason **every** `withClampedOffset` call has to be given the element: the parameter is optional in the signature and the no-element branch recentres, so the one call that omitted it silently discarded the pan on every finger lift. **`touch-action` is `pan-x` at rest and `none` once a gesture owns the finger — never `auto`, and never unconditionally `none`.** `none` at rest would eat the native scroll snapping the swipe half is built on (§ 8.1.). `auto` is worse and less obvious: the two fingers of a pinch rarely land in the same frame, and with the scroller free to claim the first one the browser `pointercancel`s it, so the pointer count never reaches 2 and the pinch silently swipes to the next photo instead of zooming. `pan-x` permits exactly the track's own gesture and reserves the rest, so the second finger still reaches JS; the track only ever scrolls horizontally, so nothing is given up. A zoomed slide freezes the track with `overflow-x-hidden`, which holds `scrollLeft` so the slide is still the one that was zoomed when the finger lifts, and arriving at another slide drops the zoom. **Movement is tracked at every scale, not only while panning**: two quick swipes land inside the `A_SECOND / 3` double-tap window (§ 13.6.) and would otherwise read as a double tap on the slide the reader was leaving. A pinch does not degrade into a pan when the first finger leaves — the surviving pointer has no origin, and adopting it jumps the photo by the distance between the two. The pan is clamped against the element box rather than the painted photo inside it, since `naturalWidth` is unavailable until a cold slide decodes. **The double _click_ cancels the second `mousedown`'s default**, which is what stops the browser selecting the photo it has just zoomed. `detail > 1` only, so the first click keeps every default it has — and never `user-select: none` or `draggable={false}`: this is the one surface that leaves the OS its own hold gesture (§ 8.11.), and iOS governs that menu off the same family of properties, so suppressing the selection declaratively is how 사진에 저장 disappears on a change that reads as purely cosmetic. **The desktop half of the gesture is `ctrl`+`wheel`, taken by the hook rather than left to the browser.** macOS reports a trackpad pinch that way, and every browser spends it on **page** zoom — which resizes the visual viewport under a `fixed` layer while the layout viewport stays put, so `ShellOverlay` (§ 4.4.) is left offset from the screen it is drawing over and the whole viewer appears to tear. Taking the gesture is what keeps the browser out of it: the wheel delta scales around the cursor by the same `zoomAround` the double tap uses, `preventDefault` stops the page zoom, and the stream falling quiet for `A_SECOND / 8` stands in for the `pointerup` there is none of. It has to be a **manually attached** listener — React registers `onWheel` passively at the root, where `preventDefault` is ignored and the page zooms anyway — and it goes on the **overlay root** (`captureRoot`) rather than on the gesture surface. A `wheel` targets the topmost element under the cursor, so one over a video slide, a placeholder, the padding beside a photo or a floating control never reaches that surface, and each of those was the page zoom this exists to refuse. The refusal is therefore unconditional and the zoom is not: a slide with no gesture surface has nothing to scale, and scaling it anyway would set `isZoomed` and freeze the track over a photo that never moved. A bare wheel is left alone: that is the trackpad's two-finger scroll, which the track still wants. **The bounds themselves still want tuning on a real device**; they are constants at the top of the hook so tuning is a one-line change
- Tapping the scroll-to-bottom button scrolls smoothly to the bottom **and marks messages read** — a **forced** `markRead` off `useChatStream`, past § 8.8.'s throttle, since the message the reader travelled to commonly arrived inside that window. Only on a restore that landed, like the scroll it follows (§ 8.6.1.)
- The same button returns the user to the newest messages after a search jump (§ 8.6.1.)

### 8.2. Message Loading ✅

Cursor-based infinite scroll: `GET /api/messages?before={id}&limit=30` (`WHERE id < :before ORDER BY id DESC`), plus `after={id}` and `around={id}`, whose callers are § 8.4. and § 8.6.1.

- **No OFFSET pagination, ever** — incoming messages shift page boundaries, producing duplicates and gaps
- `newestKnownId` is tracked alongside `oldestLoadedId` and only ever moves forward, so a delete cannot walk it back and make § 8.4.'s catch-up refetch what was already seen
- **The cursor is a snowflake carried as a string, and it is compared with `compareId`** (§ 6.). `<` / `>` / `Math.max` on it are wrong on a value TypeScript is happy to accept — every id the epoch can mint is 19 digits, so lexicographic order agrees with numeric order today and would stop agreeing the moment that width changed. `"0"` is the "from the start of the conversation" cursor and names no row

### 8.3. Virtual Scrolling (Windowing) ✅

Offscreen message nodes **must not stay in the DOM** — after years of history, thousands of infinite-scrolled nodes destroy scroll performance in iOS Safari. `@tanstack/react-virtual`: `anchorTo: "end"`, `getItemKey` on the row key, `measureElement` for variable heights, `scrollMargin` for the loading header, `scrollToIndex({ align: "center" })` for jumps. Migrated off `react-virtuoso`, whose `firstItemIndex` model cost a long tail of traps (an unguarded rAF recursion that made `GET /chat` a **500** among them) and which did not measure in a background tab at all.

**Structural constraints**

- **`flex-direction: column-reverse` is not available here.** It anchors a list from the bottom only because the browser lays items out in normal flow; every row here is `position: absolute`. Buying it back means abandoning absolute positioning and inverting every `item.start`. `transform: scaleY(-1)` is no escape either — a transform flips painting, not input, so the gesture ends up reversed

**Anchoring**

- **The prepend anchor is the row key, and it must be stable.** `getItemKey` returns `ChatRow.key`; an index-based key cannot work, since every existing row takes a new index the moment a page lands in front of it
- **The room re-anchors the prepend itself, and never on `rows[0]`.** At the top of the list index 0 is a **date divider**; a page of older messages from the same day leaves that divider at index 0 with the same key and offset, so the correction computes as "nothing moved", the whole page lands as drift, and the room walks backwards through history a page at a time. `insertOlder` records the first **non-date** row and an effect restores it. The target is **absolute, never a delta**, so it cannot double-apply on top of a correction the virtualizer did make
- **The anchor is written only while a page is actually pending.** The settle watcher listens for every scroll that goes still, not only the ones a prepend is waiting on, so an unguarded `insertOlder` records a fresh anchor each time the reader stops — and the next unrelated `rows` change (a live arrival, a send, a delete) restores them to wherever they last paused. The guard is the pending page's own length
- **`anchorTo: "end"` holds the end; it does not go there.** At mount every row is still `ESTIMATED_ROW_HEIGHT`, so one `scrollToEnd()` lands on an end the first measurement moves. The room re-parks on the scroller's own maximum each render until a real gesture (`wheel` / `touchstart` / `keydown`) takes over — never gated on the at-bottom flag, which the edge sync has already read as false from the pre-park position
- **The rows are held `invisible` until that park has run against real heights.** The estimate→actual correction is a scroll the reader would otherwise watch happen: the room paints at the estimates, the `ResizeObserver` delivers — on WebKit _after_ that paint — and the re-park shifts everything up by the accumulated error. Two `requestAnimationFrame`s and the box is released, which is a timer rather than a measurement signal on purpose: `itemSizeCache` is already populated by the ref's cached-size answer before any real measurement, a stable total size needs a commit that may never come, and a gate waiting on a signal it does not get leaves the room blank. `visibility`, never `display` — a box out of layout is one the `ResizeObserver` never reports on, which would hold the gate shut with itself. A room that loaded **empty** starts with the gate open: it has no window to park, and its scroller mounts on the first arrival, which the gate would otherwise hold back for two frames
- **That park is a layout effect, never a passive one.** Passive runs after paint, so the room's first frame would be the top of the loaded window rather than the newest message. It also has to precede the two passive effects that read the scroller back: from a pre-park `scrollTop` of `0` the § 6.7. pill flashes on every open and `requestAdjacentPages` fetches a page of history nobody asked for. React runs every layout effect before any passive one, which is the whole ordering
- **Nothing the virtualizer measures knows about the trailing spacer.** `ListFooter` is `--chat-bottom-gap` of the scroll range and is not a measured row, so `scrollToIndex`-based ends land that far short and park the newest message behind the composer. Following the live edge uses the scroller's own `scrollHeight`, keyed on the **last row's key** changing so a page of older history does not trip it
- **A pin made against an estimate has to be renewed once the row is measured.** The follow above and the post-send pin both run before the new row has ever been in the DOM, so both resolve against `estimateRowHeight`; whatever the row then measures — a § 8.9. card resolving on the link that was just sent, a photo's real box, a bubble that wrapped one line further — grows the list _below_ the fold, and the message the user sent finishes under the composer. That is what "sending does not scroll down" was. A `ResizeObserver` on the rows' own box re-pins, **thresholded on the growth itself** exactly as § 8.12.'s slot is: `scrollHeight` already counts the growth, so being within it of the end afterwards is the condition of having been at the end before, and a § 8.3. page of older history landing thousands of pixels from the end cannot satisfy it

**Measurement**

- **Rows are measured by the `ResizeObserver`, never by the `ref`.** `measureElement` is a ref callback running inside React's commit; measuring there applies a scroll correction and asks for a synchronous re-render, which React cannot do mid-commit (`flushSync was called from inside a lifecycle method`) — so the correction lands a frame before the rows that move with it. The `measureElement` option therefore returns the **cached** size when handed no `ResizeObserverEntry`, reducing the ref to registering the element
- **Wrapping is counted by `@chenglou/pretext`, not arithmetic.** `countTextLines` breaks the string the way the browser will. `ceil(totalWidth / maxWidth)` is right for Hangul, CJK and spaced Latin, but a run that cannot break — **a URL above all** — is pushed down whole, so the line before it ends early. **It is counted under `word-break: keep-all`**, which is `DESIGN.md § 4.2.3.`'s app-wide setting and is _not_ the library's default: under `normal` a long 어절 splits between syllables where the browser pushes it to the next line whole, so the estimate lands a line short on about a fifth of wrapping Korean bubbles at a phone-width shell — always short, never long, which is the accumulating direction this section's estimate exists to avoid. Reverts to the arithmetic on the server, where there is no canvas — and that path models neither mode, which is tolerable only because it is transient
- **A row holding inline emoticons (§ 13.6.) is priced from the map the page came down with, read synchronously** (`RowEstimateContext.readInlineEmoticon`, shaped like `readPreview`). The estimate runs for every row in the window inside `getItemKey`'s memoized pass, so an answer that arrives afterwards is a re-measure of a row the reader is already looking at. It reads the map `MessageRow` draws from and must go on doing so: fed from two sources, the estimate and the bubble disagree about the box by construction. **An optional prop is what hides a missing wire**: that reader and both of `MessageRow`'s inline props were optional, all three went unpassed, and the screen compiled, linted and drew the placeholder character with nothing to say so — making the row's required is what then turned up a third call site nobody had counted
- **A row still in the outbox is priced from the queue, behind the page's map rather than in front of it.** A pending row carries its emoticons whole (§ 13.6.) and the map learns them only when the echo lands, so a lone mini in an optimistic bubble was priced at nothing and corrected by its whole box the frame after it mounted — on the newest row, at the moment of sending. The send publishes what it is carrying and the estimate reads the queue behind that as well, which is what covers a queued send revived from § 16.2.'s snapshot by a page that never published it. **The page's map wins wherever both answer**: the echo is the authority on a § 13.4. version bump or a deletion, and the queue is the only place a message nobody has sent yet exists. The queue is read through a **ref written during render** rather than a memo, for `keyedRowsRef`'s reason — `pending` takes a new identity on every upload progress tick, and a dependency on it re-estimates every row in the room on each of them
- **The obvious way to measure such a box is wrong and fails silently.** `prepareRichInline` discards any item whose text trims to nothing and takes its `extraWidth` with it, so the atomic `{ text: "", break: "never", extraWidth }` measures every line as if the emoticons were not on it — short, which is this section's accumulating direction, and reads as correct in review. The box is a real one-character item whose own advance is measured once per font and subtracted, since `occupiedWidth` is `naturalWidth + extraWidth`
- **The box is pinned to one line rather than measured per line.** `walkRichInlineLineRanges` would let a line grow to its tallest emoticon and price that, but only while the render agrees line for line with the measurer — a disagreement about _which_ line a box landed on then becomes a height error that compounds. Pinned, every line is one `LINE.body()` and a mis-wrapped line costs one line and stops
- **The plain-text path is held byte-identical rather than merged into the new one.** Text with no placeholder still goes through `countTextLines`, and the run walker is entered only once a placeholder has actually produced an emoticon segment — merged, every message ever sent would be re-priced by a measurer that agrees with the old one on `word-break` and on nothing else
- **`rich-inline` models `white-space: normal` and the bubble is `pre-wrap`, and the two halves it misses cost differently.** Hard lines it cannot see, so the caller splits on `\n` and sums with an empty line counting as one — **except a trailing newline, which the browser lays out no line box for at all**: `오늘\n` is one line and was priced as two, and only the last empty hard line qualifies, since a _leading_ newline's empty line does render. Runs of spaces it collapses, so they are pre-expanded — the first left a space to keep the break opportunity where the browser has it and the rest made non-collapsible, because collapsed the text measures narrower than it draws. Both were settled against real layout by a harness that runs this module in a browser over a stage styled as the bubble is, not by reading the library
- **Known open: `1lh` and the measured line height differ on WebKit.** `measureLineHeight` reports the used value, which Safari floors, where the inline box's `1lh` resolves to the computed one — so the box is up to 0.75px wider than it is priced at. The row's height is unaffected, the strut governing the line, and the solo case is exact, its wrapper carrying an integer `line-height`. Quantified over 400 strings: a wrap flips only where a box-bearing line has under 0.75px of slack, which was about 5% of lines, and when it flips the estimate is **short**
- **Anything the estimate wraps at a computed width must actually be constrained to it.** The `DESIGN.md § 6.5.` system-notice pill is a flex item, whose automatic minimum size is min-content; `overflow-wrap: break-word` does not reduce that (only `anywhere` does), so without `min-w-0` a spaceless event title overflows the row instead of wrapping and `toNoticeHeight` counts a line the reader never sees
- **Line heights are measured off the page, never mirrored from `theme.css`.** The stylesheet says `15px` at `1.45`; Chrome lays that out at 21.75 and Safari at 21 — WebKit floors a fractional line-height. `measureLineHeight` renders four lines in the class and divides; **not `getComputedStyle`**, which reports the computed line-height rather than the one layout used
- **The estimate is rounded**, because `measureElement` stores `Math.round(borderBoxSize)`. A row estimated _perfectly_ at 41.75 measures 42 and corrects the scroll by 0.25 — once per row, always the same direction, a visible creep over a screenful
- **`estimateSize` is per row and must stay close, because a wrong estimate _moves the list_.** Every row first measured above the fold corrects the scroll by its own estimate error, and WebKit drops that correction mid-gesture — one flat guess makes reading back through history drift and snap back when the finger stops. `estimateRowHeight` resolves an attachment (`toMediaBoxHeight`) and an emoticon (`toEmoticonBox`) **exactly**, by sharing the arithmetic the bubble is drawn at; only text is approximated. `VOICE_CARD_HEIGHT` sits beside `FILE_CARD_HEIGHT` for the same reason (§ 9.3.) — a waveform has no intrinsic height for an estimate to derive one from — and `toMediaBoxHeight` tests `voice` **first**, since a voice bubble is one clip and the cell count never enters into it
- **`getItemKey` must be one stable function, and it must index defensively.** `virtual-core` memoizes the entire measurement pass on its identity, so a fresh closure per render re-runs `estimateSize` for every unmounted row — and that is now a text layout. It reads this render's rows through a **ref written during render** (an effect-written one is a commit behind), and it answers the index when the row is missing: `indexFromElement` returns `-1` for an element with no `data-index` and the library hands that straight back, where `rows[-1].key` would throw out of the render phase
- **Anything the estimate reads from the DOM is read in the scroller's callback ref, never during render and never a commit later.** The width and `warmLineHeights`' probes are both taken there, so the first render that draws a row already wraps against the real column; a layout effect is one commit behind, and that commit re-estimates every unmeasured row against `DEFAULT_CONTENT_WIDTH` first and re-parks behind it. The width is state from then on, kept current by a `ResizeObserver` for the rotation nothing else here would notice. The server has no scroller and so uses literals, which is what keeps it agreeing with the first client render at hydration

**Timing**

- **A page of older history is fetched during the scroll but inserted only once the scroller is still.** WebKit hands the scroll offset to the compositor for the length of a gesture, so a correction landing mid-flick is deferred to a scroll that has moved on — a blank frame, a jump into the past, then a snap back. `loadOlder` holds its page and `useSettledCommit` inserts it after `SETTLE_DELAY` of no `scroll` and no finger down. The loading header stays up across the wait, and a window replacement (§ 8.6.1.) **discards** the held page rather than splicing live-edge history into a jumped window
- **A § 8.9. link card is resolved a page ahead of the viewport, never on the bubble that needs it.** `useLinkPreviewPrefetch` asks the moment a message is in the loaded window, so the bubble is its final height the first time it is measured. A card arriving later grows the row while the reader is looking at it, and no scroll compensation can hide that
- **The default re-measure compensation is wrong for this list**, and `shouldAdjustScrollPositionOnItemSizeChange` replaces it. The library declines to compensate a row re-measured while the scroll direction is `backward` — but reading back through history is exactly that. A row **entirely** above the fold is compensated whichever way the finger moved; a row still spanning it grew below the anchor and must not be
- **§ 8.5.'s AI 질문 모드 toggle suppresses that override outright, for one settle window rather than per frame.** `SelectableRow`'s gutter and every bubble's `max-width` both animate on entry and exit, so the `ResizeObserver` delivers a real resize for each mounted row on each frame of the transition — compensating every one of those would jitter the scroll instead of settling it. `ChatRoom` arms a ref for `SELECTION_TRANSITION_SETTLE` (`SelectableRow`'s own constant) on the toggle, `shouldAdjustScrollPositionOnItemSizeChange` answers `false` unconditionally while it holds, and once it clears the room force-remeasures every still-mounted row (`virtualizer.measureElement` on each) and re-parks at the live edge if that is where the reader was — one settled correction in place of the frames the suppression swallowed
- **Paging must never be driven from a render-scoped effect.** Both loads commit messages, which renders, which would ask again — one page per render rather than one per landed page, and the room pages itself to the start of history with the main thread pinned. Flags refresh every render (`readScrollEdges`); paging runs on `scroll` and on the load finishing (`requestAdjacentPages`)
- **The box for a media message is reserved before the asset loads** — an aspect-ratio box from `media.width` / `height` for a single attachment, the cell layout for a grid. Without it every arriving image triggers a re-measure cascade

**The date indicator**

- **Date dividers are list items and that is the whole of it — there is no floating day indicator, and one must not be added back.** A `DayIndicator` overlay shipped and was removed: it read the day off the first row below the top fade and floated it under the § 7.12. header while the list moved. It is gone because a label pinned to the screen rather than to the conversation is chrome the reader did not ask for, and it covered the top of the message column — the § 8.10. swipe target and the § 8.11. hold target. The dividers say which day a message belongs to, and scrolling to one is how the reader asks
- Native `Ctrl+F` cannot find offscreen messages — in-app search (§ 8.6.) is the deliberate replacement, and it has landed

### 8.4. Realtime (SSE) ✅

`GET /api/chat/stream` holds one unpooled connection on both channels, replays from `Last-Event-ID`, heartbeats; the client holds a single `EventSource`, closes it on background, catches up on every return.

**`GET /api/chat/stream`** — `text/event-stream`, `runtime = "nodejs"`, `maxDuration = 300`

- The connection comes from the **direct (unpooled)** string via `listenToChannels` (`shared/db`) and is released in a `finally`. A transaction-mode pooler hands the connection to another client between transactions, silently dropping the `LISTEN`
- **One endpoint, one `EventSource`, four channels.** `LISTEN user_changed`, `LISTEN typing` (§ 8.12.) and `LISTEN message_changed` (§ 8.13.) ride the same connection; they are told apart by the SSE `event:` field (`message`, `user`, `typing`, `delete`, `edit`)
- `LISTEN` is registered **before** the replay query. Reversed, a message committing between the two is missed by both
- **`id > cursor` alone loses messages**: an id is taken at INSERT but becomes visible at COMMIT, so rows commit out of order. Replay starts at `idFloorBefore(cursor, SSE_REPLAY_MARGIN)` and lets id-deduplication drop the overlap. **The margin is a duration, not a row count** — what it has to cover is how long a write can stay uncommitted, and a snowflake carries the millisecond it was minted at, so the window is expressed in the unit it actually has (it was `20` rows while ids came from a sequence)
- **The `new_message` and `message_changed` payloads carry the id as a JSON _string_** (`NEW.id::text` in both triggers). A bigint written as a JSON number is read back by `JSON.parse` as a double, which rounds every snowflake — `getMessage` then finds no row and the stream drops the notification with no error raised anywhere
- `id:` goes on **message events only** — it is the reconnect cursor, a `messages` snowflake with no counterpart on `user`, `build` or `ping`. § 8.13.'s `delete` and `edit` name a real id and still carry none: theirs may be arbitrarily old, and the cursor may only ever advance
- **A replayed row is `event: backfill`, a live one is `event: message`.** Same payload, same `id:`, same client-side deduplication — the split exists because § 13.6.'s emoticon sound must tell them apart. Every reconnect replays, so without it a resume plays the sounds of messages already seen
- Notifications resolve **one at a time behind a promise chain** — each costs a `getMessage` query, and interleaving emits rows out of id order
- `event: ping` at `SSE_HEARTBEAT_INTERVAL`. A **named event, not a `:ping` comment**: a comment keeps proxies awake but `EventSource` never surfaces it, and the client's resume path must observe the heartbeat to tell a live socket from a frozen one
- Replay is capped at `SSE_REPLAY_LIMIT`; what falls past the cap is the client catch-up's problem, so a truncated replay costs extra requests rather than a hole

**`GET /api/users`** — the whole participant set **plus § 12.2.'s shared wallpaper id**, **no cursor**. Callers: first render, a `user` event, the resume catch-up. It answers more than its path says because it answers everything one `user_changed` invalidates (§ 6.)

- `listUsers` projects an explicit column set and `toParticipant` resolves the name before the response is built, so **`email` and `google_sub` never leave the server**. Never `SELECT *` here
- A "changed since" cursor is **rejected**: this is a small mutable set, not an append-only log — a rename produces no new row so an id cursor never fires, and an `updated_at` cursor still misses a deletion

**Client**

- **The state belongs to the `(main)` shell; the socket does not.** `ChatStreamProvider` owns the participant set, the unread count and the typing set, because the calendar, the profile screen and the tab bar all read them. The `EventSource` is mounted by `ChatStreamConnection` on the chat screen alone (§ 8.4.2.); screens attach to the delivered events with `useChatStreamListener`
- The provider delivers the message and moves the counters (tab-bar badge, `navigator.setAppBadge`). It **alerts nobody** — § 16.1.'s OS notification is the app's one alerting channel. A message that is mine moves nothing at all, since the stream echoes my own send back (§ 8.5.)
- `EventSource` auto-reconnects only for drops it notices. A fatal error (a 401, or a body that is not `text/event-stream`) leaves `readyState === CLOSED` permanently, so `onerror` reopens by hand after `SSE_RETRY_DELAY` and the open path treats a `CLOSED` source as no source. Guarding on "a source object exists" instead kills live delivery for the life of the page after one expired session. Handlers are read through a ref, so a new handler identity cannot rebuild the connection every render
- The stream closes when the tab backgrounds, so Neon compute can autosuspend. **A backgrounding is not the only close** — `blur` drops it for a window that never backgrounds at all, § 8.4.1. for idleness, § 8.4.2. on leaving 채팅. The `blur` one is the only close § 8.4.1.'s switch can disable
- **Resume is the normal sync path, not an error path.** The stream is closed on purpose, and an iOS home-screen PWA restores the frozen page rather than navigating, so the Server Component render does not re-run
  - The catch-up hangs off **`onopen` _and_ the resume events**, and is plain `fetch` either way — never gated on the socket. Hanging it off `onopen` **alone** was the iOS PWA bug: the screen stays stale as long as the reconnect takes, forever if it never lands
  - **Resume observes `visibilitychange`, `pageshow`, `focus`; background observes `visibilitychange` and `pagehide`.** iOS is inconsistent about which a standalone-PWA app-switch produces and one resume commonly fires several, so the catch-up is coalesced inside `SSE_SYNC_COALESCE_WINDOW`
  - **A restored iOS page keeps a dead socket at `readyState === OPEN`** — the system tears the connection down while frozen and never tells the object, so it emits no `error`. The zombie is detected by **silence**: every `message` / `user` / `ping` is timestamped, and a resume finding nothing newer than `SSE_STALE_AFTER` closes the source before reopening. Do not replace this with a "was the page hidden" flag — the freeze can arrive without a hide event
  - It pages `fetch(/api/messages?after=…)` until a short page, plus `fetch(/api/users)`, from a **local** copy of `newestKnownId` advanced only by what the loop itself fetched. Read from the ref each round, a live event landing mid-loop would carry it past a page the fetch has not covered
  - `after=0` is a **valid** cursor, not an absent one: a client whose window is still empty catches up from the start. Falling back to the cursorless newest page strands everything behind it unreachably
- **Received messages are deduplicated by id** and merged by sorting, never appending
- Multi-device needs no extra mechanism: `user_changed` is a conversation-wide broadcast, so "my other device" and "the other person's phone" are both just another subscriber

### 8.4.1. Idle close (절전 모드) ✅

§ 8.4. drops the stream when the app goes away. This drops **everything** — the stream, and every request to our own API — when the app is _there_ and nobody is using it, and says so on screen (`DESIGN.md § 7.17.`). A departure drops the same things, and says so only where the window it left is still on screen.

**The gap it closes.** `visibilitychange` never fires for a desktop PWA window sitting behind another app's window — occlusion is not `hidden`, and only a minimise, a tab switch or a close is. Such a window therefore held a stream open indefinitely: an unpooled Neon connection that keeps the compute from autosuspending, plus a `maxDuration = 300` Vercel invocation relaying itself forever. Measured on both dashboards; it was the dominant line on each.

**The second gap, and where it went.** Closing the stream was never the whole bill: a return to the foreground also fired `GET /api/chat/unread` from the shell and `GET /api/calendar/summary` from the calendar, each a session lookup plus its own query, each enough to bring Neon's compute back up. Dormancy went app-wide to hold those off — and what actually held them off was the **tap**, which a return no longer needs (below). Nothing on those three tabs polls while it is idle either, so a sleep there now saves nothing and only refuses requests the reader is waiting on. **Dormancy is therefore scoped to 채팅 — armed while `ChatRoom` is mounted, disarmed when it unmounts — and for as long as it is up it still gates requests rather than connections**: `shared/lib`'s `isDormant` is the flag, `shared/api`'s `request` is the gate, and every client fetcher goes through it.

- **The overlay is for a reader who can see the window, which is what a departure has to be tested for rather than assumed to lack.** A gate shut behind a screen with nothing on it to explain the silence is worse than one that never shuts. On a phone that argument reaches nobody — the window is gone by the time `blur` lands, the overlay a departure used to raise was painted into iOS's app-switcher snapshot and was still standing through the resume animation, and coming back meant dismissing a 절전 모드 the reader never watched arrive. **A desktop window is the opposite case, and it is the one this section opens with**: ⌘Tab away and it stays on screen, showing a live conversation whose stream that same `blur` has just closed. So a departure raises the overlay where the window lingers and stays silent where it does not — `isWindowedDesktop` in `useDormancy`, which is `isIos` plus `(pointer: fine)` and never a viewport width (§ 4.2.). **It exists only while the window is `visible` either way**: the app going away takes it down without ending the sleep, `revealDormancy` refuses to raise one on an already-hidden document, and every return ends the sleep itself, so the gate is open again before the first tap lands
- **`request` is the only way to reach our API from the client, and that is the enforcement.** A fetcher written the ordinary way is covered by having been written at all; a bare `fetch` to `/api/…` is the one way to defeat this section, so there is no second path to remember. R2's presigned PUT and § 8.9.'s oEmbed lookup stay on plain `fetch` — they never reach our server, so they cost Neon nothing and gating them would only break an upload
- **A blocked call rejects rather than waits.** `DormantRequestError` is a rejection like any other, so the callers that already swallow failures keep swallowing and the ones that toast keep toasting. Queueing the call until wake was rejected: the requests that fire while dormant are resume catch-ups whose answers are stale by the time anyone comes back, and holding their promises open for hours leaks every closure behind them
- **A countdown for idleness, none for a departure.** `SSE_IDLE_TIMEOUT` (a minute) governs a window the user is still in front of. Losing focus or being backgrounded is not idleness — the user has gone — so the stream drops at once
- **The `blur` and the `hidden` paths converge on one `leave`, which takes whether the departure was seen.** An earlier revision held a settling delay after `blur` to tell a window-switch from an app-switch; the platform answers that without waiting, so `blur` passes `isWindowedDesktop()` and `hidden` passes nothing — a document that has gone hidden is by definition one nobody is watching. The `hidden` path does two things more, both of them about the overlay a countdown or a desktop `blur` may already have raised: it takes it down, and it clears the timer that would otherwise come due on the resume
- **A task in flight departs without going dormant.** § 8.5.'s chain would lose its remaining links to a shut gate, and on iOS both a file picker and the share sheet take focus away mid-task — so `isBusy` keeps those on § 8.4.'s ordinary resume
- **An OS file picker is one of those tasks, and `isBusy` is structurally unable to see it.** `ActionSheet` fires the row's action and closes in the same tick, so the sheet that would have answered for it is already gone by the time the OS panel is up — 사진/영상 in the composer raised 절전 모드 _behind_ the iOS picker, and the overlay was still standing when the user came back with a photo. `openFilePicker` in `shared/lib` takes a `holdAwake` and clicks the input, so every caller is covered by using it at all; a bare `.click()` on a file input is the way back to this bug. **`useMediaPicker` is the only thing that calls it, and that is what makes the rule keepable** — every file input in the app is one of that hook's, so there is no second place where somebody could reach for `.click()`. Most of those pickers now open with their parent sheet still standing (`DESIGN.md § 7.5.`), which means `isBusy` would often answer for them anyway; that is incidental protection, and a rule with any input exempt from it is a rule nobody can apply. The overlay half of that bug went with the silent departure, and the gate half did not: a picker still blurs the window, and a sleep landing under it refuses the upload the reader came back with
- **The hold is released by `focus` / `pointerdown` / `keydown`, which is this section's own definition of a user who is here, and never by `change` or `cancel`.** The file events look like the obvious answer and are the wrong one: they say a file arrived, they say nothing to the countdown, and the countdown has meanwhile been re-arming at `SSE_BUSY_RECHECK_INTERVAL` against a deadline that expired while the picker was up. Releasing on one therefore dropped the hold straight into 절전 모드, seconds after the user came back with a photo — the same overlay, moved rather than removed. Borrowing the presence events instead makes the re-arm automatic, since `useDormancy` binds all three itself and is mounted first, so each one notes the interaction on the way past. The cost is that a picker the user walked away from holds until they next touch the app, which is the safe direction: an app awake too long, rather than a leaked hold shutting the request gate for the life of the document
- **A send or an upload in flight is one of those tasks, and it has to be registered by hand.** `isBusy` reads the DOM — an open dialog, a playing element — and a `POST` in flight is invisible to it. That cost nothing while dormancy only closed a socket, because the request carried on regardless. It is not survivable now that dormancy shuts the gate: § 8.5.'s delivery queue is a chain (`ticket → PUT → POST /api/messages`), and a departure landing between two links would leave bytes in R2 that no row points at and a message that never sends. `holdAwake` from `shared/lib` takes the hold, released in a `finally` — the queue takes one for the whole batch, and `uploadDraft` takes its own so callers outside the queue cannot forget. Picking photos, hitting send and immediately switching apps is ordinary behaviour, not an edge case
- **A hold must span the registration, not merely the upload.** `uploadEmoticonAsset` releases as soon as its PUT lands, which is correct for what it owns and useless on its own: the key still has to be registered, and § 13.4.'s bulk add runs that as a bare loop of POSTs after every per-file hold is gone, with the picker already closed so `isBusy` sees no dialog either. Twenty files outlast `SSE_IDLE_TIMEOUT` comfortably, and a dormancy landing mid-loop refuses the remaining `createEmoticon` calls **and** the `discardEmoticonAssets` meant to clean up after them — objects left in the bucket with nothing pointing at them. `addEmoticonsFromFiles` therefore takes a hold of its own across the whole batch, beside the `holdUnsentWork` it already held for § 15.1. The two are not interchangeable: one defers a reload, the other defers dormancy
- **`pointerdown` and `keydown` reset it, nothing else.** `mousemove` and `scroll` would hand the countdown to a cursor crossing the window and to momentum the user is not driving — which is most of what this exists to catch
- **The deadline is re-derived from the clock inside the timer, never trusted to it.** A frozen PWA runs no timers, so one armed before the freeze comes due the instant the page is restored — and would put the overlay in front of a user who has just this moment come back. Same wall-clock idiom as § 8.4.'s zombie-socket check, for the same reason. The hide clears the timer outright for that hazard, and `document.hasFocus()` catches the freeze that arrives without one (§ 8.4.): a resumed page has focus, and the window this overlay is for — one sitting behind another — does not
- **A countdown disarmed for being unwatched is re-armed on the way back.** The rule above kills the timer outright rather than rescheduling it, so `focus`, `pageshow` and a return to `visible` reset the deadline as well as ending a silent sleep. Without that, a departure that skipped dormancy through `isBusy` — a clip playing when the app was backgrounded — would come back to a reader who only scrolls, and never sleep again for the life of the page
  - **The return is tested on focus as well as `visibilityState`, and either one answers.** § 8.4. has iOS restoring a frozen PWA on `pageshow` / `focus` alone, so a return read from visibility alone can find `hidden` and leave the gate shut until a tap — every catch-up refused behind a screen the reader is looking at. Focus is the same second signal `sleep` already reads for the freeze that arrives without a hide, and it cannot be borrowed by a window that is genuinely away: backgrounding blurs it first
- **The countdown still refuses to expire on a window nobody has looked at.** A tab opened in the background — cmd-click, `target=_blank`, a restored session tab — starts `hidden` and fires **no** `visibilitychange` until it is first viewed, so nothing else would stop it going dormant unseen and greeting its first viewer with the overlay. Dormancy from the _countdown_ is still a visible window's state; dormancy from a departure is not, because there a departure demonstrably happened. **It reads focus beside visibility, exactly as the return above does, and the two must move together**: a window woken on focus alone is `hidden` by that same test, so a countdown gated on visibility would disarm itself and leave the wake holding an unpooled connection for the whole background stretch — the bill this section exists to remove. The tab nobody has viewed holds no focus either, so it is refused as before
- **A task in flight defers the countdown.** A recording, a playing clip, or an open `Dialog` / `Drawer` re-arms instead of sleeping, through `isBusy` (`shared/lib`). Three separate reasons, one gate:
  - **Recording is hands-off and outlasts the allowance.** `MAX_VOICE_DURATION` is two minutes against a one-minute countdown, and a recording produces no `pointerdown` and no `keydown` — so the overlay would land on top of a clip still being recorded and take 정지 with it
  - **Playback is read off the elements, not registered by hand.** Every `<video>` and `<audio>` in the app answers `isBusy` without being wired to it, and a new one cannot forget to
  - **An open sheet outranks the overlay and would strand it.** `Dialog` and `Drawer` portal to `body` at `z-50`, a later stacking context than anything `ShellOverlay` puts inside the shell — so a dormancy raised underneath one leaves the sheet fully live over an app that believes it is asleep, and the user's first click only dismisses the sheet
- **Dormancy outranks every reopen path.** `useDormancy` owns the state and lives in the shell; `useChatEventSource` is handed the answer and holds no socket while it is `true`, so the `SSE_RETRY_DELAY` retry, the resume events and the visibility handler cannot reopen one behind it. A dormant client also skips the catch-up entirely — it is several requests, and running them for a screen nobody has come back to is the cost this state exists to avoid
- **The wake catch-up has one owner, and scoping this to 채팅 is what left it with one.** It is `useChatEventSource`'s own rebuild, and it runs the § 8.4. resume — the catch-up **first**, then the socket — rather than the plain open a fresh mount does. Leaving it on `onopen` was § 8.4.'s iOS PWA bug reintroduced by the gate: a sleeping client holds no listener, so the `pageshow` / `focus` / `visibilitychange` that carried the return reach nothing and the rebuilt effect is all that is left to notice it. A return is also precisely when Neon has autosuspended, so that reconnect is the slowest one the app ever makes and the room sat on stale messages for the whole of it — forever when it never landed. The shell's own resync on the transition out of dormancy went with the app-wide gate: the three tabs that hold no socket never sleep now, so § 8.4.2.'s refetch on every return to `visible` reaches them unrefused, which is what it was written to do before the gate started refusing it
- **The read cursor is not flushed on the departure that goes dormant, and this is a deliberate loss.** § 8.8. calls the backgrounding post unconditional; it is not, any more. The alternative was to let the gate stay open for one `POST /api/chat/read` on the way out, which is a defensible reading of "leaving" — but the whole point of this section is that a departure is the one moment the app must be silent, and a rule with an exception at exactly that moment is a rule nothing enforces. What it costs is bounded and self-correcting: the § 16.1. push payload's `unreadCount` counts messages already read, so the app-icon badge over-reports until the next wake, and the unread count beside my bubbles on the other device does not clear until then. **No spurious notification results** — § 16.1. dispatches at INSERT and never consults the cursor
- **An overlay still standing is left by a press, a real keystroke, or the window taking focus back.** The rule this replaces refused the last of those, on the reasoning that a window still `visible` is one the reader has been able to see the whole time. That is only true of a _partly_ covered window, and the fully covered one is the case this section opens with: ⌘Tab away from the installed window and the departure raises 절전 모드 on a surface that may be entirely behind another, so coming back asked for a press to dismiss what the return had already answered. A key counts because focus is usually still in the composer when the overlay arrives, without which the typing that follows goes into a field the user can no longer see and nothing reconnects. What is left needing a press is the window that never lost focus — a reader sitting in front of the screen, where nothing but the press says they are back. Every path runs the ordinary § 8.4. resume, so the catch-up is lossless and the screen does not jump
  - **A modifier is not a keystroke.** `Cmd` reaches the page before focus leaves it, so waking on any key at all meant `Cmd+Tab` — a departure — opened a connection on the way out, which is the exact opposite of what the gesture asked for. A bare `Meta` / `Control` / `Alt` / `Shift` / `CapsLock`, and anything held under `Cmd` / `Ctrl` / `Alt`, is a shortcut rather than typing and does not wake
  - **The reset while awake is unfiltered, and deliberately.** `Cmd+C` in the composer is a user plainly at the keyboard, so it keeps the countdown alive. A shortcut that is a departure is caught by the blur floor instead, which lands the same tick
  - **A tap wakes a silent sleep as a keystroke does.** There is no overlay under the pointer to catch the press, and it must not land on a shut gate — a desktop window raised by a click on a tab could otherwise navigate before `focus` had been delivered. It is the same handler `pointerdown` already used to note an interaction, so the countdown is reset either way
- **`NEXT_PUBLIC_SSE_IDLE_SLEEP` turns the whole thing off, and defaults to on.** It is checked in `enterDormancy`, not merely where the countdown is armed. It used to gate the timer alone, which left every _departure_ still going dormant with the switch off — survivable when that closed a socket, and not survivable now that it also shuts the request gate. **It governs § 8.4.'s `blur` close as well, and that is the expensive half.** A desktop window pushed behind another stays `visible`, so `blur` is the only event that reaches it — with the switch off `handleBlur` holds the socket instead of closing it, which is what keeps such a window live and is the whole reason to turn the switch off. The bill is the one § 8.4.1. was written to remove: an unpooled Neon connection up for the life of the window, so compute never autosuspends. **`pagehide` and the `hidden` branch are not part of this and close unconditionally** — those mean the window is gone rather than merely behind another, and on iOS the socket is a § 8.4. zombie anyway. Absent, blank or anything but `false` / `0` / `off` leaves it enabled — a cost control that has to be remembered in each new environment is one that will be missing from the environment that needs it. It is `NEXT_PUBLIC_` because the decision is the client's, and read as a literal member access since Next inlines these at build time
- **§ 8.12.'s retraction is refused too, and the receiver's expiry is what covers it.** A `blur` on desktop leaves the document `visible`, so the typing pump is never stopped by its own visibility handler — it keeps ticking against a shut gate, and the `DELETE` that would have withdrawn 입력 중 is refused with everything else. The peer therefore holds the indicator for the full `TYPING_TIMEOUT` rather than clearing at once. Accepted rather than fixed: § 8.12. already designs for a sender who backgrounds, loses signal or is killed, and expiry is the only thing that ever clears the indicator anyway
- **Push is what makes this safe.** § 16.1.'s OS notification is the app's one alerting channel and owes nothing to the stream, and `sw.js` writes the app badge from the server's own count — so a dormant client still alerts, and still badges, exactly as an open one does. It is also why the gate can be absolute: nothing the app would have asked for while asleep is something the user finds out about any later
- **§ 15.1.'s reload is the one request dormancy does not gate, and it is not routed through `request` at all.** A `window.location.reload()` is the most expensive request in the app — a full Server Component render and several queries — so the exception is worth stating rather than leaving to be discovered. It survives because it fires at most once per deployment, only when a `build` event has already proved this document stale, and only on a backgrounding, where the app is going away regardless. Gating it would strand the client on a dead bundle until it next woke, which is the failure § 15.1. exists to prevent

**Neon bills this only if the gaps outlast its autosuspend delay.** Compute suspends after a configured idle stretch; a client that wakes more often than that keeps it up regardless of how diligently the stream is closed. The Vercel saving is unconditional, the Neon one is not — the console's autosuspend setting is half of this change and lives outside the repository.

### 8.4.2. The socket is scoped to 채팅 ✅

The stream is open only while the conversation is on screen. Leaving the tab closes it; returning opens it and catches up through the ordinary § 8.4. resume.

**Why it moved down.** § 8.4.1. closes an idle window, but a window parked on 캘린더 is not idle — the user is using the app, and the stream was held for a screen showing nothing it feeds. Scoping is what removes that case, and it removes it without waiting out a countdown.

- **The provider stays in the shell; only the socket moves.** `participants` is read by the calendar and § 12.3.'s profile screen, and the badge is drawn by the tab bar — all three outlive the chat screen, so `ChatStreamProvider` has to as well. It hands its handlers down through a context of its own, and `ChatStreamConnection` is the one component that consumes them. § 8.4.1.'s dormancy stays in the shell too, so its listeners are registered ahead of every other departure handler; the provider owns the state and renders the overlay, and `ChatStreamConnection` renders nothing at all — it exists so that "the socket is open" is a mount in the chat screen's JSX rather than a hook call another screen could copy
- **A second mount would open a second stream.** The connection component is mounted by the chat screen and by nothing else; two of them means two unpooled Neon connections for one client
- **The handlers object is lazy initial state, not a ref.** It has to be stable _and_ readable during render, and React Compiler rejects a ref read there
- **The three losses, and what covers them:**
  - **The in-app tab-bar badge** is the only one with real weight, and it has two movers, because one is not enough:
    - **The push.** Every § 16.1. push posts the server's `unreadCount` to open windows, which the provider applies. It **replaces** rather than increments — a client that has been off 채팅 missed every arrival and has nothing to add to — and it is ignored while the reader is in the conversation, where § 8.8.'s cursor is about to clear it anyway. The worker still shows its banner unconditionally; informing the page is additive, and standing the notification down is the shape that lost the subscription
    - **Dormant beats reading in that gate.** "The conversation is mounted" and "the conversation is live" stopped being the same thing when § 8.4.1. arrived: a sleeping room is covered by the overlay or has been left behind, and either way the stream that would have delivered the message is closed — so the reader is not reading and the count is real. Read from `shared/lib`'s flag at the message rather than held as state, since no render observes it
    - **A refetch on every return to `visible`**, because push is **not** universal: § 12.'s 알림 row has three inert states — permission denied, unsupported (an iOS Safari tab), and in flight — and a client in any of them receives nothing at all. Without it the badge would sit at whatever the last render produced until it next walked into 채팅. It costs one `GET /api/chat/unread` per return. It once had to fire on the wake instead, because § 8.4.1.'s gate refused it on every other tab; scoping dormancy back to 채팅 is what returned it to the plain resume it wants to be
    - **Still not live for a window that stays open on another tab with push off.** That client learns of the message on its next resume or its next visit to 채팅. Closing it properly would need a second stream, which is the cost this section exists to remove
  - **A nickname or avatar change** no longer reaches an open calendar the instant it happens. It is picked up on the next chat visit or the next load, and § 5.'s token is resolved against `users` on every request, so nothing is stale for long
  - **The § 15.1. build signal** now arrives on the chat tab rather than any tab. The check also runs on every backgrounding, which is what usually collects it first

### 8.5. Sending ✅

The client generates `client_msg_id` (uuid) and renders optimistically; `POST /api/messages` uses `ON CONFLICT (client_msg_id) DO NOTHING` so a retry cannot duplicate a row; a retry affordance shows on failure.

- **A media send carries the uploads themselves and takes its ids back in the reply.** The body is `media` — § 9.'s R2 keys — rather than ids from an earlier registration, and the 201 answers a top-level `media` array, since nothing holds a `media.id` until the request that registers and attaches them has answered. **A retry appends what the first attempt never attached**: the conflict re-read happens inside that transaction and every row this attempt registered that the existing message does not already carry is added after its current `sort_order` max, where `DO NOTHING` alone handed the old row back and silently dropped them
- The re-read after a conflict is scoped to `sender_id` and `deleted_at IS NULL` and answers **409** when it misses. `client_msg_id` is unique table-wide rather than per sender, so matching on it alone would return the _other_ user's row and swap the optimistic bubble for a stranger's message
- The SSE echo is matched by `client_msg_id` and replaces the optimistic entry. The echo routinely beats the POST response, so the pending bubble retires on whichever arrives first
- **Every send goes through one delivery queue**, not one chain per call. `messages.id` is assigned by the POST, so a caption sent alongside attachments would otherwise be POSTed while the uploads were still running and land _above_ the photos everywhere but the sender's optimistic render
