- **`↑` off the first row of the grid goes to the region above it, and the panel is read upwards.** § 13.6.'s three regions stack menu bar, strip, grid, so the way out of the cells is `↑` — to the strip on 이모티콘 and 미니, to the field on 검색 — then `↑` again from the strip to the menu bar. `↓` walks back down: the menu bar into the strip (or 검색's field), the strip into the cells. **This pair used to run the other way**, `↓` off the end of the list arriving at a strip that was then the last thing on the panel; the third region inverted it, and `↓` off the end now leads nowhere and is deliberately unhandled, because handling it would move focus **up** the screen. **`↓` in the field still navigates whether or not the search found anything** — into the grid when it did, and to the menu bar when it did not, since a dead key on an empty search is exactly where the reader most needs to leave, and 검색 has no strip to fall to. **`↑` in the field is the way back to the menu bar**, and it is what makes the first region reachable from below on 검색 as the strip makes it on the other two; the price is `↑`'s own meaning in a one-line field, which is the caret to offset 0 and is still spelled `Home`
- **The menu bar is walked without being opened, where the strip below it opens what it lands on.** Both are toolbars with one tab stop, and the difference is what a step costs: crossing a pack is one list request, crossing a **menu** swaps the kind, the column count and the whole second region — so reaching 미니 to get to 검색 would rebuild the panel twice for somewhere the reader was only passing through. `Enter`/`Space` is what opens the focused menu, and the bar's roving stop therefore follows the **focused** menu rather than the open one. That stop is re-synced on the open menu **changing** and never on it merely differing, or the walk would be snapped back on the next render and the arrows would read as dead
- **`⌃1` / `⌃2` / `⌃3` name 검색, 이모티콘 and 미니, and open the panel where it is shut. They never close it.** So the digit of the menu already on screen changes no menu: the request reaches a panel that is already there and already on that menu, and `selectMenu` finds nothing to do. `⌃1` additionally **focuses the search field**, which is what separates it from an arrow landing on the same chip — it is a request to type — and it is owed on that no-change case too, so the focus is keyed on the request's own token rather than on the menu changing — and carries § 13.8.'s seed, the word underlined in the draft, claimed by `MessageComposer` under the same guards and `preventDefault`ed there so the room answers only the presses the composer does not. `⌃2` and `⌃3` return to the tab their menu was last left on, through the same per-menu memory a step across the bar uses. The panel is told through a `menuRequest` token, applied in an **effect** rather than during render unlike § 13.8.'s and § 13.9.'s, because what it is compared against is a ref a render-phase adjustment may not read — and the room's own focus effect then runs after that child's, which is what lets it land on a menu that has already changed
- **`⌃`/`Alt` and neither modifier this section already spells, and that is a conclusion rather than a taste.** Three were tried in turn. `⌘1/2/3` is the browser's own tab switch, so where the browser reserves it the page is never sent the `keydown` and the binding is dead outside an installed PWA. `⌥1/2/3` replaced it and was dead a second way: `⌥` is a **character** modifier on macOS, where `⌥1` types `¡` — so the key could not be pressed in the composer, in § 13.8.'s search field or in § 8.13.'s correction, which are the three places a shortcut claimed by `MessageComposer` is most wanted. `⌃` types nothing there and holds no Cocoa key binding on the digit row: that table is letters, punctuation and arrows, so the three digits take nothing from a text field. **`⌃E` is where that stops being free, and it is spent rather than avoided**: `⌃E` is Cocoa's `moveToEndOfParagraph`, so putting the panel toggle on this modifier costs the composer line-end on macOS. One modifier for the whole panel is worth more than one editing key most readers reach by other means — `⌃A` and the rest of the table are untouched. **The other price is a second label in the sheet and a platform branch where `⌥` needed neither**, and it is paid for the same reason — a shortcut that cannot be pressed is worth less than one that has to be spelled twice. macOS's own `⌃1`/`⌃2`/`⌃3` is Mission Control's switch-to-desktop, which is **off by default**; that was confirmed before this was chosen, because enabled it would swallow the keystroke above the browser exactly as `⌘1` does. `⌥` stays the scroll modifier, where a character it types is no obstacle — nothing is focused
- **Off an Apple platform the menu digits are `Alt+1`/`Alt+2`/`Alt+3`, and Firefox on Linux binds those to its own tab switch** (where Windows and macOS use `Ctrl` and `⌘` for it). That collision predates this modifier — `⌥1` was already `Alt+1` there — so it is not a cost of the change and is not solved by reverting it. It is recorded so the next survey does not have to be run twice
- **The menu test runs ahead of the `⌥` scroll branch, and that ordering is load-bearing on Windows and Linux only — which makes it more dangerous than when it was load-bearing everywhere.** There the menu modifier **is** `Alt`, so the digits satisfy `isAltKey` too, and that branch reads every key but `ArrowDown` as a step towards older messages — so `Alt+1` walks the conversation backwards instead of opening a menu. macOS spells the menus with `⌃` and never reaches that branch, so the bug is invisible to anyone developing on a Mac and appears on the first press for everyone else. It is the exact inverse of the ordering the `⌘` digits needed. **`key` **and** `code` are both still tested and `code` is the fallback again**, neither menu modifier spending itself on the character the way `⌥` did. **The picker's applied-token ref is seeded empty**, never from the prop: with no close branch left there is nothing for a seed to protect, and the panel is now mounted _by_ the keystroke whenever that beats § 13.6.'s idle mount — a seeded ref drops the only menu request that open will ever get
- **`⇧←/→` is 이모티콘 and 미니 only**, which narrows what the combination already did. 검색's second region is a field rather than a strip, so there is no tab beside the open one to turn to
- **`Enter`/`Space` on a mini inserts it into the composer instead of staging it**, and that is § 2.2. rather than a keyboard rule: a mini is stored as a fragment of a `text` message and never in `messages.emoticon_item_id`, so a staged one would promise a send with no row to land in. It is the **pointer's** path too, for the same reason, and `⌘Enter` on a mini is the same insertion — there is no staged form for a quick send to skip to. It goes in at the caret the field last held, one rule with no branch on whether that caret is live, because `EditableField` remembers it across a blur
- **The keyboard's stage-then-send pair keeps no time window, and the pointer's still does.** They are one rule and one `handleSelect`, so the second press sends what the first staged — but `DOUBLE_TAP_WINDOW` exists to tell a double tap apart from two deliberate taps, and a key pressed twice on a cell focus never left is already unambiguous. Timed, the second `Enter` simply staged what it had just staged
- **The sheet names the arrows once and does not enumerate those edges.** The strip, the grid and the field are one surface to the reader; which arrow crosses which boundary is what the keys _do_, and a line per edge reads as four rules where there is one. **`Enter`/`Space` is the exception and takes two lines**, because the regions mean different things by it — the bar switches, a cell stages, and a mini has no staged form to reach
- **Every handler guards on `isComposing`.** A Hangul IME fires `keydown` for the keystrokes that settle a syllable — the arrows that steer its candidate list, and the Enter that closes a composition. This is the same trap `KeywordField` records for § 13.4.'s chips, one layer up
- **`⌃1` is offered with no word underlined too**, opening the search on an empty field. Offered only where a word happens to match, it answers on one draft in ten and reads as broken on the rest, so there is nothing to learn from it — the underlined word is a **seed** it carries when there is one, not its precondition. It is withheld while § 8.13.'s correction is open, exactly as the underline is
- **One key decides whether the panel is on screen and three decide which menu it holds.** That replaced a `⌘E`/`⌘⇧E` pair in which each key both opened and chose, so a second press of either had two meanings to pick between; `⌘⇧E` is withdrawn outright and what it did is § 13.8.'s seed on `⌃1` above. `⌘K` was weighed and refused, and still is: in this screen the natural owner of "search" is § 8.6.'s 메시지 검색, and spending `⌘K` on emoticons would block that
- **`⌃E` is the only key that closes the panel, and it is the room's alone for that half.** Closing, and opening with no word underlined, mean open-or-closed from everywhere on the tab in `ACTIVE_TAB_KEY` — the same stored tab the toggle button restores — so a remembered 미니 pack opens in 미니 rather than being forced through 이모티콘's 최근 사용 tab. It blurs whatever had focus first — § 13.6.'s gate gives the panel to a keyboard that is down, and iOS lowers the keyboard for a blur alone — and then moves focus **into** the panel through `focusRequest`, because a panel opened by a key that leaves focus behind is one the arrows cannot reach. That request waits for a cold pack's cells rather than settling for `<body>`
- **Opening is the one half `MessageComposer` claims, and only where a word is already underlined.** Guarded exactly as `⌃1` is — not editing, panel not already open — plus `match`, so the shortcut never fires on an empty draft the way `⌃1` does; the panel restores its remembered tab for that case, where `⌃1`'s empty search has none. Claimed and `preventDefault`ed in the composer's own handler, ahead of the room's fallback, it seeds `onKeywordTap` the same way `⌃1` does — the panel opens on 검색 with the underlined word already in the field, rather than on its remembered tab. The panel already open is deliberately left to the room, so a second `⌃E` still closes it instead of re-seeding a search the reader may have since changed
- **`Esc` peels one layer and `Enter` peels none, although both end with the caret in the field.** `Escape` takes the § 13.6. staged emoticon, then the open panel, then nothing — one press undoes one thing, which is the only reading that stays predictable once a screen has layers. `Enter` is pressed by someone who wants to start typing and must never discard what they staged to say it, so the two are separate callbacks rather than one
- **`⌥↑`/`⌥↓` scroll the conversation, on the modifier that needs no platform branch** — `⌥` and `Alt` are the same physical key and the same `altKey` flag. **Off an Apple platform the menu digits share it**, which is why they have to be tested ahead of this (above); on macOS they do not, which is exactly what hides that ordering from a Mac. The step is a share of the scroller's height rather than a pixel count, so it reads the same on a phone and a desktop. **It sets `hasTakenScrollRef` by hand**: § 8.3.'s park is released by a real gesture, and the listener that hears one is on the scroller, which a key pressed with focus in the composer never reaches — left unset, the next arrival drags the reader back to the live edge they had just scrolled away from. **Smooth**, and § 13.6.'s refusal of `behavior: "smooth"` does not reach it: that one is about an animation running _across_ the emoticon panel's collapse, which changes the content size underneath it, where this runs across nothing and § 8.3.'s prepend is held back until the scroller has been still for `SETTLE_DELAY`. A repeat aborts the animation in flight and starts a new one from where it had reached, so a held key is one glide rather than a queue of jumps
- **Waking from § 8.4.1.'s 절전 모드 puts focus back.** That overlay takes focus on mount precisely so keystrokes cannot reach the composer behind it, and it restores nothing on the way out — so waking left focus on `<body>`, where every shortcut here is refused and the panel's arrows reach nothing. The open panel wins over the composer, being what the reader was in the middle of. The wake is observed through `subscribeDormancy`, a `useSyncExternalStore` pair over **`countVisibleWakes`** — a count of the overlays the reader has dismissed, not a flag. A silent sleep takes no focus to give back, and the app going away takes a standing overlay down without one either; neither can be told from a press by reading `isDormantVisible` in the effect that follows, because a freeze can defer that effect to the resume, where it finds a `visible` document and raises the keyboard at somebody who has only just come back. The ref it compares against is seeded at first render rather than at `0`, or a room re-entered after a wake would open by seizing the caret
- **The composer's placeholder carries `⌘/`, and only where a pointer is driving.** These keys appear nowhere else on screen, and the field is the one thing every reader already looks at. **Whether the app is installed does not enter into it** — that was tried and reversed: a browser tab and an installed window are the same keyboard, and the hint is about a key being pressable rather than about which chrome is around the page. `useIsFinePointer` is **not** the negation of `useIsCoarsePointer` — both answer `false` before the media query has been read, so the pair fails towards the phone: one withholds a hardware-keyboard behaviour until it is sure, and this one withholds a hardware-keyboard hint until it is sure. The label is read inside the ternary, which is also what keeps a platform guess out of the server's HTML
- **Where it is carried, the placeholder is the hint alone — `⌘ + / 단축키 보기`, not `메시지 입력 · ⌘ + / 단축키 보기`.** The field's `aria-label` is already `메시지 입력`, so naming it again bought nothing and made a line long enough that the shortcut sat at the end of it, past where an eye resting on a chat room stops reading
- **`Enter` reaches for the composer only where nothing has focus.** A focused control spends it activating itself — a cell stages an emoticon (§ 13.6.), a tab opens, the send disc sends — and a field with the caret in it has no use for a shortcut that would fetch the caret. What is left is exactly the case this answers, and the test is `document.activeElement` being `<body>` rather than a list of roles to exclude
- **The field takes focus on arriving at 검색, except under 따라하기.** § 13.8. gives that focus to every route onto the menu because each of them is a request to type; 따라하기 is a request to _look_, on § 13.9.'s own argument
- **The seed is only armed when it is a real word.** § 13.8.'s send spends the searched word out of the draft by comparing it against `text.trim()`, and `""` is what a cleared draft trims to — left armed on an empty `⌃1`, the next quick send would swallow a message typed after it
- **Roving tabindex over the grid, the results row and the tab strip** (ARIA's own mechanism): one item of each list carries `tabIndex={0}` and the arrows move both the focus and the stop. Every cell is a `<button>`, so without it a pack of forty is forty tab stops between the composer and its send control, and a library of two hundred packs is two hundred more
- **Focus stops at the edges rather than wrapping**, which is ARIA's grid **keyboard** pattern — a partial last row would otherwise swallow `ArrowDown` into the row above it. `EMOTICON_GRID_COLUMNS` is the vertical step and the `grid-cols-4` the panel draws, and the two MUST agree — **there are two counts now**, since 미니 is drawn at `MINI_GRID_COLUMNS`/`grid-cols-6` (§ 13.6.), so each pair has to agree separately and neither class can be built from its constant, Tailwind reading literals
- **The keys follow that pattern and the roles do not claim it.** `role="grid"` requires `row` elements this layout has nowhere to put — a `display: contents` wrapper is the only candidate, and that is the property browsers spent years dropping from the accessibility tree. The grid and § 13.8.'s row are `role="group"` with a label, which is what they are: a named set of buttons
- **The focus move scrolls by hand, with `preventScroll` on the `focus()` itself.** `focus()`'s own scroll and `scrollIntoView` both walk _every_ scrollable ancestor, and § 13.6.'s clipping strip is `overflow: hidden` — which mid-collapse counts as one, so the panel would be scrolled inside its own clip. `revealWithin` is the one copy of that workaround, and the tab strip's own reveal shares it rather than restating the arithmetic
- **A tab switch made from the strip needs no focus restoration, and that is why the switch lives there.** The grid subtree is `key={activeTab}`, so switching destroys the cell focus was on and drops it to `<body>` — from where nothing in the panel is on the event's path and every following arrow is dead. The strip's own buttons are keyed by pack and survive, so focus never leaves them
- **That ring paints on plain `:focus` while the panel is being driven by the keyboard, which is DESIGN.md § 3.2.'s third recorded exception.** `:focus-visible` is decided by a heuristic this panel breaks: a click focuses a cell **without** it, and a programmatic `focus()` — which is what every arrow key here performs — is judged by whether the _previously_ focused element had it. So one click made every later arrow move invisible while the navigation itself went on working, which reads worse than no ring at all. A key anywhere in the panel turns it on and a `pointerdown` anywhere turns it off; the `focus-visible` rules stay beside it, for a `Tab` that arrives before any arrow has been pressed. **The `pointerdown` listener is on the capture phase**, because § 13.8.'s results row stops that event propagating while it still has somewhere to scroll
- **The cells' focus ring is `ring-inset` over a `primary-tint` fill, not DESIGN.md § 3.2.'s offset ring.** Two reasons at once: the cells tile their scroller, which is `overflow-x-hidden` in the grid and `overflow-y-hidden` in § 13.8.'s row, so an outward ring is clipped away on every edge cell — the same trap § 7.5. records — and 2px of `primary` over an arbitrary user-authored picture is not a contrast anything can rely on. The fill is what makes it legible; the ring is what makes it a focus ring
- **`Enter` is § 13.6.'s tap and counts against the same pair**: once stages the preview, again on the same cell sends. It routes through `handleSelect` rather than staging directly, so the keyboard and the thumb are **one** rule the sheet states in one line rather than two behaviours that drift — the window is the half that differs, and only that half (above). `⌘Enter` is that send in a single press, kept because it is what every other app spells it with and left out of the sheet for the same reason
- **The native `click` is prevented on those keys.** Unprevented it reaches `handleSelect` a second time and every single press reads as a pair — which is a send on the first `Enter`
- **The repeat guard is what keeps a _held_ key out of the pair.** The browser fires the handler per repeat, so without it a key left down stages, sends, and goes on sending at the repeat rate
- **A pointer press has to move focus by hand, and one that does not ends keyboard navigation outright.** `HapticTap` takes the tap on its own overlay and replays it as a scripted `control.click()` (`DESIGN.md § 7.15.`) — a scripted click moves no focus, and the real `pointerdown` landed on a `<span>` nothing can focus, so it dropped focus to `<body>`. Every handler in the panel reads the key off the focused item, so one click anywhere in it left the arrows dead. Each cell and each tab focuses itself in its own `onClick`, with `preventScroll` for `focusItem`'s reason
- **The sheet's handle is one extra tab stop**, a plain `<button>` outside every roving row: `Enter`/`Space` toggle rest ⇄ expanded through its `click` and `aria-expanded` reports which, while the pointer's drag never reaches the keyboard
- **The tab strip is ARIA's toolbar, not its tablist**, which is what a labelled group of `aria-pressed` buttons with one tab stop is. A tablist would additionally need `aria-controls` and a `tabpanel` the two panes cannot both be
- **`OPEN_OVERLAY_SELECTOR` is not enough on its own, and the room has to say what is covering it.** That selector answers for Radix, Vaul and the two screens `useModalOverlay` marks — `MediaEditor`, `VideoTrimmer` and § 8.6.'s results list are all plain `ShellOverlay` children with no marker of their own. Unsaid, `Esc` pulls the caret into a composer behind the crop editor and raises the keyboard into a field nobody can see, and `⌘↓` moves the conversation underneath a list the reader is still in. The room passes `isCovered`, and the hook reads `isDormantVisible()` at the keystroke — 절전 모드 takes focus on mount precisely so keys cannot reach the composer behind it (§ 8.4.1.), and answering `Esc` there would hand that focus straight back
- **`Esc` is refused a second time inside the room's own handler**, because that is where the invariant lives: `focus()` on a field inside an `inert` subtree silently does nothing rather than failing, and § 8.6. hides the whole composer stack that way. Closing the search itself belongs to the header that owns it and is deliberately not bound here
- **Windows is covered by the mapping rather than by a second set of bindings.** `isCommandKey` reads `Ctrl` off an Apple platform and `toCommandKeyLabel` writes it, so the sheet and the placeholder both say `Ctrl` there without a branch at any call site. The two collisions worth recording: `Ctrl+E` focuses the omnibox in Chrome and the search bar in Firefox, and `Ctrl+⇧+E` opens Firefox's network panel — all three are preventable page-level bindings rather than the reserved tab and window ones, and this app is macOS-first
- **The room's listener tests the key before it reads anything else.** It runs on every keystroke typed into the composer, and neither the dormancy read nor the `OPEN_OVERLAY_SELECTOR` query may be part of that path
- **`⌘↓` is claimed inside the composer too**, where WebKit spends it moving the caret to the end of the draft. That is a near-nothing in a field capped at five lines, and the composer is the one place this shortcut has to work from
- **`⌘/` opens a `Modal` and not DESIGN.md § 7.5.'s sheet**, which is the app's default overlay. A sheet is for something that started at the bottom of the screen or in a list, and this starts nowhere on it — nothing in it can be reached without a hardware keyboard, so thumb reach, the whole argument for a sheet, does not apply
- **Discoverability is `⌘/`, and the composer's placeholder is where it is offered — as `⌘ + / 단축키 보기` alone, wherever a pointer is driving** (§ 8.14. above). These keys appear nowhere else on screen, which is the price of a UI with no menu bar; the one key worth learning is the one that lists the rest, and the field is the one thing every reader already looks at. **The sheet does not list `⌘/` itself** — anyone reading it has already pressed it, so the row taught nothing and cost a line of the five it has
- **A cell's `aria-label` is its own keywords** (§ 13.8.), falling back to `이모티콘` where nobody has described it. Stepping a grid of forty identical labels tells a reader nothing about which picture each cell is
- **§ 8.4.1.'s wake filter is unchanged and a modified key still will not wake the app.** `isTypingKey` refuses anything carrying `⌘`/`Ctrl`/`Alt` so that `⌘Tab` — a departure — cannot wake it on the way out. These shortcuts are separately withheld while 절전 모드 is up (see `isDormantVisible()` above), so the first unmodified key or tap is what brings the room back and nothing here acts behind the overlay

### 8.15. Ask AI (쨈미니) ✅

- The composer's `Sparkles` toggle enters **AI 질문 모드**: a KakaoTalk-style forward-select sweep (`useAiSelection`) reusing § 8.11.'s selection gutter (`SelectableRow`), auto-selecting the newest 30 live `text` and `assistant_reply` messages the moment it opens. There is **no selection cap** — every other message stays reachable by hand. A `text` or `assistant_reply` message that arrives while the mode stays open joins the selection once, on arrival, so an unchecked one is not re-added on the next render; the 30 cap applies only to what auto-selects at entry
- **The mode's controls split between the composer and the header.** `AiSelectionBar` (the composer's pill) carries only the 모델/생각/AI 지침 pickers; the count, `전체 해제`/`자동 선택` and the exit take over the chat header while the mode is up, the way REQUIREMENTS.md § 10.'s selection bar takes over 보관함's own — `ChatRoom` owns `useAiSelection` (its `messages` is the paginated list) and reports the header's half up through `onAiSelectionChange`, since the header belongs to the screen (§ 8.6.'s search is the same split)
- Media and emoticon bubbles are **never auto-selected**, but a reader may toggle one in by hand; selected, they are sent to the provider as real inline bytes (`buildPromptContext`), not descriptions
- The question itself is an **ordinary `text` message**, sent through the normal path — there is no separate "question" row shape
- **A tray or a staged emoticon sends along with the question, ahead of it** — the same bubbles a normal send makes, through the same functions. The request waits for the question's own send and every attachment's to land before it goes out, and folds their `messageIds` into the selected set; an attachment whose send fails is dropped rather than waited on forever
- **An AI answer is a `system` row, `system_action = 'assistant_reply'`, with the asker as `sender_id`.** It is drawn with the provider's own avatar and name (`AssistantMessageRow`) rather than the asker's, and is a wide bubble — the content column minus the avatar gutter, not § 6.2.'s 72% cap — since it is the one bubble in the room meant to be read in full at a glance
- **Provider credentials, model and fallback order live in `llm_agents` rows, not code** — `priority` (higher first, ties broken by the older row) plus a `config` jsonb for provider-specific knobs. A new provider is one implementation file plus a row, never a migration
- **A 429 stamps that agent's `disabled_until`, and the duration is read from the error when the provider offers one.** Gemini's own hint (the `google.rpc.RetryInfo` detail its error body carries) plus a small safety margin, clamped to `LLM_MAX_AGENT_COOLDOWN`; `LLM_AGENT_COOLDOWN` (one minute) is the flat fallback when there is no hint to read. The fallback chain tries the next candidate regardless; `listCandidateAgents` excludes a still-cooling-down or disabled row outright
- **A model picker offers `자동` (the priority-order chain above) or a specific model**, listed from `GET /api/chat/ai/agents` — every distinct enabled `(provider, model)` pair, never the row's `api_key`, `config` or id (`llmAgentOptionSchema`). Pinned, the agents naming that model are tried first, in their own priority order, before the rest of the chain falls back exactly as `자동` would have run it
- **A thinking-level picker (`기본` / `낮음` / `높음`) sends an optional provider-neutral `low`/`high`** (`LLM_THINKING_LEVELS`, `llmThinkingLevelSchema`) on `POST /api/chat/ai` — `기본` sends nothing. Gemini's provider implementation maps it onto `thinkingConfig.thinkingLevel`; a model with no such knob is expected to ignore an unrecognised config field rather than reject the request
- **Both picks are remembered per device in `localStorage`**, never synced between the two participants and never written to any row — a fresh device or a cleared store falls back to `자동` / `기본`
- **Concurrent questions queue globally.** One `pg_advisory_lock` (`LLM_GENERATION_LOCK_KEY`) on a dedicated unpooled connection per request is the whole FIFO — this asker re-asking or the other participant asking at the same time both block on it, and Postgres grants waiters roughly in arrival order
- **Either participant may stop any generation**, not only the one who asked (`DELETE /api/chat/ai`) — cancellation reaches the request in flight through a `LISTEN` on `llm_cancel`. Partial text already streamed is kept as the `assistant_reply`; only a cancel with nothing streamed yet answers with no message at all
- Progress streams over the existing SSE connection as `event: llm` (`queued` → `start` → `delta` → `end`/`error`). A **queued** question is caught up on any `assistant_reply` that landed while it waited, appended after its own selection, since those did not exist when the client built it
- **Every attempt republishes `start`** — a second agent, or the same one retried without `thinking` — which drops whatever the abandoned attempt streamed, from the registry and from every bubble alike. It carries the run's current `seq`, since that counter never restarts: a client re-anchors its reorder buffer to it rather than to `0`, or holds the whole attempt waiting on deltas that already went by
- **A client connecting mid-queue or mid-stream gets a backfill only where the in-memory generation registry is populated** — a Vercel invocation's memory does not survive past its own request, so this is effectively a `NEXT_OUTPUT_STANDALONE` deployment's benefit; on Vercel a reconnecting client sees only the queued/streaming indicator and the eventual `assistant_reply` echo
- **The streaming row is not a virtualized row**, unlike every other row § 8.3.'s estimate prices ahead of render — it is simply mounted in flow and grows with each delta, and stays up until the finished `assistant_reply` echoes back rather than dropping the moment the stream ends
- **Gemini is branded 쨈미니**, with its own avatar and background art — an unrecognised or not-yet-started provider falls back to a bare `AI` label
- **Attachments Gemini can read inline share one 14MB byte budget across the whole question**, not one per attachment — an item that would push the running total over falls back to its Korean description instead of its bytes, and a voice message's container mime is deliberately excluded so it almost always falls back to text
- **Seeding a provider is a hand-written `INSERT`**, e.g. `INSERT INTO llm_agents (provider, model, api_key, priority) VALUES ('gemini', '<model id>', '<key>', 100);` — no id column; `(provider, model, api_key)` is the primary key
- **A finished `assistant_reply` pushes both participants, never only the asker** (§ 16.1.) — `sender_id` is the asker, but they may have left the app while the answer streamed, so the usual "everyone but the sender" rule is inverted here. 조용히 보내기 narrows this to the asker alone rather than to nobody, since asking is what earns the answer
- The push title names the provider (`toLlmProviderBranding(message.llmProvider).name`, e.g. `쨈미니`) rather than the asker, and the body is the same `toMessageSummary`-truncated text an ordinary send's banner carries
- `POST /api/chat/ai` reads 조용히 보내기 once, at request time — a question that queues behind the FIFO keeps the value it was asked with, not whatever the toggle becomes while it waits

---

## 9. Media Storage (R2) ✅

- `POST /api/media/upload-url` issues a **pair** of presigned PUTs — the object and its `{key}_thumb` sibling. The upload goes **client → R2 directly**, which gets a full-resolution iPhone photo past Vercel's 4.5MB request body limit and makes `XMLHttpRequest`'s `upload.onprogress` a real byte count
- **There is no registration endpoint, and a row is written by the request that attaches it** — `POST /api/messages`, `PATCH /api/users/me`, `PATCH /api/chat/background`, § 13.4.'s emoticon writes — inside that request's own transaction. Registering on its own committed a row whose referrer was a _later_ request, and a referrer that never came left a live row nothing pointed at, its claim already spent and its `deleted_at` NULL, which no reclaim marks and no sweep sees. The two halves are split rather than merged: `validateMediaUpload` runs the R2 `HeadObject`s and every rule below **outside** any transaction — they are network round trips, and a pooled connection held across one is how a two-person app runs out of connections — and `insertMedia` performs the INSERT on the caller's transaction, so a refusal past it rolls the rows back with the write. **`uploadDraft` therefore registers nothing**: it PUTs and answers a `MediaUpload`, the key plus what only the browser could measure (the box, a duration, the § 9.3. peaks, the blurhash, the picked name)
- **The key is built server-side from the uploader's id** (`{scope}/{userId}/{uuid}`, `toScopePrefix`) and never read off the request — a signature the browser could aim is a signature that overwrites any object in the bucket. `validateMediaUpload` re-checks that prefix, and **that is the whole of the ownership check**: the key carries the uploader's id and the scope, so no separate test of who owns what is left to keep in step with it. `MEDIA_UPLOAD_SCOPES` is `chat`, `avatar` (§ 12.) and `background` (§ 12.1. and § 12.2. both, which is why neither may discard an object without asking `isMediaWorn` first), deliberately **not** `emoticon`, or a § 13.3. object could be claimed as a `media` row and would then be a library row
- **`validateMediaUpload` is the only place § 14.'s type and size limits actually hold — for the thumbnail as much as for the original.** Verified against the live bucket: **R2 enforces neither the signed `Content-Type` nor any size on a presigned PUT.** So the server `HeadObject`s both keys, reads back what R2 really holds, and refuses to write a `media` row for anything outside the allow-list. Nothing in the app addresses R2 by key, only by `media.id`, so an unregistered object is unreachable — **which is still true and is no longer the whole story**: unreachable used to be the end of it, and the invariant below is what now says where those bytes go
- Caps: **50MB per photo, 500MB per video** (`shared/config/media.ts`). The video ceiling is an upload-**reliability** limit, not a storage one — a presigned PUT is one request with no resume, so raising it means adopting multipart upload, not a bigger number
- **`variant=edit` is a third variant and the one that does not redirect** — it is the original again, **streamed through this origin** with `readObject`. `CLAUDE.md § 5.3.` is the whole argument: the 302 lands cross-origin, so a canvas drawn from it is tainted unless the fetch is made in CORS mode, and a CORS request is cached separately from the plain one every `<img>` and preload makes — the photo is downloaded twice, the read answers differently cold and warm, and a bucket policy that does not name the origin turns a failed crop into a failed image load. Streaming same-origin avoids all of it and changes no bucket configuration. **It re-states the allow-list rather than inheriting one**, refusing any row with a `filename` and any mime that is not an image or a video: answering bytes from this origin skips § 9.1.'s attachment forcing, and `isFileMime` is a shape test rather than an allow-list — `image/svg+xml` is deliberately filed as an attachment (§ 14.) precisely so nothing renders it, and one streamed inline would run its own script under the app's origin and session. **`thumb` and `original` are untouched**: the display path keeps its redirect and its cache, and this variant is asked for once, when § 12.1.'s editor opens. Its ceiling is `maxSizeForScope(mime, "background")` rather than `maxSizeForMime`, which is what keeps a 500MB chat video from being buffered into a response
- `GET /api/media/{id}?variant=thumb|original` validates the session and `302`s to a presigned GET. `variant` defaults to `thumb`; only the fullscreen viewer asks for `original`. Same-origin, so a bare `<img src>` carries the session cookie. `Cache-Control: private, max-age` is **shorter than `MEDIA_URL_EXPIRY - MEDIA_SIGNING_BUCKET`** — not merely than the expiry, since the URL is dated to the start of its window — or the browser replays a cached redirect to a signature R2 has stopped honouring. `MEDIA_CACHE_MAX_AGE` is **a day**, on § 13.3.'s terms: it used to be 5 minutes on the argument that a `media` URL carries no version, but the only thing that changes behind one is deletion, and the read side recovers from that (§ 13.3.'s cache-busted retry, and the row's own `deleted_at`, which is what actually draws the tombstone)
- **The bytes get a `Cache-Control` of their own and a URL stable enough to be found under it, and neither half works alone.** R2 stores no header, so with nothing signed in, the browser fell back to a heuristic tenth of the object's age — a month for last year's picture and nearly nothing for yesterday's, which made the newest month, the one 보관함 opens on, the one that never cached. `MEDIA_ASSET_CACHE_CONTROL` is safe as `immutable` because the key holds a UUID and nothing rewrites an object at one: a replaced avatar or wallpaper is a new row at a new key. `MEDIA_SIGNING_BUCKET` (**5 days**) is the other half — measured on 보관함, two requests three seconds apart returned two `X-Amz-Date`s, and a cache keyed by URL therefore paid the whole 158KB thumbnail again every time a 302 fell out. `MEDIA_URL_EXPIRY` is **7 days**, SigV4's ceiling, and the grid and the redirect cache are both spent out of it: 5 + 1 < 7, so a URL handed out at the very end of a window still has 2 days of signature left and may be replayed for 1 of them. The hour it held before was shorter than any redirect cache worth keeping, which is what left the year-long asset cache unreachable — the numbers were § 13.3.'s all along and only the version parameter was ever the difference
- **Never rely on a UUID in the URL as access control** — every read revalidates the session **and then checks the object itself**: readable when the caller owns it, when it was put in the library, when it hangs off a message that has not been deleted, or when `isMediaWorn` finds it on an avatar (§ 12.), a profile cover (§ 12.1.) or the shared chat wallpaper (§ 12.2.). An unreadable id answers **404 rather than 403**, so the response cannot confirm it exists
- **A soft-deleted row is refused before it is signed**, ahead of the readability check and on the same 404. Its bytes belong to the reclaim once `MEDIA_DELETE_GRACE` has run, so signing that key mints a signature for an object we deleted — replayable for `MEDIA_CACHE_MAX_AGE`, which is now a day. This does not touch the grace, which covers a 302 **already answered** whose GET at R2 has not landed; refusing affects only a request arriving now, and the object really is gone. Nothing legitimately reads a deleted row through this route: every screen that renders one draws `MediaTombstone` from `isDeleted` on its payload instead, `isInLibrary` keeps deleted rows out of 보관함, the § 8.1. track query excludes them, and the § 16.2. offline mirror carries no media URLs at all
- **Saving the original is a `Content-Disposition` on the presigned GET**, requested with `?download=1`. An `<a download>` is not enough: the route answers a 302 and the spec makes the browser drop the attribute once the navigation resolves cross-origin, which in a standalone PWA replaces the app with a bare asset view and no way back
- **Attachments are re-encoded in the browser before the PUT, at send rather than at pick** — `optimizeDraft`, off `NEXT_PUBLIC_UPLOAD_OPTIMIZATION` (default on). A still image becomes AVIF capped at 1920 on the long edge; a video is re-encoded to H.264/MP4 at that same cap under a resolution-tiered bitrate ceiling. **Every failure answers the original file** — a saving that cannot be had is not a send that fails — and so does a result that came out no smaller. Send is the phase because the bytes are already staged and the bubble is already drawn, so the re-encode has a place to report progress into; pick would have held the tray open instead. **A re-encode that shrinks the picture must say so**, and `MediaUpload` takes its box over the draft's: the draft measured § 8.3.'s box from the original, and the row has to record the resolution the stored object actually has
- **An animated image is never drawn to a canvas — that would flatten it to one frame — and is re-encoded a different way.** `optimizeDraft` reads the container itself (`isAnimatedImage`) ahead of the still path and routes an animated GIF/WebP/APNG to `optimizeAnimation` instead, which transcodes it to animated WebP through a self-hosted `@ffmpeg/ffmpeg` (`-c:v libwebp_anim`, never plain `libwebp` — the latter loses libwebp's inter-frame optimization while still producing a playable file). `encodeCanvas`/`encodeStillImage` keep their own `isAnimatedImage` refusal as the structural guard regardless, since a still pick outside `optimizeDraft` (an emoticon's still slot, § 13.2.) still needs one. The ffmpeg core (~32MB) is `import()`ed only on the first animated send and is not part of the PWA precache (`scripts/generate-offline-precache.ts` only walks `.next/static`). A voice message (§ 9.3.) is excluded from both still and animated paths — it is already bounded by `MAX_VOICE_DURATION`
- **AVIF is produced by wasm (`@jsquash/avif`), never by `canvas.toBlob`.** Only Chrome encodes AVIF natively, and the spec makes the other engines answer a **PNG** with no error at all — which is routinely larger than the JPEG it replaced, so the optimization would silently invert. The encoder is `import()`ed on first use so its wasm stays out of the main bundle, and an engine it cannot run on falls back to JPEG
- Thumbnails are rendered in the browser at pick time (long edge 720, AVIF, falling back to JPEG on an engine that cannot encode one) and uploaded beside the original. **The uploader declares which of the two it produced** and the ticket signs the `_thumb` PUT for that type (`THUMBNAIL_MIMES`, defaulting to JPEG for a client predating the field): a presigned PUT pins `Content-Type`, so deciding the format server-side alone would let a stale PWA store JPEG bytes under an AVIF signature, under the key every cell loads. For a video the same slot holds the **poster frame**, extracted by seeking a decoded `<video>` onto a canvas — which on iOS requires `muted` + `playsInline` + an actual `play()`, since WebKit will not decode a frame for a video that has never played. The seek is guarded on **both** sides: a container whose `duration` is `NaN` at `loadedmetadata` throws when assigned to `currentTime`, and seeking to the position the video already holds fires no `seeked` at all — either way the promise never settles, and decoding is serial, so one such file silently swallows the rest of the pick. A timeout backs both guards
- R2 credentials live only in server env. `shared/storage` is `server-only`; `toMediaUrl` lives in `shared/config` rather than `entities/media`, because a client value-import from that barrel drags `server-only` into the browser bundle
- Read path for chat: the `message_media` rows for a whole page arrive in **one** query, ordered by `sort_order`, and only when the page actually holds an attachment
- **Nothing in this app deletes a stored object inline except an emoticon's** (§ 13.4.). Every other removal stamps `deleted_at` and the reclaim takes the bytes once `MEDIA_DELETE_GRACE` has passed — 보관함's 삭제 (§ 10.), a withdrawn message's media (§ 8.13.), a replaced avatar, cover or wallpaper (§ 12.)
- **R2 bucket configuration is a deploy step, not code** (§ 15.): private with public access fully disabled, and a CORS policy allowing `PUT` **and `GET`** from the app origin — without `PUT` every upload fails preflight, without `GET` the § 10. share route cannot read the bytes it hands to the share sheet. `AllowedHeaders` needs **`Content-Type` only**, and no upload may add a second header without this line changing first: the live policy names headers one by one. That is not hypothetical — § 13.3. once sent a `Cache-Control` on the emoticon PUT and every emoticon upload failed until the header moved to the download side
- **`media.blurhash` is written at the pick, and it stands in for the object on every screen that draws one.** The column has existed since `0001` and was dead until now — a loading tile was an empty box held open by § 8.3.'s reservation. It is encoded in the browser from the **thumbnail canvas itself**: `renderThumbnail` hands back the blob and the hash together, at a 32px long edge with **4 components down the long axis and 3 across the short one** — the budget is fixed, and a phone's portrait photo needs its frequencies down the picture rather than across it, so the pair is swapped rather than pinned to `x`/`y`. That is a correctness rule rather than a convenience. The hash stands in for the `_thumb` object and for nothing else, so one taken from the source pixels morphs into a different picture at the moment the real one lands — a video's poster is seeked well past frame 0 (`toPosterTime`) and `MediaEditor`'s crop and filters make the original a different image again. `toBlurhash` is deliberately un-exported so it cannot be handed anything but the canvas that produced the object, and `applyEdit` re-derives it with the rest rather than carrying the pick's over. `DESIGN.md § 7.8.` owns what is drawn with it; § 12.2. additionally reads its average colour
- **A malformed hash is nulled at registration, never refused.** `mediaUploadSchema` shape-checks it with the encoder's own `isBlurhashValid` — the leading character encodes the component counts the rest of the length has to agree with, so a regex here would be a second copy of the wire format — and then `.catch(null)`. This is § 9.1.'s `filename` rule reaching a second column, and what a nulled placeholder costs is the empty box every row drew before this column was written, which is the cheapest failure anywhere on this path. Shape is also **all** that is checked — the server never sees those pixels, so it cannot tell a forged hash from an honest one, and the consequence is bounded the way § 9.3.'s client-supplied peaks are: a wrong blur is replaced by the thumbnail a moment later, and nothing branches on the value
- **A file attachment and a voice message store none.** Neither uploaded a `_thumb` object for a placeholder to stand in for, and neither is drawn in a box one could be painted into (§ 9.1., § 9.3.) — so `validateMediaUpload` nulls the hash from the same `hasNoBox` test it zeroes `width`/`height` with. **A worn photo needs no exception any more**: § 12.1.'s 사진 사용하기 crops in the browser and uploads through this pipeline, so the § 12.2. wallpaper's hash — which is also its chrome colour — is encoded from the pixels it was made from, like every other pick. The server-side copy that used to carry a source's hash across is gone

**The invariant (`0044`): no object may exist in R2 that a row did not name first, and the record outlives the bytes.**

- **A key is claimed with `reserveKey` before anything is signed, copied or PUT to it.** Both `upload-url` routes reserve before presigning — a signature handed out first may be redeemed while that request is still in flight — and the rule binds any future server-side write the same way: a claim placed afterwards is the one thing a half-landed write skips, on exactly the path that has already put bytes in the bucket. **There is no such writer today** — `copyObject` went with § 12.1.'s copy route, and every object now arrives through a presigned PUT
- **A reservation is a claim on a _key_, never a claim about bytes.** It says the key is spoken for and nothing else; § 14. still holds at registration alone, against what R2 actually stored. `insertMedia` consumes the claim inside the attaching request's transaction, with the freshness test in the `DELETE`'s own qual so the answer is still true at the moment it is used (§ 12.2.). A claim that has **lapsed** is a refusal — a reclaim may already have taken the bytes the row would point at. **No claim at all is not a refusal**: registration is idempotent on `r2_key`, so a retry legitimately arrives after the first attempt consumed it
- **`_thumb` is reserved implicitly, with its base key**, matching how `media` already holds the pair in one row. A row of its own would split a convention `purgeNow` and jandh-ops each resolve in one place
- **`STORAGE_RESERVATION_TTL` MUST outlast `UPLOAD_URL_EXPIRY`**, or a reclaim runs while the ticket for that key can still be redeemed and the late PUT lands with nothing naming it. It is a day rather than a wider ten minutes because § 13.7.'s import reserves across chunks and finishes long after the first signature expires. `expires_at` is computed by the **database**, since the reclaim compares it against `now()` on that same clock
- **Bytes first, stamp second, and only the keys R2 confirmed — and never onto a live row.** A failed delete then leaves the row exactly as the next pass expects to find it, which is why there is no attempt counter and no dead-letter state — and why `deleteObjects` answers **the keys R2 confirmed** rather than swallowing the outcome. It is confirmations and never the complement of the errors: `DeleteObjects` fails per key inside a 200, so inferring success from a missing error costs an orphan nothing looks for again, where leaving a key unstamped costs one no-op delete on the next pass. `Quiet` mode stays off for the same reason — it omits `Deleted` entirely, which would read as every key having failed — and the call is batched at 1000, because `DeleteObjects` refuses a larger payload outright and a refusal confirms nothing. jandh-ops found the reachable case: an `AWS_DEFAULT_OUTPUT=text` made every response unparseable at exit 0, which the complement reading would have taken as total success. **The `media` stamp additionally requires `deleted_at IS NOT NULL`, and that is a guard rather than a filter**: a live row stamped as purged leaves the queue while `GET /api/media/{id}` still serves it, and nothing marks that state, so no later pass can find it — let alone repair it. jandh-ops carries the same guard
- **The reclaim runs in two places, and jandh's own copy is why jandh-ops is removable.** `reclaimExpiredStorage` performs the same two passes `POST /api/purge` does — soft-deleted `media` past `MEDIA_DELETE_GRACE`, then expired claims under the same `NOT EXISTS` guard — bounded per call and handed to `purgeNow`. Both services may select the same candidates and issue the same deletes: deleting a key R2 no longer holds succeeds, and every stamp is guarded on `r2_purged_at IS NULL`, so the two need no lock between them. **It is triggered by `POST /api/media/upload-url`, inside `after`**, and that is its known weakness — the work is produced by _deletes_ and paid for by _uploads_, so a stretch of deleting without uploading leaves the bytes in the bucket until § 12.4.'s interval catches them. Accepted: this is the half that keeps cleanup alive without that service, not the schedule anything depends on
- **What this buys is that leftover garbage is _row_-shaped rather than key-shaped.** An orphan is structurally impossible instead of merely findable, so what is owed can be enumerated in SQL where the old answer was a whole bucket listing subtracted from the database (§ 12.4.). **The row-shaped remainder is closed by the bullet above**: a registration shares its attacher's transaction, so a write that fails past the INSERT rolls those rows back with it rather than leaving live ones nothing points at. `pnpm ops:orphans` is what clears the ones already stored: it reports unreferenced live rows older than an hour and writes only under `ORPHANS_WRITE=true`, which soft-deletes them into the reclaim

### 9.1. File Attachments ✅

Any file, not only what the app can draw. The composer's `+` offers `파일` beside `사진/영상`, and the same object goes to R2 the way a photo does — what differs is that nothing renders it, which is exactly what the rules below protect.

- **`media.filename` is the discriminator, and the server sets it.** A row with a filename is a file attachment; a row without one is a photo or a video. `validateMediaUpload` decides that from the mime R2 actually stored (`isFileMime` = shaped like a mime **and** outside the § 9. media allow-list) and never from the client's claim — letting the client set it would file a JPEG out of the library, or claim a `.zip` was a photo and put an undrawable row in the grid
- **A type the app cannot render is a file, rather than a refusal.** That is what makes the two lists one list: `image/svg+xml` and a `.mkv` arrive as downloadable cards instead of as broken tiles, and no second allow-list has to be kept in step with the first. `MIME_PATTERN` is a shape check, not a policy — the compensating control is the disposition below
- **One PUT, not the § 9. pair.** There is no frame to render a thumbnail from, so `POST /api/media/upload-url` signs no `_thumb` URL and `validateMediaUpload` requires no `_thumb` object. Both HEADs still go out together, because deciding first would put the two round trips in series on the send path
- **`GET /api/media/{id}` serves a file as `Content-Disposition: attachment` whatever the query asks for**, under its stored name in RFC 5987 form (`filename*=UTF-8''…`, which is what carries a Korean document title). It is also forced to the `original` variant, since there is no thumb object to answer a default `?variant=thumb` with. Nothing in the app _navigates_ to one, so a download is the only thing that URL is ever opened as — and it is why an arbitrary stored type is not a rendering risk
- **The one thing that reads that URL without downloading it is a media element**, which is how 보관함's audio row plays (below). `Content-Disposition` governs a **navigation**; an `<audio>` fetching a resource ignores it entirely, so the inline player needed no second route and the header stayed exactly where the security argument wants it. **Do not relax the disposition to make some future inline view work** — the 파일 shelf's 저장 is a navigation, and it would start opening documents in the browser instead of saving them
- **`width`/`height` are stored as `0` and the name is sanitized at registration** — `toSafeFilename` strips control characters and path segments, so nothing a drop carries can be built into that header. It truncates **by code point**: a `slice` counts UTF-16 units and would cut a trailing emoji inside its surrogate pair, and the lone surrogate left behind makes the header encoder throw on every later attempt to save that file. The encoder is not `encodeURIComponent` alone either — that leaves `'` unescaped, which is the delimiter `UTF-8''…` is built from
- **A file never reaches the 사진 grid, the viewer, or a background — but it does reach 보관함, under its own segment.** The kind test is `isOfKind` (§ 10.), which is `filename IS NOT NULL` for 파일 and, for 사진, `filename IS NULL` narrowed by the § 9.3. voice clause; `validateMediaUpload` still refuses a file under the `avatar` and `background` scopes, an avatar or a background being drawn from its own object; the § 8.10. quote drops its thumbnail and shows `파일` from `toMediaKind`. Until the 파일 segment existed the exclusion was total, and a sent file could not be found again anywhere — that is what § 10. fixed
- **§ 8.11.'s 공유 is withheld on a chat bubble, and that is the whole of its scope — 보관함's 파일 segment does offer it.** The original reason was that `useMediaShare` was written for photos: it named every `File` from the mime, which for a file attachment has no extension and produced `{uuid}.bin`, and it worded each step in 사진 and 장. **That was a fact about the code path, never about files**, so § 10. fixed the path rather than inheriting the exclusion — `collectShareFiles` takes an id→name map and every counted sentence takes its counter. What is left on the bubble is a different and durable argument: the card's own tap already downloads it, so a second route to the same bytes in the hold sheet is a row that does nothing new. In 보관함 a selection of several is the thing being handed over and no tap has downloaded any of them
- **A shared file MUST be named from `media.filename`.** R2 keys carry no name, and the server's own `Content-Disposition` cannot be read back in the browser: the header is not CORS-safelisted and the response has already redirected cross-origin (§ 9.). Reaching it would mean adding `ExposeHeaders` to the bucket policy, which is a deploy step (§ 15.) — passing the names the client already holds is the cheaper and more direct answer
- **A file's 저장 is the download route on every platform, iOS included.** § 10.'s `isIos()` branch exists because the share sheet is the only way to the **photo library**, and a document has no business there: on iOS a downloaded file lands in Files, which is exactly where it belongs. So `savesToPhotoLibrary` is false for the 파일 segment, and with it go both the merged `저장/공유` row and the `MAX_ARCHIVE_SHARE_FILES` selection cap iOS otherwise imposes. **Do not reuse § 10.'s platform branch for files**
- **Tapping a file card saves it, and says so** (`파일을 저장하고 있어요`). `downloadMedia` clicks a detached anchor and resolves whatever the navigation then does, so a 404, a blocked popup or an iOS standalone context that swallows it would otherwise leave a tap with no feedback at all — the same reason § 10.'s 저장 toasts, since the control that started it is gone by the time anything happens
- **An audio attachment plays where it sits in the 파일 shelf, and it is still a file.** The row grows an `AudioPlayButton` in front of the card, drawn as `VoicePlayer`'s transport is; the card's own tap is unchanged and still saves. **They are siblings, never nested** — `FileCard` is a `<button>`, so a control inside it would be invalid markup and one of the two taps would be unreachable. The card takes a `progress` fill along its bottom edge and swaps its size line for an elapsed clock while it is the active track, both of which are `undefined` on every other file row. Playback goes through `shared/lib/audio`'s **one shared element** (§ 13.6.), which is what keeps two clips from running at once and what carries the audio-session decision; the shelf `stopVoice()`s on unmount, as the room and the 음성 shelf do
- **Why an attached audio file is not moved to the 음성 shelf, now that it plays.** The old reason written here — "it is a thing that downloads when tapped" — stopped being true the moment the row grew a transport, and it was never the real one. **The real reason is the cost of a waveform.** § 9.3.'s discriminator is `waveform_peaks`, and peaks are produced by sampling an `AnalyserNode` _while recording_, which is affordable only because `MAX_VOICE_DURATION` caps a recording at two minutes. An attachment has no cap at all, so extracting peaks would mean decoding an arbitrarily long clip in the browser and holding it in memory. So the two are genuinely different objects: **a recording is audio that has a waveform, and an attached audio file is audio that only plays.** That is also why the player here is a play control and a 2px progress rule rather than `VoicePlayer` — that component takes `peaks` as a required input and draws its whole seek surface from them
- **The audio row also needs no stored duration.** `validateMediaUpload` nulls `duration_ms` for a file, and nothing recomputes it — `useVoicePlayback` is passed `0` and falls back to what the element resolves at `loadedmetadata`, published through the shared store as `elementDurationMs`. The consequence is deliberate and visible: a row draws no progress until its first tap, because until the track is adopted there is nothing that knows how long it is
- **What kind a pick is gets resolved one way, through `toStoredMime`.** An empty `File.type` is routine, and reading it raw in the composer's media-only gate meant the same typeless JPEG staged as a file card in the room and was refused outright in the library — the two screens have to agree on what a file _is_ before they can differ on whether they take it
- **`mediaUploadSchema` puts no length bound on `filename` at all**, neither `.max()` nor a code-point refine. The bytes are in R2 before the schema ever sees the name, and `toSafeFilename` already truncates it by code point — so a rejection here throws away an upload the user has paid for, and the retry pays again for the same 400. Sanitize where sanitizing is honest; refuse only where the value is one the row must not carry
- **A typeless pick is resolved from its own name before the fallback is taken.** Routing it straight to `FALLBACK_FILE_MIME` made both screens agree on the wrong answer: `application/octet-stream` fails the media gate exactly as `""` does, so calling `toStoredMime` in front of that gate changed nothing at all and the divergence above went on shipping. `toMediaMimeFromName` reads the extension against the **reverse of `MIME_EXTENSIONS`** — one table, so the name a type is stored under and the type a name implies cannot drift — and answers the § 9. allow-list or nothing, since guessing a type the app cannot draw would push a file attachment onto the media path instead. `File.type` still wins whenever the browser reported one: it read the bytes, where an extension is only a claim. The resolved type is what the draft carries into the signed PUT, or a name-resolved photo would be uploaded under an empty `Content-Type` and refused at registration with the bytes already in R2
- **A bubble is files or photos, never both, and `isHomogeneousBatch` is where that holds.** § 6. gives one `messages` row one layout and the two are drawn at different heights, while five readers — the grid, the § 8.3. estimate, `toMediaKind`, the quote and 공유 — take the whole bubble from `media[0]`. `toBubbles` splits a pick into **consecutive runs** by kind before chunking each by `MAX_MEDIA_PER_MESSAGE` (so a pick of photo, file, photo keeps the order it was made in), but that runs in the browser and a stale client does not — so `createMediaMessage` refuses a mixed `media` set outright, before it registers anything
- **A zero `width`/`height` is accepted only from a file.** `mediaUploadSchema` had to widen to admit the one kind with no box to measure, so `validateMediaUpload` re-establishes positivity for everything else: `toMediaBoxHeight` divides by `width` for a single attachment, and one stored zero resolves the virtualized list's total size to `NaN` and stops it laying out entirely
- **A zero-byte pick is refused at the pick** (`빈 파일은 보낼 수 없어요`), because `POST /api/media/upload-url` takes a `positive` size — staged, it produced a bubble whose 재전송 reproduced the identical 400 forever. Media never reached that: a zero-byte image or video fails to decode and is already refused with `파일을 읽지 못했어요`
- **A file's mime is bounded in length as well as in shape.** The file branch admits anything matching the mime pattern, and that pattern constrains no length — so an unbounded client string was signed into the PUT's `Content-Type`, stored on the row, and echoed by every download. `isFileMime` caps it at 255, which is where every other user-controlled string on this path already was
- **The size ceiling refuses an unrecognised type as its own branch, never by returning `0`.** The registration test is `size <= ceiling`, and a zero-byte object passes `0 <= 0` — reachable, because a presigned PUT enforces neither the signed type nor any size (§ 9.)
- **`POST /api/media/upload-url` mirrors the scope rule rather than leaving it to registration.** A ticket is live for `UPLOAD_URL_EXPIRY`, so refusing a file only at registration means a 500MB archive already sitting in `background/` with no row able to name it
- Cap: **500MB**, the same upload-reliability ceiling § 9. sets for a video and for the same reason — one presigned PUT with no resume

### 9.2. Drag, Drop and Paste ✅

Dropping photos, videos and files anywhere over the chat room stages them in the composer's tray, exactly as a pick does. **The 보관함 tab takes the same drop** (§ 10.), into the same staging tray its picker fills. **Pasting one into the chat room does the same thing** — the rules the two share are below, then the paste's own.

- **Desktop-only by nature, never by a branch.** Touch platforms fire no drag events, so this needs no pointer or user-agent test to stay off there — `AGENTS.md § 4.2.`'s one sanctioned UA branch stays the only one
- **A drag is armed off `dataTransfer.types`, never `files`** — the file list is empty on `dragover` for security, so a guard reading it would never arm, and this is also what keeps a dragged text selection from lighting the overlay up
- **`dragover` must `preventDefault` on every event**, not only the first: the default action is what the browser reads as "this target does not take drops"
- **Enter/leave is counted, not flagged** — `dragenter` fires again for every child the cursor crosses, so a boolean flickers off over every bubble the pointer passes
- **A window-level `preventDefault` swallows drops that miss.** Unguarded, the browser navigates to the file, which in a standalone PWA replaces the app with a bare asset view and no way back. It is `FileDropGuard`, **mounted once on the `(main)` shell rather than inside the drop targets** — the hazard is app-global while only two of the five screens under the shell take a drop, so a guard living in the hook left 캘린더, 설정 and the emoticon screens navigable straight out of the app
- **The guard also has to _say_ it is refusing** — `dropEffect = "none"` beside the `preventDefault`. Cancelling `dragover` is what makes an element a valid drop target, so the guard alone had every screen in the app advertising the copy cursor for a file it then discarded in silence. **It sets that only on a drag nothing else claimed**, tested through `defaultPrevented`: `useFileDrop` runs first (React dispatches from the root, which is below `window` in the bubble path) and sets `copy`, and overwriting that with `none` would not merely restore the wrong cursor — a target whose effect is `none` is delivered no `drop` event at all, so the chat room and the library would stop taking files entirely
- **The overlay is `pointer-events-none` and never unmounted.** An overlay that takes pointer events appears under the cursor mid-drag, the target below reads that as `dragleave`, and the whole thing flickers at frame rate while the drop never lands
- **A dropped folder is discarded through `webkitGetAsEntry`** — it arrives as a `File` with no type and no bytes, which no size check can tell from a real empty file
- Drops are refused for the length of a § 8.6. search, where the composer and its tray are put away and nothing could send what was staged
- **In the library they are refused for the length of a selection** for the same class of reason: the selection bar deletes, and a drop that uploaded into the grid behind it would recreate exactly the window § 10. closes by withholding selection while an upload is in flight
- **They are refused under an editor or the viewer on both screens.** React bubbles a drop through the _component_ tree, not the DOM one, so `MediaEditor`, `VideoTrimmer` and the § 7.10. viewer all deliver one to the target they are rendered inside however they are portalled — and the staging sheet is suppressed for exactly the duration of the two editors, so the drop staged an attachment into a tray the user could not see. Every refusal reason is passed by the caller, since only the screen knows what is covering its tray
- **The library's overlay is portalled into the shell box; the room's is not.** The library screen _is_ the shell scroller's content, so an `absolute inset-0` overlay left inside it spans every month ever loaded and centres its label far below the fold — `ShellOverlay` is the one box sized to what the user can see, and going `fixed` to reach it is what `AGENTS.md § 4.4.` rules out
- The overlay says what the drop lands in, in the screen's own words: `여기에 놓으면 첨부돼요` in the room, `여기에 놓으면 추가돼요` on **all three** shelves of 보관함. **The label is a required prop with no default** — defaulting it to either string would make the shared overlay partial to its first caller, and the next drop surface would inherit the wrong copy with nothing failing. The shelves share one string because they all reach the same two 갈래; a shelf-specific label would have to promise which shelf the drop lands on, which § 10. says it cannot
- **Only 사진 refuses a non-media drop** (`사진과 동영상만 올릴 수 있어요`, the picker's own `useMediaSelection` guard), because the grid draws thumbnails and a § 9.1. file has none. 파일 and 음성 pass `acceptsFiles: true` and take anything; where it lands and how that is reported is § 10.

**Paste** (`useFilePaste`), on the chat room only:

- **`toTransferFiles` is shared with the drop; the `types.includes("Files")` arming test is deliberately _not_.** That sentinel exists because `DataTransfer.files` is empty during `dragover`, which is a fact about dragging that no clipboard shares — pre-gating on it would make the extraction's own `files` fallback unreachable and would stake the whole feature on an engine spelling a pasted screenshot's clipboard the way a drag spells it. A paste reads the items and asks only whether it got a file
- **The folder discard rides along but does nothing here, and that is accepted.** `webkitGetAsEntry` answers `null` for an item that did not come from a drag, so a pasted folder is not recognised as one. It stages as the zero-byte typeless `File` a folder arrives as, which § 9.1.'s pick-time refusal stops before R2 — the cost is that it is refused as `빈 파일은 보낼 수 없어요` rather than dropped in silence
- **Refused by the same three covers, through the same flag** — a § 8.6. search, an editor, the § 7.10. viewer. It stages into the tray the drop stages into, so a separate guard could only drift from it
- **And by a fourth that only this route needs: the room's own box.** A `window` listener has no position in the React tree, so it does not inherit the scoping a drop gets from React dispatching through the _component_ tree — § 8.4.1.'s 절전 모드 overlay is a sibling of `ChatRoom` and takes focus on mount, so a paste there would `preventDefault` and stage behind a surface covering the whole screen. `containerRef` is a **required** prop with no default: the target must be inside the room, or `body`/`html`, which is what a paste with nothing focused reports and is the case the `window` listener exists for
- **It is the one attachment route that is _not_ desktop-only, which is why it earns its place beside the drop.** Touch platforms fire no drag events at all, so a phone otherwise reaches an attachment through the `+` picker alone — and a paste is how iOS hands the app a screenshot without leaving the room. It still needs no branch: an engine with nothing on its clipboard never fires with files
- **The listener is on `window`, never on the composer's field.** A `paste` is dispatched at the focused element and bubbles, and with nothing focused the engine dispatches it at `body` — a field-level handler would take one only while the keyboard was already up, which is the case where a photo is least likely to be what is being pasted
- **Files win over any text riding the same clipboard, and the event is cancelled when they do.** A copy out of a document carries `text/plain`, `text/html` and an image at once, so preferring the text would put the feature out of reach of every rich source; not cancelling would insert the text half into the composer beside the attachment it had just staged. Verified against the macOS pasteboard, which is the case that decided it: a copied file carries `«class furl»` and **no string at all**, so nothing is lost on the path this exists for. The accepted cost is that a spreadsheet region pastes as its image
- **It needs no `FileDropGuard` counterpart.** An ignored drop navigates the window to the file and strands a standalone PWA with no way back (above); an ignored paste inserts text into whatever is focused, or does nothing
- **보관함 takes the drop but not the paste.** There is no field there a paste is already aimed at, and the shelves' 추가 control is one tap. `useFilePaste` is generic, so adopting it later is a call site and nothing else

### 9.3. Voice Messages ✅

A recording made in the composer, sent as its own bubble and played inline. It rides § 9.'s pipeline unchanged — what is new is a column, a discriminator, and a microphone.

**Why almost nothing in the transport had to change**

- **An audio mime was already a § 9.1. file mime.** `isFileMime` is "shaped like a mime **and** outside the § 9. media allow-list", and `audio/mp4` is exactly that — so `POST /api/media/upload-url` already signed the single thumbnail-less PUT, already refused every scope but `chat`, and registration already admitted a zero `width`/`height`. **The audio allow-list is deliberately not added to `ALLOWED_MEDIA_MIMES`**: doing so would make `isFileMime` false for `audio/mp4` and an attached `.m4a` would stop being a file attachment
- **The one genuinely new problem is telling a recording from an attached audio file**, and mime cannot: both are `audio/mp4`. That is what the discriminator below exists for, and it is why "just check `mime LIKE 'audio/%'`" is wrong

**The discriminator**

- **`media.waveform_peaks` is what makes a row a voice message** — peaks present, recording; peaks absent, not. It is the payload and the flag at once, which is the § 9.1. pattern (`filename` does the same job) applied a second time rather than a new idea
- **An explicit `media.kind` enum was weighed and rejected.** It is the more honest model and it is what a third or fourth kind would eventually want, but it costs a `NOT NULL` column backfilled across every existing row, leaves `filename` as a second source of truth for the same question, and rewrites the § 10. predicate that a parallel change was already restructuring. The derived column costs one nullable column, **no backfill at all** (every existing row is correctly "not a voice message"), and one clause in § 10. If a fourth kind arrives, revisit this — the argument is about the cost of the second kind, not a claim that derivation scales
- **The client supplies it, and that is the one place a discriminator is not server-decided.** It has to be: the server never sees the bytes (§ 9.), so it cannot extract a waveform. What `validateMediaUpload` enforces instead is that peaks may ride **only** a mime this app records into, **only** in the recorder's exact shape (`isWaveformPeaks` — the fixed count, integers in range), and **only** under the `chat` scope. Peaks that fail any part of that are a **refusal**, never a silent downgrade to a file — stored as a file, the object renders as a download card in the bubble the sender recorded a voice message into
- **A recording staged on the 음성 shelf is sent to the conversation like anything else** (§ 18. #1.). It has no filing-without-sending to be refused any more — the message is what puts it on the shelf
- **The consequence of a forged claim is bounded**, which is why client provenance is acceptable here where it was not for `filename`: the worst case is an attached `.m4a` rendered as a player instead of a card. Both are legitimate renderings of an audio file and both are outside the library, so nothing is exposed that was not already

**Storage**

- **Peaks are `smallint[]`, integers `0`–`VOICE_PEAK_SCALE`, always `VOICE_WAVEFORM_PEAKS` of them.** A float array doubles the row's width for precision no 2px bar can show. `toVoiceTrack` is the **one** place the scale is crossed into the `0`–`1` everything above the wire works in, and it answers `null` rather than `{ peaks: [] }` for a non-voice row — every reader tests that value for truthiness, so an empty track draws a photo bubble as a voice card
- **The peaks are extracted in the browser, while recording.** The server has no bytes to analyse and decoding server-side would mean buffering the upload through the app, which § 9. exists to avoid. An `AnalyserNode` sampled every `VOICE_SAMPLE_INTERVAL` yields RMS amplitudes, resampled at the end by **maximum per bucket, never mean** — speech is mostly silence between syllables and a mean flattens a sentence into a uniform band
- **`duration_ms` is wall-clock, measured by the recorder.** `MediaRecorder` writes no duration into a WebM at all and the MP4 yields one only after a decode — and the player draws its progress against this figure, so a null reads as a waveform that never moves. `validateMediaUpload` **keeps** it for a voice row where it nulls it for a file; that asymmetry is the whole point and it was a real bug first
- **`width`/`height` are `0`, as § 9.1. stores them for a file.** Both kinds are drawn at a fixed height, so there is no ratio to reserve — `hasNoBox` is the single test both take
- **A recording reserves one key and no `_thumb`** (§ 9.), for the same reason it is one PUT: there is no frame to render a thumbnail from

**Recording**

- **The container is negotiated at runtime through `MediaRecorder.isTypeSupported`, never hardcoded.** No type is both recordable and playable everywhere: WebKit records only MP4/AAC and **cannot play WebM at all**, while Firefox records only WebM/Opus. **MP4 leads the candidate list even though Opus is the better codec** — what matters is the receiving device, and a recording the other participant's phone refuses is worse than a larger one. A Firefox-recorded WebM is the accepted gap
- **The codecs parameter is stripped before the mime is stored.** `MediaRecorder` reports `audio/webm;codecs=opus`, and `MIME_PATTERN` refuses a `;` — unstripped, the PUT succeeds and registration then rejects the object _after_ the bytes are in the bucket
- **`MAX_VOICE_DURATION` is 2 minutes**, and the ceiling is legibility rather than bytes: the peak count is fixed, so a longer clip buys nothing but a coarser bar, and past this length a recording is a file rather than a turn in a conversation. Reaching it **stops** the recording and keeps what was said — losing two minutes to a limit nobody was watching is the failure this avoids. Under `MIN_VOICE_DURATION` it is dropped silently, since a press released immediately is a mis-tap and a toast would scold the user for a gesture they had already abandoned
- **A recording's `duration_ms` is required, not merely nullable, and registration refuses a voice row without one.** It is wall-clock from the browser and nothing on the server can recompute it, while the player draws its progress against it — at `0` the bubble reads `0:00` under a waveform that never fills however long the clip runs
- **`MAX_VOICE_SIZE` is enforced at registration only.** The ticket cannot narrow it — a recording and an attached `.m4a` are indistinguishable before the peaks arrive, so `upload-url` measures against the § 9.1. file ceiling and registration applies the real one
- **The microphone is refused for three different reasons and says which**, off `DOMException.name`: `마이크 권한이 필요해요`, `이 기기에서는 녹음할 수 없어요`, `녹음하지 못했어요`. One shared string leaves the user whose permission is off with nothing to act on
- **`start` runs inside a user gesture**, and the recorder bar starts the microphone from its own mount for that reason — from a **layout** effect, never a passive one. React schedules passive effects onto a task of their own that runs after paint, which is off the discrete event's call stack; Chromium and Gecko survive on the transient-activation window but WebKit gates `getUserMedia` on the stack itself and refuses the first recording on iOS as `NotAllowedError` without ever asking
