The breaker is **per year**, because a year already in the Data Cache costs no request at all and must not be withheld because the other one failed; it lives at module scope, so it is per serverless instance and a cold one pays a failed load of its own — that is the bound being bought, not an oversight
- **A run at runtime covers the current year and the next; `pnpm holidays` covers the current year and the two after it.** Past years never change, so asking for them costs a request and can only introduce error, and 특일 정보 answers nothing past the year after the current one. A year inside the window is replaced outright; everything outside it keeps what the table holds, which is what leaves the projected tail projected until a 월력요항 reaches it. `pnpm holidays --from=YYYY` widens the script's window backwards when that is deliberately wanted
- **A year replaces a committed one only if it is _covered_, and coverage is never a row count.** Asked about a year 월력요항 has not reached, 특일 정보 answers with the fixed dates it derives by rule and **nothing else** — around ten entries, which is enough to pass any `length > 0` test and is short exactly the tail no arithmetic can reconstruct. Every Korean year has 설날, 추석 and 부처님오신날, so an answer missing any of them is the rule-derived half of a year: the committed year is kept whole and one line says so. Without this the projected tail is not merely stale, it is a **hole** — every `2028-*` key deleted and nine written back, reading as settled
- **Twelve months per year, in rounds of four.** Nothing pins what omitting `solMonth` returns, so every month is named; but twelve simultaneous connections is the shape data.go.kr answers with `LIMITED_NUMBER_OF_SERVICE_REQUESTS_EXCEEDS_ERROR`, and because one month rejecting rejects the whole year, a throttled burst discards a year that was otherwise fine. One deadline covers a whole year's requests rather than each one separately — a per-request timeout multiplies by however many rounds there are, and a render cannot be bounded by a number that does that
- **The API's wording is not this app's copy.** It says `기독탄신일` where the UI says `성탄절`, and it names 설날 and 추석 alone where the cells read `설날 전날` and `설날 다음 날`. `shared/holiday/resolve.ts` maps every name explicitly and **fails the year on one it does not recognise**, rather than letting an unreviewed government string reach a cell. **That module is the single copy** — the runtime and `pnpm holidays` both resolve rows through it, because a second mapping beside the first is the drift this arrangement exists to prevent
- **A failed fetch is silent for the reader and loud in the log.** A timeout, a rejection, a non-JSON body, an unmapped name — any of them drops that year back to the committed table and writes one `[공휴일]` line naming the year, the reason, and how long the breaker will hold. Nothing surfaces on screen: the calendar must not lose its 빨간 날 because a government server had a bad afternoon. **The log line is the only alarm there is**, and it has to be, because a data.go.kr key is granted a two-year **활용기간** and the gateway refuses every call the day it lapses — behind a month-long cache, an expired key is otherwise a table that silently stops moving. A condition that persists rather than recurs (a 월력요항 not yet published) is stated **once per instance**, not once per render
- **No key at all is two different facts and the log must not conflate them.** Locally it is the documented default — `.env.example` says a key is not needed there — so development says so **once**, on `console.info`. On a deployment it is § 15.'s list unfulfilled, so it is a `console.error` on every load. One message for both is how a lapsed production key comes to read like somebody's laptop
- **The 140 inherited entries carry a claim nothing in the repository checks.** The commit that introduced them (`44345df`) states 월력요항 through 2027 and computation cross-checked against two further sources for 2028–2030 — but no fetch is recorded and no source is cited, so the claim rests on that message alone, which is absence of evidence rather than evidence of error. Runtime now overlays the current year and the next whatever the table says, so the claim only governs the years outside that window; `pnpm holidays --from=2024` re-derives them against the API, once, and its diff is worth reading date by date
- **It degrades by disappearing.** Past the table's last year every day answers `null` and the grid renders exactly as it did before this section existed. That is the trade being bought: a missing 빨간 날 is a non-event, where a Wednesday painted red on an assumed decree, or a `RangeError` thrown inside render on a swipe into an uncovered year, are not
- Election days are 공휴일 only for **임기만료 선거 already held or officially scheduled**; a future one is politically contingent (the 2025 조기 대선 is the proof) and is added when 선관위 confirms it, never projected. The API does not project one either, so this survives generation rather than being enforced against it
- **A holiday is not an event and takes no dot.** It rides its own field on the grid cell, so it can never consume one of § 11.3.'s three marker slots, and the agenda's holiday row is static like a milestone's — it opens nothing. `대체공휴일` is labelled as such rather than shown as a second copy of the same name
- **`primary` stays out of it.** The milestone diamond is `primary` (§ 11.2.) and Saturday's header letter is too; extending Saturday's colour to its numeral would collide with today's, so the grid colours Sunday and holidays only

---

## 12. Settings Tab ✅

**Landed:** the profile editor (nickname + avatar + cover), the tap-to-enlarge avatar, the 입력 중 표시 switch (§ 8.12.), the two backgrounds (§ 12.1., § 12.2.), the profile screen (§ 12.3.), 로그아웃, the device list and the ops console (§ 12.4.). App info remains.

The screen opens on a **fixed-height cover header** (`DESIGN.md § 7.16.`) rather than the old padded avatar block — the § 12.1. background is drawn behind the avatar and the name, and tapping the avatar opens § 12.3.

**The rows run 나 → 대화 → 앱 → 계정**, with § 12.4.'s 서버 관리 last of the 계정 group, and that order is a decision rather than the sequence the features landed in: 프로필, then 이모티콘 and 채팅방 배경, then 화면 테마, then 알림, 전송음 and 입력 중 표시, then 로그인된 기기 and 로그아웃. What a user opens this screen for sits at the top and what they touch once sits at the bottom; the two switches end up adjacent and the account rows end up beside the exit, so the shapes group as the topics do. 입력 중 표시 must stay directly under 알림 — it is the per-account counterpart to a per-device row, and the code says so by pointing at "the row above."

**Every row on this screen is per-account or per-device except one.** 채팅방 배경 (§ 12.2.) writes what the _other_ participant sees, and it is the only control here that does — which is precisely why its copy has to say so out loud rather than rely on its position in the 대화 group. A reader who has learnt the rest of the screen has learnt the opposite rule.

**Profile** — `PATCH /api/users/me`, reached from a 프로필 row or § 12.3.'s 프로필 편집; `features/update-profile` owns the sheet and reuses § 9.'s picker, editor and upload through `@x/update-profile`.

- The nickname set here is exactly what appears as the sender name in chat and inside system sentences (§ 8.7.), initialized from the Google account name at first login. `MAX_NICKNAME_LENGTH` bounds it, and the body is trimmed **before** the length check — twenty spaces would otherwise pass and § 8.7. would fall back to the email local part for a name the user believes they set
- Saving needs no explicit broadcast — the UPDATE lands on `users`, so the § 6. trigger fires `user_changed`. The Settings screen itself is a Server Component, so it takes the new row from a `router.refresh()`
- **An avatar is a `media` row on the § 9. pipeline, under the `avatar/` key scope.** Such a row reaches neither the library (no `message_media` child) nor the § 10. delete path. The patch carries the **upload** rather than an id, and each photo key reads three ways: absent leaves the current one, `null` is 기본 이미지로 되돌리기, an object registers and is worn in the same transaction as the write
- **The key prefix carries the scope, and each pipeline's objects stay inside it** (§ 9.). Ownership alone fails in both directions: an `avatar/` object attached to a message acquires a `message_media` child under the row the next profile change deletes, which fails on that non-cascading key (§ 6.) and leaves its owner permanently unable to change their photo; a `chat/` object worn as an avatar puts that same delete in front of a photo a bubble still renders. `POST /api/messages` validates under `chat`, `PATCH /api/users/me` under `avatar`
- **The crop is not optional and it is square.** The editor opens on the pick with `fixedAspectRatio={1}` and the ratio chips gone; backing out of it is backing out of the pick. A circle (`DESIGN.md § 7.7.`) crops anything else anyway, and squareness is what lets the full-screen view reserve its box without a round trip. **§ 12.1.'s 사진 사용하기 takes the same rule**, from the same editor: the avatar row is 1:1 with the chips hidden and both background rows are free-form, so a photo already in the conversation is framed by exactly the rule a picked one is
- **`canReadMedia` admits an object somebody is wearing.** Without it the other participant 404s on the avatar that names every bubble in front of them. The check is **last**, after the message join, so it costs nothing on the common path — and an avatar the owner has since replaced falls through it, which is what makes the swap real
- **The write reads the photo it replaces in the same statement**, through a `users` self-join whose alias Postgres resolves against the pre-update snapshot. A separate `SELECT` first cannot promise it: two devices saving different photos would both read the same row, and the loser would be reported by neither — orphaning its objects permanently, since `canReadMedia` admits only a **currently worn** avatar
- **The patch body must carry at least one key.** Both are optional, so `{}` parses, and the empty `.set()` behind it makes drizzle throw `No values to set` — a 500 for what is a malformed request
- **A replaced photo's row is soft-deleted and its bytes reclaimed after `MEDIA_DELETE_GRACE`.** Deleting the object in the same request would break a read already in flight; the grace covers that and deliberately no longer covers `MEDIA_CACHE_MAX_AGE` (§ 9.), which a day-long redirect cache put out of reach. What makes that safe on this path is that a replacement is a new row at a new key, so `user_changed` moves the peer onto a different URL rather than leaving them re-asking for the old one. **The delay used to be a `deleteObjectsAfterCacheWindow` `setTimeout` and is now a stamp** (§ 9.): a serverless instance is not there to fire a timer, so every objects-deleted-later promise this app made was lost to the first restart after it was scheduled. `MEDIA_DELETE_GRACE` is jandh's statement of that window; the value the reclaim actually applies is jandh-ops' `PURGE_GRACE`, and the two are the same five minutes. `discardScopedMedia` is narrowed to the caller's own prefix **for the scope it is given**, not merely their own rows: a crafted `PATCH` can aim any of the three media columns at any object its sender owns, and a chat photo reached that way would be deleted out from under the bubble carrying it. It runs in `after`, never inline: the profile write has already committed and `user_changed` has already fired, so a transient failure in cleanup answering 500 would leave the sheet open on `프로필을 저장하지 못했어요` for a save that landed — and the retry uploads a second avatar
- **A `background/` discard is filtered against `isMediaWorn` before it runs.** § 12.1. and § 12.2. share one scope, so one photo may sit in both — and changing only the cover would otherwise delete the object the wallpaper is still drawn from. The two slots now live in different tables, so this cannot be the row-local comparison it once was: the route asks after the write commits, which is also the only reading that is actually current

**로그인된 기기** — `listUserSessions` renders the live `sessions` rows and `DELETE /api/sessions/{id}` revokes one, scoped to the caller's own `user_id` and away from their own session (§ 5.2.). The current device is marked rather than revocable; signing it out is the 로그아웃 row, which also clears the cookie.

- **A 로그아웃 here retires that device's § 16.1. push subscription too**, through `push_subscriptions.session_id` and its `ON DELETE cascade`. There is no second statement and there is no endpoint in the request — the two tables describe the same device only because the launch upsert stamps the session it registered under. The same cascade closes a gap the shell's own 로그아웃 had: a signed-out browser went on holding a subscription that went on being pushed to
- **The column is nullable and must stay so.** A row written before it existed points at nothing, and § 5.2.'s expiry sweep deletes a session out from under an installation that is still opened daily. Both heal on the next launch, and until then the § 16.1. disuse prune is what covers them — which is why that prune cannot be replaced by this cascade
- **What is revoked is the server's row, not the browser's `PushSubscription`.** Nothing can reach into a device that is not running the app; the endpoint simply stops being addressed, and the next login re-registers the same one through `PushSync`. 알림 소리 returns to its default with it, exactly as it does when the toggle is turned off and on (§ 16.1.)

**Avatar enlargement** — tapping an avatar opens the photo full screen in the § 7.10. viewer. A single square cell with **no save control**: a profile photo is the person, not an attachment they shared. `Avatar` owns both halves behind `mediaId` and `canEnlarge`, and lives in `shared/ui` (§ 2.).

- **`canEnlarge` is off by default and stays off in the § 11.3. day agenda** — that avatar sits inside the row button that opens the event (§ 11.4.), where a nested `button` is invalid markup and swallows the tap the row exists for
- **The button takes over sizing when it wraps the circle**, which keeps `className` meaning one thing either way. Left on the circle, a caller's `size-*` would size the wrapper while the circle kept its own — and the agenda's event row passes exactly that
- **The enlargement moved one level in when § 12.3. landed.** An avatar in chat or on the Settings cover now opens the **profile screen**, and it is that screen's own avatar that enlarges. `Avatar` carries both affordances and `onClick` wins over `canEnlarge`, because one avatar can only mean one thing per screen

### 12.1. Profile Background ✅

`users.profile_background_media_id` — the cover behind the § 12.3. profile screen and the Settings header. **Published**: it is projected by `toParticipant` and the other participant sees it.

- **A column on `users`, not a table.** § 8.12.'s argument, unchanged: one nullable reference per user is read for free by the Server Component that already selects the row. `ON DELETE SET NULL`, like the avatar beside it
- **Its objects live in their own `background/` scope**, which is added to `MEDIA_UPLOAD_SCOPES` and to `StorageScope`. The key prefix is what enforces it at the `PATCH`, exactly as `avatar` is enforced — the two arguments are the same one (§ 12.)
- **`canReadMedia` admits an object worn as a profile cover**, beside the avatar clause and for the same reason: without it the other participant 404s on the photo the profile screen is drawn from. **The chat wallpaper is in that clause too now** (§ 12.2.) — it was the one exception while it was private, and sharing it removed the exception rather than adding a special case
- **사진 사용하기 is three targets, not one, and every one of them crops.** The viewer's sheet offers 프로필 이미지로 / 프로필 배경으로 / 채팅방 배경으로, in that order, over a photo already in the library or in a chat bubble. Three rows rather than one because the slots do not mean the same thing — a profile image is who the person is, a cover decorates their profile, a wallpaper is the room they are both in — so a single row would choose on the user's behalf and be a surprise two thirds of the time. It was 배경으로 설정, and `features/set-background` is now `features/apply-photo` (`useApplyPhoto`, `ApplyPhotoSheet`, the viewer's `onApplyPhoto`), with three `@x` gates renamed along with it
- **It no longer copies server-side. It downloads the original, crops it in the browser, and uploads a new object.** `POST /api/media/copy`, `copyMediaIntoScope` and `MEDIA_COPY_PATH` are **deleted** — the crop left them with no caller. What that buys is § 9.'s invariant: the copy route's `unregistered` status was one of the orphan producers this app set out to remove, since it had put two objects in the bucket before anything could name them, and the upload that replaces it is reserved like every other. The pixels come from `variant=edit` (§ 9.), which is why they can be read into a canvas at all. What the copy was **for** is unchanged and still forbids pointing the column at the source row: § 12.'s replacement cleanup would stand in front of a `chat/` object a bubble still renders, and § 10.'s library delete would strip the background through `ON DELETE SET NULL`. Each target then writes through the endpoint it already had — `PATCH /api/users/me` for the two columns, `PATCH /api/chat/background` for the room's row — so there is no route of this feature's own left to fix a scope on
- **Over a video the sheet has one row.** The cover takes a clip and the wallpaper does not, so the sheet **drops** the other two rather than disabling them (`DESIGN.md § 7.10.2.`'s rule for a control that is never available there) and its description says why the remaining row is alone — 동영상은 프로필 배경에만 쓸 수 있어요, in place of the § 12.2. sharing note (채팅방 배경은 상대방 화면에도 같이 깔려요) that row was written for. The control itself is withheld only where **neither** background could take the source: a draft, which has no stored object to read pixels back from, and a video past the caps below. `isWearableBackgroundVideo` is that rule and it lives in `shared/config`, so the viewer's gate and the cropper's own re-check cannot drift
- **A background video is croppable, and a spatial crop is not a trim.** `cropVideo` runs `mediabunny`'s `Conversion` in the browser, and where § 12.1.'s trimmer is fast precisely because it copies encoded samples across whenever the codec fits, cutting a rectangle rewrites every frame — a full decode and re-encode, slower and lossy where the trim is not. The user is told rather than left waiting (`다시 인코딩하고 있어요` in the 영역 자르기 bar). It stays affordable only because the caps below bound the source at 30s and 20MB **before** the control is drawn, and `MAX_BACKGROUND_VIDEO_SIZE` is re-checked on the **result**: a re-encode can come out larger than what it was cut from. **The rectangle is applied in a `video.process` hook with a five-argument `drawImage`, never through `mediabunny`'s `crop` option**: for a decoder-produced `VideoFrame`, WebKit ignores the source rectangle of the nine-argument `drawImage`, of `createImageBitmap` and of `visibleRect` alike, so the library's crop shipped the whole frame squashed into the box on Safari and every iOS browser. Offsetting the full frame has no source rectangle to ignore; rotation is already baked in by then, since `Conversion` rerenders before `process`
- **The stencil is drawn over the draft's poster still, not over the element.** `react-advanced-cropper` cannot measure a `<video>`, and the poster is the same picture at the same ratio — so the rectangle is chosen on a still and scaled back into the clip's own pixels. Every field of it is rounded to an **even** number, because the 4:2:0 chroma planes every codec here writes are half-resolution and an odd offset shifts them by half a pixel where it is not rejected outright
- **사진 사용하기 is one hook, not two copies.** The chat room and the library both mount the § 7.10. viewer and neither may import the other (§ 2.), so `useApplyPhoto` returns `{ open, sheet }` as a pair. The sheet is handed back **separately** because it has to be mounted outside the viewer's own conditional — inside it, dismissing the viewer unmounts the flow mid-write and the upload is left with no column pointing at it
- **The sheet cannot gate a second tap with state.** `ActionSheet` fires `onSelect` and closes in the same tick, so by the first `await` the sheet is already closed and any `isBusy` it holds gates nothing — while the viewer underneath is still up, still offering the control. The guard is a **ref**, and without it a slow connection turns an impatient second tap into a second uploaded object only the last write is reachable through. **The refusal is said out loud** (`앞의 사진을 설정하고 있어요`), because a swallowed second tap is a sheet dismissing exactly as it does on success. The wait is reported by `toast.promise`, which is the only surface left on screen
- Edited in the **프로필 편집** sheet beside the avatar, with a free-form crop: the cover is drawn `object-cover` against a box the sheet does not know, so a fixed ratio would crop it twice. The sheet's preview box is a letterbox and promises no framing — the cover is worn at two different geometries (`DESIGN.md § 7.16.`) and no single preview can stand for both
  **A profile cover may be a video** — the chat wallpaper may not (§ 12.2.), and that asymmetry is the whole of the decision. A profile is opened and closed, so the decode is bounded; the wallpaper sits behind § 8.3.'s virtualized list for as long as the tab is open — and now on the other participant's device as well as this one, which is a battery cost one person would be choosing for both.

- **Capped at `MAX_BACKGROUND_VIDEO_DURATION` (30s) and `MAX_BACKGROUND_VIDEO_SIZE` (20MB)**, and the size cap has nothing to do with `MAX_VIDEO_SIZE`. That one bounds an upload's reliability; this one bounds a **download** — a cover is fetched in full every time either participant opens that profile. `maxSizeForScope` is what applies it at registration, where § 14. actually holds
- **The length cap is enforced by trimming, never by refusal.** A longer pick opens `VideoTrimmer` rather than being rejected, so the cap never costs a re-pick
- **Every video pick opens the trimmer, cap or no cap.** Inside the window there is nothing to enforce, so it opens free-range and a clip needing no cut is finished by tapping 완료 — the cap is handed over only when the clip actually exceeds it, or when the container reports no duration at all, which is the one case that would otherwise walk past the ceiling
- **Trimming is `mediabunny`, in the browser** (the first dependency added since § 1. was written). It copies the encoded samples across whenever the source codec fits the output container **and the cut starts at 0** — `Conversion` transcodes the whole track the moment `trim.start` passes the first packet, so only an end-only cut of an iPhone clip is a re-mux; a start cut is a full WebCodecs re-encode, the same path `cropVideo` takes. That path hit mediabunny 1.52.3's Safari `isConfigSupported` throw (fixed in 1.53.0), which is why the dependency is pinned at or above it. Input formats are named **one by one** (`MP4`, `QTFF`, `WEBM` — exactly `ALLOWED_VIDEO_MIMES`) rather than `ALL_FORMATS`, because the library tree-shakes to whatever that list names
- **The audio track is discarded, and that is a compatibility decision as much as a size one.** A background plays `muted`, so the track could never be heard — and dropping it means the whole operation needs only WebCodecs' **video** interfaces, which Safari has had since **16.4**. The audio interfaces landed in Safari **26**, so keeping a track nobody can hear would have raised the app's floor by ten major versions
- **A `<video>` must mount with its source, never receive one afterwards.** A browser judges autoplay once, when the element loads its source, and it is not re-judged when `src` changes — so an element rendered while its object URL is still being minted in an effect sits on frame 0 forever, which is the black frame it starts on. Every preview here holds its player back until the URL exists. `PreloadVideo` also starts playback by hand at `loadeddata`, because React assigns `muted` as a property rather than an attribute and the element can be unmuted at the moment autoplay is judged
- **A `<video>` with no decoded frame paints black, not nothing**, so every one the app draws _as content_ goes through `PreloadVideo` — `PreloadImage`'s contract for the element that cannot use it. **The § 7.10. viewer is the one deliberate exception**: it renders a bare `<video controls>`, because there the element is a player rather than a surface — it keeps the platform's own controls, and an unplayable container already has its 이 기기에서는 재생할 수 없는 형식이에요 fallback with 원본 저장 beside it. Four rules the wrapper carries, each of them a bug it shipped without:
  - **A `poster` suppresses the skeleton rather than sitting under it.** The element paints a poster as soon as _that_ image loads, independently of the video data, so an opaque skeleton on top hides the very frame this feature keeps a poster for
  - **A poster is also why this wrapper takes no `blurhash` where `PreloadImage` does.** The shell carries the prop (`PreloadFrame`), so the exception is this element's alone: a postered `<video>` is revealed on its **first** render, which mounts the blur already at `opacity-0` and paints it never. Every stored video is postered — the § 9. upload writes a `_thumb` for exactly that — so wiring a hash here means first changing what a poster reveals, which is the thing this bullet fixed the reveal for
  - **`hasSkeleton` is off for a full-bleed surface**, exactly as `PreloadImage` requires. `Skeleton` is an opaque `surface-strong`, so over a cover it is a pulsing light plate the size of the screen. **A hash overrides it in the other direction**: where one exists it replaces the skeleton outright and `hasSkeleton` stops deciding anything (`DESIGN.md § 7.8.`)
  - **`loadeddata` reveals a poster-less element; `loadedmetadata` only starts a bounded deadline.** `HAVE_METADATA` is _zero decoded frames_, so revealing there reveals the black this wrapper exists to prevent — but waiting for `loadeddata` outright is a hang, because an engine honouring `preload="metadata"` stops at `HAVE_METADATA` and sends nothing further, and neither does WebKit once it has refused autoplay (iOS then falls back to `preload`, so **`autoPlay` does not guarantee `loadeddata` either** and cannot be the discriminator). `FIRST_FRAME_GRACE` is the ceiling: prefer a real frame, never wait past it. With a poster none of this applies — the element is already revealed and there is something painted the whole time
  - **WebKit's own start-playback button is suppressed** (`no-start-playback-button`). Where autoplay is refused, WebKit paints a large play glyph over the element — a control the app never rendered, which hides the poster a refusal is supposed to leave, and which on the Settings cover is not even pressable, since the § 7.16. gradient spans the same band. The rule needs `!important`: it is a UA shadow pseudo-element and WebKit's own declaration otherwise wins on iOS
  - **The caller reserves the frame's box, and a preview over a dark surface has to.** `PreloadFrame` is sized by whatever is inside it, and a `<video>` with no frame has no intrinsic size — so a caller passing only `max-h-full max-w-full` renders the skeleton and the failure glyph at 0×0. On `VideoTrimmer`'s `bg-scrim` overlay that was the whole bug: a clip that never decoded left a black screen with no preview, no error and 완료 disabled forever, reachable from 프로필 편집 on every video pick. The trimmer reserves the box from the draft's own dimensions, falling back to 16 / 9 for a container `toVideoDraft` could not measure
  - **An unplayable container is named, not merely glyphed.** The trimmer answers `onError` with the § 7.10. viewer's sentence — 이 기기에서는 재생할 수 없는 형식이에요 — because the glyph says a load ended and only the sentence says the pick is unusable and 취소 is the way out
  - **The failure glyph's `bg-surface-strong` sits on the same element as `placeholderClassName`**, never on a child of it, or a full-bleed caller's `bg-scrim` stops overriding it and a failed cover becomes the half-viewport light plate `hasSkeleton={false}` was added to prevent — with the `text-on-primary` name on top of it
- **The two wrappers are one shell, not two copies.** Each rule above was a prop the copy dropped, so they now share `useLoadStatus` (status, the reset when `src` changes, the § 13.3. retry) and `PreloadFrame` (the reserved box, the skeleton, the failure glyph) in `preload-media.tsx`. Each file keeps only what its element does — the `<img>`'s cached-status read-back and retry URL, the `<video>`'s reveal events and hand-started playback. **A prop added to one is added to the shell**, not copied across
- **The poster is the `_thumb` sibling § 9. already stores, and it is load-bearing rather than decorative.** iOS refuses muted autoplay outright in Low Power Mode, and nothing in the page can detect that state — without the poster the cover would be a black rectangle for those users instead of a still frame
- **`Participant` carries `isProfileBackgroundVideo` beside the id.** A `media` id says nothing about its kind, and the renderer has to choose between `<img>` and `<video>` before it fetches anything. `listUsers` resolves it with a **`leftJoin`** — an inner one would drop every user who has set no cover, which is most of them
- **The kind is checked server-side on every write path**, not only where the control is drawn. `PATCH /api/chat/background` refuses a video on its own, because the wallpaper is writable with any `background/` object its setter holds — including one uploaded a moment earlier for the profile. The slot check that used to sit on `POST /api/media/copy` went with that route; what replaced it is that the bytes now pass through a client again, so registration's size check applies to the **cropped result** rather than to a source it was told about

- **Each slot has its own file input, and the pick names the slot it came from.** Both controls open the OS picker outright (`DESIGN.md § 7.5.`), so an `accept` computed from a slot held in state would still be the previous slot's by the time the dialog is up — the cover takes a video and the avatar does not, and one input cannot be both on the tap that opens it. The slot state that remains is written **by the pick**, for the crop that follows it, never by the tap
  - **This is the shape of an old bug, and the reason the two must not share one flag.** The picker used to be a `MediaPickerSheet`, an `ActionSheet` that fires `onSelect` and closes itself in the same tick while the OS dialog opens asynchronously — a slot stored in that open flag was already `null` when the file arrived, so **every pick landed in whichever slot the fallthrough named**. Shipped that way, choosing a new profile photo silently replaced the cover instead, uncropped

### 12.2. Chat Background ✅

`chat_settings.background_media_id` — the wallpaper behind the conversation. **Shared**: one wallpaper, drawn on both participants' screens, settable and clearable by either of them.

- **A one-row table, and it is the one thing on this app that is neither per-user nor per-device.** § 12.1.'s argument for a column on `users` is exactly what fails here: a shared value has no user's row to hang off. The row is pinned by `id boolean PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT true` plus `CHECK (id)`, so `true` is the only key it can take and there is no second row to disagree with the first
- **It is not the `conversations` table § 6. refuses**, and the distinction is the whole reason it is affordable. That section's objection is to a conversation _identity_: an id, a membership join, an FK on every row, an `ensureConversation` on the login path. This carries none of them — nothing references it, and a second conversation would still need every one of those things built from scratch
- **`canReadMedia` admits an object worn as the wallpaper**, through the same `isMediaWorn` clause the avatar and the profile cover go through. This is the reversal of the old design: the wallpaper used to be readable by its owner alone, and now the object one participant uploaded is drawn on the other's screen, so without the clause they 404 on the photo behind every bubble in front of them
- **The object stays owned by whoever set it.** The prefix check still holds at the write, so a wallpaper always lands under its setter's own `background/{userId}/` prefix — sharing changes who may _see_ the object, never who may point the row at one
- **It is reached from 사진 사용하기 as well as from the 설정 row, and either way it is cropped** (§ 12.1.). The viewer's 채팅방 배경으로 sets the room's wallpaper from the other participant's photo, so the object it uploads is this user's own even though the picture was not — which is exactly what the bullet above says, and is what makes the discard below reach the right prefix. **The crop is free-form here for § 12.'s reason**: the wallpaper is drawn `object-cover` behind a room whose geometry no preview knows
- **The replaced object is discarded under the previous setter's id, never the caller's.** `discardScopedMedia` narrows to `background/{ownerId}/`, so handing it the person doing the replacing silently reclaims nothing every time the two take turns, and the bucket grows by a full-resolution photo per swap. `writeChatBackground` reads the owner off the `media` row rather than storing it, since the object's own `owner_id` is the ground truth the prefix is built from
- **Every `background/` discard goes through `discardUnwornScopedMedia`, whose guard is inside the DELETE.** § 12.1.'s cover and this wallpaper share the `background/` scope but now live in different tables, so the row-local `isStillWorn` comparison that used to guard them cannot see across. **Asking first and deleting second is not a fix**: two cleanups running at once each see the object still held by the _other_ table and both skip, leaking it — and in the mirror case a slot can take the object between the question and the delete. A `NOT EXISTS` in the DELETE's own qual is evaluated by the statement that does the deleting, which is the only moment the answer is still true when it is used. `avatar/` keeps the plain `discardScopedMedia`: nothing else accepts that scope, so it has no such hazard. **That doctrine is now cited in the other direction too**: § 9.'s registration consumes a reservation with the freshness test inside the `DELETE`, and refuses on what survives it, rather than asking whether the claim is live and then taking it
- **A Settings row of its own, not a slot in 프로필 편집.** That sheet is named for one person's profile, and this belongs to the conversation
- **The sharing is stated in the UI, in three places, because nothing else in 설정 behaves this way.** The row's description names 상대방 화면 in both its set and unset states (상대방 화면에도 이 배경이 깔려요 / 바꾸면 상대방 화면에도 같이 깔려요); the action sheet that opens over it says 상대방 화면의 배경도 같이 바뀌어요 immediately before the change lands; and the success toast says 상대방 화면에도 이 배경이 깔렸어요 rather than naming only this user's screen. The § 12.1. 사진 사용하기 sheet carries the same note, since it is reached from a photo and never passes through 설정 at all — **except over a video, where that sheet has no wallpaper row to explain** (§ 12.1.) and the sentence says which background a video can reach instead. **Left unsaid, this is only ever discovered from the other side** — the 입력 중 switch above the row withholds a signal and the theme below it is per device, so a reader has no reason to expect a third behaviour
- **Every one of those strings uses 깔리다, and none of them says 둘 다.** 깔리다 is the verb the row has always used for a wallpaper and the one Korean actually reaches for when a picture sits behind something; the first revision of this copy said 둘 다 이 사진을 대화 뒤에서 보고 있어요, which is a literal translation of the requirement rather than a sentence anybody would write. Naming 상대방 화면 also states the shared half more concretely than a count of people does
- **`PATCH /api/chat/background`, not a key on `PATCH /api/users/me`.** "me" stopped being true: either participant may write it, and the object being replaced generally belongs to the other one. Its body is one required `media` key — an upload to register and wear, or `null` for 기본 배경으로. The route keeps § 12.1.'s image-only check, because that column is still writable with any `background/` object its owner holds — including one copied a moment earlier for the profile
- **The change reaches the other participant over `user_changed`**, the channel § 12. already rides. `chat_settings` has an `AFTER UPDATE` trigger firing `notify_user_changed()`, and `GET /api/users` answers with the wallpaper id beside the participant set — so the client's existing refetch carries it and no second channel, listener or request is added. **The resume catch-up covers the backgrounded gap for free**, since § 8.4. already calls that refetch on every return
- **Both the room and the 설정 row read the id from the stream provider, not from a server render.** A prop would only move on a navigation, which is precisely the case a shared wallpaper has to survive — the other participant changing it while this one is looking at either screen. **The Settings row is the sharper of the two**: that change also _deletes_ the object the row draws a thumbnail of, so a stale prop there is a broken image over a description claiming the photo is up, offering 기본 배경으로 for one that is already gone. **A writer pushes the server's answer into that state by hand, and must.** § 8.4.2. mounts `ChatStreamConnection` in 채팅 alone, so 설정 and 보관함 — the two screens 채팅방 배경 and 사진 사용하기 are actually reached from — hold **no socket**, and the write's own `user_changed` arrives nowhere. A `router.refresh()` is no substitute either: the provider is seeded by the `(main)` shell, which a tab change does not re-render. Shipped without the hand-off, the row's thumbnail went on showing the photo it had just replaced. The server render still reads it once, to seed the provider from the `(main)` shell and to emit the preload below
- **The write takes `FOR UPDATE` in a transaction, and deliberately does _not_ copy § 12.'s `UPDATE … FROM previous` self-join.** That pattern does not survive two writers, and this is the app's only row that has two. Under READ COMMITTED a blocked UPDATE re-evaluates its qual against the **target** row's new version, while the aliased scan beside it keeps the statement's original snapshot — so both participants saving at once report the _same_ previous id, one object is discarded twice and the object the losing write actually detached is reported by nothing and orphaned in R2 forever. `users` gets away with the self-join because its only concurrent writers are one person's own devices
- **The write re-creates the row if it is missing rather than answering 404.** `0025` seeds it and the CHECK leaves `true` as the only key it can take, so the sole ways to lose it are a restore or a hand-run `DELETE` — and an `UPDATE`-only write would then fail every wallpaper save forever, behind a Settings screen showing 기본 배경 with nothing to explain it. The INSERT fires the notify trigger too, since that write has to reach the other participant like any other
- **The wash over the photo is a fixed token (`chat-scrim`), not a slider.** Every meta colour in the room — `chat-meta`, the date pill, `수정됨` — was chosen for contrast against `chat-canvas` (`DESIGN.md § 4.1.`), and a photo underneath them is an arbitrary colour. A user-chosen opacity has a setting at which the timestamps are unreadable. **Shared makes this stricter rather than looser**: a slider would now be one person choosing how legible the other person's timestamps are
- **The backdrop is the room's first child and every sibling after it is absolutely positioned too**, which is what orders them: both are in the positioned-descendant paint order, so DOM order decides and no `z-index` is involved. One here would have to outrank the composer
- **It is `pointer-events-none` throughout** — it sits under the § 8.3. scroller and under the § 8.11. hold on every bubble, and a photo in the wallpaper must not compete with the photos in the conversation
- **The asset is preloaded from the chat screen's server render** (`react-dom`'s `preload`, `as: "image"`, `fetchPriority: "high"`), not requested by the `<img>` on mount. It covers the whole screen behind the first bubble the reader sees, so a request that waits for hydration paints the flat `chat-canvas` first and swaps the photo in underneath a conversation already being read. It asks for `variant=original`: § 9.'s 720px thumbnail is visibly soft at full-bleed
- **Only the wallpaper is preloaded.** The profile cover (§ 12.1.) is behind a tap that may never happen, and `/api/media/{id}`'s 302 is cached for `MEDIA_CACHE_MAX_AGE` anyway — a preload starts a request early within one page load, it does not keep anything warm across sessions
- **iOS 26 tints the chat route's chrome from the wallpaper, and the colour comes off the § 9. blurhash rather than off the photo.** `DESIGN.md § 3.3.` gives the mechanism and § 4.3. the geometry: `ChatScreen` is `fixed` and deliberately flush against both obscured content insets, so it covers `body` entirely and is the element Safari samples its status bar and toolbar from. `toChromeTint` reads the hash's **DC term** — characters 2–5, base83, stored already sRGB-encoded — which _is_ the image's average colour, so the tint is string arithmetic on a value the shell was already seeded with and it lands on the room's first paint. It is decoded by hand because the package's own `decode` renders pixels: a 1×1 render is the top-left corner, where every basis function evaluates to `1` and the AC terms are therefore summed into the answer, and there is no DC accessor to call instead. A hash the base83 pass rejects yields nothing and leaves the chrome on `chat-canvas`
- **That replaced a canvas read of the photo, which failed three separate ways.** The old `useBackdropTint` loaded `variant=original` with `crossOrigin="anonymous"` and averaged a 16×16 draw of it. It cost a **second full-size download**, because the browser caches a CORS request separately from the plain one the preload above makes — which is why the displayed `<img>` had to carry the attribute too. It published nothing until that full-size photo had decoded, so the chrome visibly changed colour under a reader already in the room. And `getImageData` is subject to a CORS check that `/api/media/{id}`'s 302 into R2 answers differently cold and warm, so it failed on a refresh and worked after a route change. **`crossOrigin` is gone from `ChatBackdrop` and must not come back**: in CORS mode a bucket whose rules do not name the origin (§ 15.) turns a lost tint into a lost _wallpaper_, since the element errors outright and `PreloadImage` falls to its failure state
- **The tint is published as a `color-mix` against the live token, never as three resolved numbers.** What borders the chrome is the photo _under_ the § 12.2. wash rather than the photo, so the value is `color-mix(in srgb, var(--color-chat-scrim) 45%, {average})` and that 45% is the same one `ChatBackdrop` draws — **the two move together or the status bar sits a shade off the room it is meant to disappear into** (`DESIGN.md § 7.16.`). Expressing the composite in CSS is what lets it re-resolve when the theme moves: `--color-chat-scrim` is a token the dark theme changes, and a value composited in script is baked against whichever theme was up when the wallpaper landed, with no signal to recompute on. It is applied as the box's own inline `background-color` over a `bg-chat-canvas` class that stays as the no-wallpaper answer, which is why `ContainerProps` carries a `style` — the tint is the one value on that box no class can express, since it is a photograph's
- **The hash rides beside the id everywhere the id already travels.** `readChatBackground` answers both from one query — an `innerJoin` on `media`, so a cleared wallpaper and a missing row give the same `null` — and `GET /api/users` returns both, so the `user_changed` refetch above carries the colour with the photo and no second request, channel or listener is added. The `(main)` shell seeds the provider with both, which is what puts the tint on the chat route's first paint rather than a request later. **A writer declares an id and therefore clears the hash**: 사진 사용하기 knows the id the server answered and nothing else, and a hash kept across that write is the _previous_ wallpaper's colour on the chrome of a room the new one is already behind. Cleared, the box falls back to `chat-canvas` until the write's own `user_changed` — or § 8.4.'s resume catch-up — brings the payload the real hash belongs to. **The bars themselves do not follow any of that**, and cannot: `DESIGN.md § 3.3.` is that iOS 26 samples an element once at its first paint, so a wallpaper set, replaced or cleared mid-session moves this box's `background-color` while the status bar and toolbar keep the colour they were sampled with until the next document parse. That is the price of the whole tint and not a defect of the write path — nothing in the room can force a re-sample, which is why the value has to be right in the **server's HTML** rather than merely right eventually
- **`toChromeTint` is called from `ChatScreen`, not from the backdrop that draws the photo.** The tint is that box's own background, so it belongs to that component and cannot outlive it. **`ChatRoom` therefore has no floor of its own, and must never get one back.** It carried an opaque `bg-chat-canvas` on the belief that Safari reads the `fixed` box's computed `background-color` rather than the pixels over it. It reads the pixels: with the tint in place and the wallpaper on screen, the status bar measured `#efeae2` — `chat-canvas` exactly, neither `body`'s `#fbf9f6` nor the tint — which can only have come off that floor. The class was invisible to a reader (`ChatScreen` is the same colour underneath), so nothing between the top edge and that box may be opaque
- **`useBlurhashStyle` publishes the average as an ungated `background-color`, and only the `background-image` waits for hydration.** With the room's floor gone the backdrop's placeholder was the next flat plate at that edge, painting `placeholderClassName` until a canvas existed to decode the blur on. Splitting the two costs nothing and needs no `suppressHydrationWarning`: the DC term is base83 on four characters, so the server and the client's first pass produce the same string. The placeholder takes the **bare average**, never `toChromeTint`'s composite — the § 12.2. wash is a sibling painted over it, so the tint there is washed twice and the bars land pale. `placeholderClassName` stays as the failure floor, which the inline colour outranks wherever a hash parses
- **It is a plain function called in the render, and it was a layout effect that published a root custom property.** `DESIGN.md § 3.3.` gives the mechanism in full: iOS 26 samples the chrome at an element's **first paint** and re-samples for no later change to its `background-color`. An effect runs after the server's HTML has painted, so the effect version was correct on a client route change into 채팅 — where the box first paints already tinted — and wrong on **every cold launch and PWA relaunch**, which is the path that matters, since iOS kills a backgrounded standalone app and relaunching it is a fresh document. Moving the value into the render puts it in the SSR HTML, which is the only paint Safari reads — **and that is necessary rather than sufficient**: it was shipped alone and changed nothing on the device, because the room's opaque floor above was what the sampler had been reading all along (the bullet below). Both halves are load-bearing; the colour has to be correct at the first paint _and_ has to be the topmost thing painted at that edge. **Caching the colour in `localStorage` and restoring it from a blocking inline script is not a second layer and must not be added**: every path that parses a new document already carries the hash server-side (the `(main)` shell seeds the provider, and `sw.js` caches `/_next/static/` and `/offline` alone — never app HTML), and a bfcache restore runs no inline script either, so the one case neither approach covers is a case the script does not cover on its own. What it would add is a stale colour for a wallpaper the other participant changed while the app was closed, plus a second copy of the base83 decode and of the § 7.16. 45%

### 12.3. Profile Screen ✅

Tapping an avatar opens a full-screen profile, KakaoTalk-style: the § 12.1. cover, the avatar, the name, and one action.

- **A `ShellOverlay`, not a route.** It has to cover the tab bar, which a screen under `(main)` cannot — and `absolute`, never `fixed` (`AGENTS.md § 4.4.`)
- **`ProfileViewerProvider` lives in the shell**, for `ChatStreamProvider`'s reason and one of its own: an avatar is rendered by `widgets/chat-room`, by the Settings cover and by the § 11.3. day agenda, and a widget may not import a sibling widget (§ 2.) — so an overlay mounted beside any one of them would need a copy per screen, with two able to be open at once
- **`Avatar` does not reach the provider and must not.** It is `shared/ui`, below every layer the provider reads from, so the tap is wired by whoever renders the avatar — and the § 11.3. day agenda deliberately wires none (§ 12.)
- **The person is resolved live against the participant set**, so a rename or a new cover reaches a profile that is already open (§ 8.7.). A `userId` the set does not hold closes the screen rather than drawing an empty one
- **The base is `scrim` and the text is `on-primary` whether or not a cover is set.** A colour that followed the photo would have to be derived from it, and the § 9. hash that makes that cheap for § 12.2.'s wallpaper is not in reach here — `Participant` carries the cover's id and `isProfileBackgroundVideo`, not its `media` row. The cover sits under a **two-stop** gradient — the top darkens the strip the close control is in, the bottom darkens the name, and the middle is left alone rather than dimming what the user came to look at
- **`Escape` is guarded on nothing being open above it.** The avatar's own § 7.10. viewer composes no `Dialog`, so it marks itself with `data-media-viewer` and this screen tests for that alongside `OPEN_DIALOG_SELECTOR`
- **`role="dialog"` and `aria-modal` are written by hand**, because nothing here composes a Radix primitive. The opaque fill hides the screen underneath from the eye and from the pointer, and without these it was still announced as live content with no boundary
- Own profile → **프로필 편집**, opening the one § 12. editor. The other participant's → **대화하기**

- **A focus trap**, shared with the § 7.10. viewer. This screen and the § 7.10. viewer are the app's two hand-rolled overlays and neither has one, so `Tab` walks into the composer behind them. It wants **one** owner shared by both rather than a second copy of the same logic — `useModalOverlay`, which owns `Escape`, the `Tab` cycle **and** the marker the next overlay down reads, because all three turn on the same question of whether anything is open above. Both screens already dismissed from their own copy of that predicate, so a trap beside them would have written it a third and fourth time. An overlay defers only to one that **follows it in document order** — never to "any other overlay". Both screens carry the marker, so the any-other rule made a stacked pair defer to each other, and the viewer an avatar opens inside the profile screen left neither answering `Escape` and the trap skipped in both. Document order is the stacking order here: `ShellOverlay` portals both into the same shell node at one `z-index`, and Radix and Vaul portal past it to the end of `body`. Focus lands on the **container**, not its first control, so a reader announces the dialog rather than opening on 닫기 — which is why **both** callers must carry `role="dialog"` and an `aria-label`, or focus lands on an anonymous `div` and nothing is announced. It is restored on close only if the opener is still connected — a 대화하기 navigates away and an avatar's message can be deleted under it. The cycle is filtered by **visibility**: the viewer keeps its controls mounted as `invisible` (they must not resize the track mid-swipe), the browser skips them on `Tab`, and a cycle ending on one would `focus()` something that cannot take it and strand focus on `body`. The hook binds through a **ref callback with a cleanup return**, not an effect reading `ref.current`: this screen renders `null` until the participant reaches it, and an effect that found no element on its one run would never bind again. `onClose` is held in a ref rather than being a dependency — every caller passes a fresh arrow, and re-binding on it would restore focus to the opener and seize it back on each parent render, which in the chat room is every message that arrives. Replacing `data-media-viewer` with the hook's own marker also closes a § 8.4.1. gap — `isBusy` matched `[role="dialog"][data-state="open"]`, which this hand-written screen never set, so an open profile did not hold the stream awake

**Other rows**

- Entry point to the emoticon management screen (§ 13.5.) — authoring, pack order, and per-pack hiding
- **알림** — the push toggle (§ 16.1.), **per device** rather than per account, so it reflects this browser's subscription rather than a stored preference. Three inert states carry their own copy: permission denied (only the browser's site settings can undo it), unsupported (iOS Safari tab — install to the home screen first), and in flight
- **입력 중 표시** — the § 8.12. switch, on by default. **Per account**, unlike the row above it: it governs what this user broadcasts, which is not a property of whichever browser they happen to be typing in. The toggle moves optimistically and is put back on a failed write, since a switch reading 꺼짐 over a preference the server never took would sit in front of a ping that is still going out
- **No camera-permission toggle.** Taking a photo goes through `<input type="file" capture>`, which hands the shot to iOS's own camera app — the web page never touches the camera, so no site permission is created

**Device management**

- List of logged-in devices, with per-session revocation. Reads `sessions` (`device_label`, `last_seen_at`, `created_at` are written and nothing renders them), **not** the § 16.1. push subscriptions — a different set, and not revocable. A § 12. row opens the screen; the caller's own session is marked rather than revocable. `DELETE /api/sessions/{id}` is scoped to the caller's own `user_id` **and** away from their own session id — the first because the id comes from the client and one participant could otherwise sign the other out everywhere, the second because revoking the running session leaves a cookie the proxy still waves through (§ 5.2.), which is what the 로그아웃 row exists for. The id is parsed as a UUID before it reaches the query, or `sessions.id`'s `uuid` column raises `22P02` and a malformed path segment surfaces as a 500 rather than a 400. **Expired rows are filtered from the list, never deleted**: § 5.2. clears a session only when its own cookie is presented, so a device that was simply never opened again leaves a row that would otherwise offer a 로그아웃 for a session already dead — and deleting it would make a read route write. The row shows an **absolute** date, because `last_seen_at` slides at most once a day (§ 5.2.) and "3분 전" would claim a precision the column does not carry. **Revocation reaches an already-open stream.** § 8.4.'s SSE connection authenticates once and is then held for as long as the tab is, so a revoked device would keep reading the conversation live until it happened to reconnect — which an idle one does within § 8.4.1.'s minute and an active one never does. The heartbeat re-asks through `isSessionLive`, which is deliberately **not** `cache()`d: `getSessionContext` is memoized per request, and one request is this whole connection, so it would answer with the session it opened with forever. A revoked stream aborts and the client falls into the same reopen-and-401 path an expired session already took
- Log out
- **화면 테마** — a three-way segmented control, 시스템 / 밝게 / 어둡게, defaulting to 시스템 (DESIGN.md § 5.1.). **`<meta name="theme-color">` follows the app's resolved theme, not the OS's.** The tags `app/layout.tsx` emits are keyed on `prefers-color-scheme`, which stops being the answer the moment this row can override it — a phone in light mode with 어둡게 chosen would frame a dark app in a `#FBF9F6` status bar. `ThemeColorSync` rewrites **every** tag rather than the matching one, since the browser honours the first whose `media` matches and a stale sibling reintroduces the band as soon as the OS setting moves. **Per device**, like 알림 and unlike 입력 중 표시: `next-themes` keeps the choice in `localStorage`, so it describes this browser rather than the account, and there is no column to write. The row itself is **not** a button — three segments are three targets, and a row that also took a tap would advance the theme from wherever the finger missed

### 12.4. Server Management ✅

**A 설정 row opens `/settings/server`, which drives jandh-ops** — the ops service that dumps Postgres into R2 and sweeps the bucket for objects no `media` row can name. That repository owns the work; this screen is the only way to ask for it by hand, and it is where the answer is read back.

- **The backup list is R2's own answer**, newest first, each row showing when the dump was taken and how large it is. `formatStorageSize` rather than § 9.'s `formatSize`, because a dump passes the MB ceiling that one was written for
- **The list and the per-backup deletion are read from R2 by _this_ app**, no longer proxied to jandh-ops. The dumps sit under `backups/` in the bucket the app's own objects live in, so `listBackups` and `deleteBackup` need no credential § 9. did not already hold — which is why that repository's sweep whitelists the four media prefixes instead of blacklisting this one. **This is the first half of retiring jandh-ops**, and it is the half that can go first because it needs no binary: the run does, and stays there
- **Reading them through the SDK deletes a class of bug rather than moving it.** jandh-ops scrapes `aws s3 ls` text, which forced a `TZ=UTC` pin (the CLI renders `LastModified` in the process's local zone and prints no marker), a re-comparison of the printed name (`ls` on a full key is a **prefix** match, so probing `X.dump` reports `X.dump.sql.gz` beside it) and a filter for the `PRE tmp/` line. `ListObjectsV2` answers `LastModified` as a `Date` and `Delimiter: "/"` puts the staging prefix in `CommonPrefixes`, so none of the three has an equivalent here
- **A listing that fails answers `upstream_failed`, never an empty array.** "The bucket is empty" and "the bucket could not be read" draw the same 아직 백업이 없어요 otherwise — and the second would also report every stored dump as already deleted
- **Deleting still lists first.** `DeleteObject` succeeds for a key that was never there, so the listing is the only thing that turns a stale row into a 404 rather than into a deletion that removed nothing. It is `DeleteObjectCommand` and not § 9.'s `deleteObjects`: that one never throws because it is cleanup behind somebody else's request, and this one **is** the request
- **백업 생성, per-backup 삭제, and the orphan sweep**, the last one as 미리보기 beside 정리 실행 — the sweep subtracts the database from the bucket, so a preview is the only thing that shows what a run would take before it takes it. Deleting is confirmed in a `Modal` either way
- **The sweep is an audit now, not a collection round** (§ 9.). Its keep set is `media` UNION `storage_reservations`, both on `r2_purged_at IS NULL`, which is every key either app can have written — so **zero is the normal result and a non-zero count is a bug report**, naming a writer that uploaded before it registered. `deleted_at` subtracts nothing from that set: a soft-deleted row is _waiting_ for the reclaim, and sweeping it here would race that pass into stamping rows whose bytes had already gone
- **The interval is this service's, but the job is no longer only its own** (§ 9.). jandh runs the same two passes off `POST /api/media/upload-url`, so retiring jandh-ops degrades cleanup to an upload-driven schedule rather than stopping it. `ORPHAN_MIN_AGE` is the one number the two must agree on: jandh drops a spent claim on **that** delay rather than on the grace, or the row leaves the keep set while this audit is still consulting it and the object is reported as an orphan
- **The sweep's push is unconditional; the SCHEDULED purge's is opt-in (`PURGE_NOTIFY`, default off), and a hand-started purge always notifies.** The schedule fires every ten minutes where the other two are daily, so a banner per run is a hundred-odd pushes a day — and a person notified that often stops reading the ones that matter, on the channel this app raises 메시지 on. A dispatch is the opposite case: somebody pressed 회수하기 and is waiting, so `ops-purge.yml` turns the push on for `workflow_dispatch`, **including the one that reclaimed nothing** — the schedule usually drained the queue minutes earlier, so "회수할 파일 없음" is the likeliest answer and withholding it would leave the button promising a banner that never came. Every ops banner is silent regardless of `push_subscriptions.sound_enabled` (`pushToUser`'s `silent` option) — that preference is about messages, where somebody is waiting, and a 05:00 backup is not
- **A dispatched run reports to whoever asked for it, not to `BACKUP_NOTIFY_EMAIL`.** The routes pass the session's own address as `notify-email` and `notifyOps` prefers it, because 서버 관리 is reachable by both allowlisted accounts (§ 5.1.) and its modals deliberately report the REQUEST — so a fixed recipient would answer on the other participant's phone and leave the presser with nothing at all. A scheduled run sends it empty and falls back
- **A hand-started sweep previews; the scheduled one deletes.** `workflow_dispatch` defaults `dry-run` to true, which is the 미리보기 the screen offers spelled as a workflow input — a sweep subtracts the database from a whole bucket listing, so a preview is the only thing that shows what a run would take before it takes it
- **`ORPHAN_MIN_AGE` drops from 24h to 1h**, because there is no legitimate gap left for it to cover — the row is always written first. It survives as a floor under a future writer that gets the order wrong, and an hour is short enough that the audit still reports the violation the same day. **Its old justification was already false when it was written**: it claimed the emoticon authoring form uploaded up front, where § 13.4. has always uploaded on final submit
- **All four scheduled runs are GitHub Actions in this repository, and jandh-ops no longer schedules anything.** `scripts/ops/{backup,purge,sweep,remind}.ts` are CLI entries over the code the app already has — the fourth is § 16.3.'s event reminders, which is the one job here that serves a product feature rather than the deployment — the reclaim is `reclaimExpiredStorageOnce`, the dump streams `pg_dump` into `@aws-sdk/lib-storage`, and the banners go through `pushToUser`. `.github/workflows/ops-job.yml` carries the one thing a runner cannot do without: the database is on the private network, so every job forwards `127.0.0.1:15432` to it over SSH through the web server. **15432 and not 5432** — the runner image ships a Postgres of its own, and `ExitOnForwardFailure` is what makes a refused forward fail at the tunnel rather than at whatever else answers
- **A dump is staged before it is promoted, and the size check has three answers rather than two.** `pg_dump` streams into `backups/tmp/`, and only an object R2 confirms as non-empty is copied to `backups/` — a stream that dies mid-dump otherwise leaves a truncated file where retention counts it and a restore trusts it. `readStoredSize` answers the stored size, `0` when R2 holds nothing there, and **`null` when R2 could not be asked**: the last is no verdict on the dump, so the staged object is left in place rather than deleted, since a throttle on a HEAD is not a reason to discard a dump that took minutes to stream. `backups/tmp/` is invisible to `listBackups` (`Delimiter`) and to the orphan sweep (which whitelists the four media prefixes), so **every successful run reclaims staged objects older than a week** — without that, a spell of transient failures bills for multi-gigabyte dumps forever with nothing on screen naming them
- **The schedules are 05:00 (backup), 04:30 (sweep) and every ten minutes (purge and § 16.3.'s reminders), Korean time, which cron reads as UTC.** **None of the four is punctual**: a scheduled workflow runs late under load, is occasionally skipped outright, and stops entirely after 60 days with no activity on the repository — which is public, so that rule applies. **`infra/keepalive-worker` overwrites `.github/keepalive` weekly to answer the last of those** — a Cloudflare Worker on a cron trigger, deployed separately, carrying a `public_repo` classic token, and `build-deploy.yml` ignores that path so a keep-alive commit cannot redeploy production. It lives outside this repository on purpose: a workflow that kept its own schedule alive would be disabled by the same rule it was written to escape. All four are idempotent and resume, so lateness costs latency and nothing else — which is why the purge, the one that wanted five minutes, is the one that minds least. The reminders mind most, and answer it in their copy rather than in their cadence (§ 16.3.)
- **The scripts import through deep paths rather than the slice barrels, and only there.** They run in plain Node with no bundler to shake a tree, so `@/entities/message` would drag that slice's `ui/` and the React under it into a process that renders nothing. `tsconfig.ops.json` maps `server-only` to a stub for the same class of reason — and deliberately does **not** use that package's own `react-server` condition, which swaps React for its server build and kills the `@dnd-kit` hook `@/shared/lib` re-exports
- **The reclaim runs on the ten-minute schedule, with 미리보기 and 회수하기 beside it.** The schedule is the routine half and needs nobody; the buttons are for the occasion somebody has just deleted a great deal and wants to know, or to have the space back now. **A preview but no confirmation**, and the two are different questions: `countReclaimable` counts what a run would take, which is worth knowing before spending one, where a confirmation would be ceremony — every row here is one the database has ALREADY marked deleted and the schedule would have taken it within minutes. Asking twice about a safe action is how people learn to dismiss the dialog guarding the unsafe one below it, and both buttons are `secondary` rather than `destructive` on the same argument: that colour means "this removes something you may want", and these bytes are already unreachable
- **The preview counts rather than `LIMIT`s.** `RECLAIM_LIMIT` bounds one pass of the real work, so a preview reading it would answer "200" for a backlog of thousands. Both halves share one predicate with the reclaiming queries — a preview asking a differently worded question would report a number the run does not then match — and it reaches neither `purgeNow` nor a stamp, which is the whole value of pressing it
- **A hand-started reclaim notifies; the schedule does not.** Somebody pressed the button and is waiting for an answer, where the ten-minute run fires whether or not there was anything to do — so `ops-purge.yml` turns the push on for `workflow_dispatch` and leaves it off otherwise, and `PURGE_NOTIFY=true` opts the schedule in anyway. Without that the button would be a dead end: it can only report that it asked
- **Every call goes through this app's own Route Handlers under `/api/ops/`**, never to a third party from the browser. The credential is the whole reason, and it is now a GitHub token rather than jandh-ops' pair: a client that could dispatch a workflow directly would have to carry one that can write to Actions
- **`OPS_GITHUB_TOKEN` has no default, and that is what the screen toggles on.** `isOpsDispatchConfigured` gates 백업 생성 and the sweep out of the UI **and** answers `unavailable` from both routes, since hiding a button is not a guard against a stale client. The flag crosses as a prop, never the credential — 서버 관리 is a client component. The backup list and the per-backup deletion do not consult it: they read R2 directly. `OPS_GITHUB_REPO` and `OPS_GITHUB_REF` default to this repository and `main`, which is also the only ref `schedule` ever fires on
- **This app holds no jandh-ops token at all any more.** `BACKUP_TOKEN`, `CLEANUP_TOKEN` and `OPS_API_URL` are deleted from it, along with `shared/ops`' proxy: 백업 생성 and the sweep dispatch the workflows that already run on a schedule, and the deletion acts on R2 itself. What replaces three secrets is one, and it authorizes starting a named workflow rather than deleting a bucket
- **A failure answers a § 14. code and nothing else**, and the modal turns that code into a sentence. The upstream message is an `aws` stderr line carrying bucket paths and key ids, so it never crosses. **No copy here points at jandh-ops' push**: that notification goes to `BACKUP_NOTIFY_EMAIL` alone — one account of the two — so the reader who pressed the button may be the one it will never reach
- **The two dispatching controls report a REQUEST, not a result.** `workflow_dispatch` answers 204 the moment the run is queued and says nothing about how it goes, so the modal says 백업을 요청했어요 / 정리를 요청했어요 and the run's own push carries the outcome. This retires the timeout problem rather than solving it: the old proxy waited on a `pg_dump` that outlived undici's 300s header timeout, and there is now nothing to wait on
- **No admin role, and none is invented.** § 5.1. already admits exactly two allowlisted emails, so anyone signed in is trusted with this screen

---

## 13. Emoticons ✅

**Emoticons are authored inside the app.** There is no asset drop, no `scripts/seed-emoticons.ts`, and nothing to supply by hand — a user creates a pack from the Settings tab, adds items to it, and both users can send them. This reverses the earlier "supplied manually, registered by an admin script" premise, which assumed § 9. did not exist yet.

### 13.1. Ownership and Scope ✅

- **A pack belongs to the conversation, not to its creator.** Either user may create, rename, add to, and delete any pack; `emoticon_packs.created_by` is a record, never a permission check. An ownership tier here would be the only one in the app
- **A pack is one of two kinds (§ 6.), and the kind is a required argument of every read rather than a filter a caller may forget.** Every list and every by-id read takes it as a required field with no default anywhere, so an omission is a compile error naming the missing property; a default would be the same forgotten filter in another place, and what it hides is a mini rendered into a four-column grid or offered for import beside the ordinary sets. **It is required on the wire too**, so a client that has not been updated fails loudly rather than quietly listing both, and `GET`/`PATCH /api/emoticons/packs/{id}` **select** on it — a pack of the other kind is a 404 instead of a wrong-shaped screen. **`?type=all` joins the two on the list route and only there**, because § 13.6.'s picker fetches one pack list and partitions it itself; an omitted `type` is still a 400, and the detail and create routes refuse `all`
- **Three reads stay kind-agnostic on purpose**: the existence check behind a prefs write, prefs being per-pack whatever the kind; one pack's item list, since the items _are_ their pack's kind and the id came from a list that already named it; and § 13.9.1.'s keyword search, which a mini cannot reach because `0045` keeps its pack out of the index — a filter there would be a second mechanism free to drift from the first
- **Order and visibility are per-user and pack-level**, carried by `user_emoticon_prefs` (`enabled`, `position`, keyed on `(user_id, pack_id)`)
- **Item order is not per-user.** `emoticon_items.sort_order` is **shared** — a per-user item table would make the same pack look different to each user with no surface saying so. **No screen reorders items** (§ 13.4.); only packs are reorderable (§ 13.5.)
- **`PUT /api/emoticons/packs/{id}/items` takes the whole ordered list** and renumbers it in one statement, because `sort_order` is positional and a row per UPDATE would leave the pack half-renumbered if the connection dropped. **The route has no caller since the item drag was removed** — it is kept because the ordering it writes is still the one every picker reads. The list must be exactly the pack's current items; a stale one is answered `409`. The renumbering `CASE` carries an `ELSE` of the row's own `sort_order`, because the completeness check is a separate statement and a row inserted between the two would otherwise renumber to NULL. Like § 13.5., it raises no `user_changed`
- **A user with no `user_emoticon_prefs` row for a pack sees it hidden** — `coalesce(enabled, false)`, in the one SQL predicate and the one `?? false` that read it. **Creating a pack writes one row, the creator's own**, `enabled: true`, in the same transaction as the insert — the pack is in the creator's 사용중 immediately, while the other participant sees it only once they turn it on from 이모티콘 묶음 검색. Both creation paths write it — 직접 만들기 and jandh-emoticons' import (§ 13.7.)
- **This was the other way round until it was inverted, and the inversion needs no migration and no backfill.** `user_emoticon_prefs.enabled` keeps its `DEFAULT true`, because the default fires only when a row is inserted and the missing row is the case being decided — every writer states `enabled` rather than leaning on it, including the reorder upsert's insert branch. What it costs instead is that **every pack nobody has explicitly enabled went hidden at once**, which was accepted deliberately, and that **both deployments must ship together** (§ 3.1.): a build on either side of the change reads the same rows to the opposite conclusion
- A pack is still **ordered by its own creation time** while it has no row, rather than behind every pack the user has expressed an opinion about — § 13.5.'s sparse key puts moved and untouched packs on one number line, and hiding by absence does not disturb it

### 13.2. Item Composition ✅

One item is **two image slots plus one optional sound**, each its own object in R2, and **either image alone is a whole emoticon**.

| Slot     | Column              | Required | Notes                                                                                                                                        |
| -------- | ------------------- | -------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Still    | `still_image_id`    | either   | One frame. What an 이모티콘 picker cell, a tab icon and a quote thumbnail draw                                                               |
| Animated | `animated_image_id` | either   | The same types, animating. What the bubble, the staged preview, and a 미니 picker cell play — a mini is only ever shown moving, never frozen |
| Audio    | `audio_id`          | no       | Played on tap only (§ 13.6.). An item may carry audio whether or not it animates                                                             |

- **A slot names a `media` row rather than an R2 key**, so an emoticon's objects are the same rows every other upload writes (§ 9.). The `r2_key`, `mime`, `audio_key`, `audio_mime`, `width` and `height` columns the single-image era stored beside them are gone, dropped by `0042`
- **`emoticon_items_has_image_check` is the floor**: `still_image_id IS NOT NULL OR animated_image_id IS NOT NULL`. § 13.3.'s asset route falls back **both ways**, so an item holding one slot answers a request for the other and no screen has to test which it has
- **Which slot a file belongs in is read off its bytes, never its type.** `image/webp` and `image/gif` are each perfectly legal for a single frame, and an APNG is stored as whichever of `image/avif` / `image/png` it was re-encoded to — so a MIME test is wrong in both directions and wrong silently: a picture in the animated slot is a bubble that plays nothing, an animation in the still slot is a picker cell that moves. `isAnimatedImage` reads the container instead — `acTL` before the first `IDAT`, a second GIF image descriptor, a WebP `ANMF` — and is the same answer in the browser and on the server. A decoder cannot stand in for it: `createImageBitmap` and an `<img>` both report success for one frame and for sixty, and neither runs server-side at all
- **The server repeats that test against the stored object**, for the reason § 14. gives about type and size — the upload goes straight to R2, so the browser's answer never reached it. It reads the **whole** object rather than a prefix, because a GIF's second image descriptor may sit anywhere in the file: a prefix can confirm an animation and can never refuse one, and refusing is the half that matters
- **The box is the slot's own `media.width` / `height`**, read from the decoded image in the browser exactly as § 9. does for chat media — `features/upload-media` is reused, not reimplemented. Every read that builds an `Emoticon` joins both image slots for it and takes the **animation's** wherever there is one, because § 8.3. reserves the _bubble's_ box and the bubble plays the animation. An animated file is measured from its decoded first frame, which is the box every later frame shares
- **`updated_at` is exposed as `Emoticon.version` and rides on every asset URL.** Editing an item (§ 13.4.) swaps the object behind an unchanged item id, and the asset route answers a cached redirect — without the version the browser keeps serving the image the edit replaced. A screen holding only a pack summary carries the thumbnail item's version on the summary itself (`EmoticonPackSummary.thumbnailVersion`): an unversioned URL is not merely stale, it names an object the edit's cleanup will remove
- **A pack's thumbnail is one of its own items**, not a separate upload: `emoticon_packs.thumbnail_item_id`, nullable FK, **`ON DELETE SET NULL`, never cascade** — deleting the item a pack uses as its tab icon must not delete the pack. Null means the pack is drawn with its first item instead. The FK is **circular**, so it is added as a separate `ALTER TABLE` after both tables exist
- **That fallback is resolved on the server, in `listEmoticonPacks`, and cannot move back.** § 13.6.'s picker used to reach for `pack.items[0]` in the browser; it holds summaries and no items now, so a pack with no chosen thumbnail would simply lose its tab icon. `EmoticonPackSummary.thumbnailItemId` therefore means "the item this pack is drawn with", and stays null only for a pack with no items at all — the one case that still draws a glyph. § 13.5.'s management rows draw the same resolved thumbnail, which is the intended consequence rather than a side effect
- **`getEmoticonPack` deliberately does not resolve it**, and this exception is the subtle one.
