That is the pack detail screen, where the thumbnail is _chosen_ — resolved there it would mark an item nobody picked as 대표 and offer 대표로 지정 on a pack that has none. The summary a detail screen carries is the pack's **stored** choice; the list's is the drawn one

### 13.3. Asset Storage ✅

Emoticon objects ride § 9.'s presigned-PUT pipeline — client → R2 directly, key built server-side — and they **are `media` rows**, reached through the three slots of § 13.2. `emoticon_items` holds no R2 key of its own; `0042` dropped the last of them.

- That reversed three objections, each answered by something the restructure changed rather than waived. `media` is still the library's single source (§ 10.) and an emoticon still never appears there — an emoticon asset hangs off no `message_media` row, and § 18. #1. made that the whole of membership, so the exclusion is structural rather than a filter over the library's one source. The `_thumb` sibling is now required per **scope**, and an emoticon needs none: its own object is already tile-sized, and the fixed 720px JPEG would flatten the alpha that transparent art is drawn with (`DESIGN.md § 6.5.`). `media.width` / `height` are nullable, so an audio object may carry neither
- What is **not** duplicated is § 14.'s enforcement: registration `HeadObject`s every key it is about to write and refuses anything outside the allow-list, by the same shared helper. R2 enforces neither the signed `Content-Type` nor any size (§ 9.)
- Key scope is `emoticon/{userId}/{uuid}`. The uploader's id stays in the key even though the pack is shared, because the key is the ownership proof at registration
- **Read authorization is a plain session check**, not `canReadMedia`: packs are conversation-wide. This is the one place a bare session is sufficient, and it is sufficient _because_ the pack is shared
- **The asset redirect is cached for days, not minutes, and that is the difference from § 9.** The URL carries `v` (§ 13.2.), so it addresses one immutable version of one item — an edit produces a different URL rather than different bytes behind the same one. The presigned GET is signed for `EMOTICON_URL_EXPIRY` (7 days, SigV4's ceiling). The old 5-minute pair meant a **freshly uploaded emoticon fetched over the network on every mount** — R2 answers no `Cache-Control` of its own, so the browser fell back to a heuristic lifetime of a tenth of the object's age
- **The signature is dated to a 5-day grid (`EMOTICON_SIGNING_BUCKET`), not to the wall clock, and that is what makes the year-long `immutable` on the bytes worth anything.** Signed at `new Date()`, two requests a second apart returned the same object under two different URLs — measured in production, `X-Amz-Date` and the signature differing across a 5-second gap. The browser's cache is keyed by URL, so every 302 that fell out of cache re-downloaded an object already held (343KB and ~430ms for one animated emoticon). Quantised, a whole window's requests are answered with the byte-identical URL, and nothing is stored anywhere to achieve it — the presign is a pure function of key and window
- **`EMOTICON_CACHE_MAX_AGE` is a day, deliberately shorter than the window above.** The two are spent out of the same 7 days and their sum must stay under it (a request landing at the end of a window holds a URL already 5 days old), but the ordering is the point rather than the arithmetic: a browser re-asking inside the window is handed the _same_ URL, so it pays one 302 — a session check and a row read, no bytes — and keeps the object. Bytes are re-fetched only when the window itself rolls over. The previous 6-day cache spent the entire budget on the redirect and re-downloaded on every miss
- **The bytes get their `Cache-Control` from the presigned GET, not from the object** (`EMOTICON_ASSET_CACHE_CONTROL`, a year, `immutable`, signed in as `response-cache-control`). Storing it on the object instead is what **broke every emoticon upload**: `Cache-Control` is not a CORS-safelisted request header, so the PUT carrying it needed the bucket's `AllowedHeaders` to name it (§ 9.) and failed preflight. It is **not** signed on the PUT either way — `cache-control` is in SigV4's always-unsignable set
- **`DELETE /api/emoticons/upload-url` takes back objects that landed for a submit that never produced an item** (§ 13.4.). It **releases reservations** (§ 9.): only keys under the caller's own `emoticon/{userId}/` prefix, and only claims still live and still theirs. A key that reached a `media` row has had its claim consumed, so this structurally cannot reach a registered object — where the old test asked which keys were **slotted into an item**, and was wrong: an object registered but not yet slotted had a live `media` row and was deleted anyway. **Expire, purge, then delete**, and none of the three may be collapsed into a `DELETE … RETURNING`: the row is the only thing naming the object, so removing it before R2 answers is the orphan the table exists to prevent. Expiring first is what makes the middle step safe — `consumeReservations` refuses a lapsed claim, so a registration racing the takeback cannot commit a live row and then have its object deleted out from under it. The expiring `UPDATE` also carries a `NOT EXISTS` against `media`, so a key that **has** already registered is skipped outright rather than merely left un-deleted, and the final `DELETE` names only the keys R2 confirmed

### 13.4. Authoring ✅

**Landed** in full.

- **Create a pack from a title alone.** No thumbnail, no items required — an empty pack is a valid intermediate state, because the thumbnail can only be chosen from items that do not exist yet
- **Add an item from one image**, still or animated (§ 13.2.); the sound is optional and added in the same form. An item carried **no title**, because nothing rendered one — **§ 13.8. reversed that** and the sheet now edits a keyword _list_, which the composer reads back. The rule the reversal keeps is the original one: a field nothing reads is a field that should not exist
- The image is picked through `features/upload-media`'s `useMediaPicker` and may then be cropped and filtered in its `MediaEditor` — both reused, not reimplemented. A **free-form crop** (`자유`) is the default alongside the fixed ratios
- **A still is encoded lossy exactly once, at 저장, and only when it is a new picture.** The pick, the cutout and every crop are carried as lossless PNG for the life of the sheet, so no number of edits adds a generation; 저장 takes a fresh pick's one AVIF q55 pass (PNG fallback, never JPEG — which flattens alpha onto an opaque box, glaring on the bubble-less emoticon of `DESIGN.md § 6.5.`; chat keeps JPEG) and uploads a stored still's re-edit as the PNG it is. Cancelling a stored still's flow before its first crop unstages it, so 저장 re-uploads nothing
- **An animation never enters the editor, but it is still re-encoded.** A canvas crop decodes one frame and re-encodes a still, so the 편집 control is not offered for an animated pick; it is transcoded to animated WebP by `optimizeAnimation` instead (§ 9.), at `EMOTICON_MAX_EDGE` and **lossless** — lossy WebP codes alpha separately and frays the edges of art drawn without a bubble behind it, which is why this departs from the chat path's lossy setting. A static one is carried as PNG at the same cap and may be cropped. What decides is `isAnimatedImage` (§ 13.2.) and never the file's type — the stored object keeps whatever type the presigned PUT was signed with, which is `image/avif` or `image/png` and is what § 14. therefore checks
- **One image field, and an animated pick fills both slots.** The animation is the picked bytes; the still is extracted from them in the browser — the **fullest** of at most eight evenly spaced frames, because an animation that fades in opens on a nearly empty one and a first-frame still reads as a half-transparent tile in the picker. `ImageDecoder` is what reaches past frame 0; where it is unavailable the first frame is all `createImageBitmap` can give. A static pick fills the still alone and sends `animated: null` to drop the animation it replaced, which is no longer a rendering of that picture. There is **no clear button** — `emoticon_items_has_image_check` forbids an item with no image, so replacing is the only operation the field has
- The picker is restricted to images — `useMediaPicker` takes its `accept` from the caller. **The image slot opens the OS picker directly** (`DESIGN.md § 7.5.`), because one image is its whole choice. The sound row offers either one audio file from that picker or a direct microphone recording through the shared § 9.3. recorder; its finished file enters the same draft, validation and submit path as a picked file. 여러 장 한 번에 추가 on the pack screen is the same — the row that names it is already the choice, and the sheet it used to open added a second one saying 사진/영상
- Everything uploads on **final submit, not on pick**, so abandoning the form leaves nothing in the bucket. A submit that fails after some slots have landed gives those objects back through § 13.3.'s discard endpoint; the slots are awaited with **`allSettled`, not `all`**, precisely so every key that reached R2 is known to the failure path
- **The editor opens over the authoring sheet, not under it.** `MediaEditor` portals into the app shell (`ShellOverlay`) while the sheet portals into `body`, so no z-index inside the shell can lift it — the sheet closes for as long as the editor is up
- **Set a pack's thumbnail by choosing one of its items**, from the pack's own detail screen
- **Every write on the pack screen ends in `router.refresh()`.** Back/forward remounts a page from the router cache's RSC payload, so 채팅's 뒤로 (`router.back()`) brought the grid back without the item just added until a reload; the refresh replaces that payload and keeps the screen's own `items` state
- **Items are not reorderable.** Long-press-and-drag was implemented and removed: with no room for a grip handle in a 4-column square the cell had to carry the drag listeners itself, so the gesture sat on top of the tap that opens the item's actions. The grid renders in the server's `sort_order`
- **Edit an item after it is uploaded**, from the same sheet that authored it: replace the image, add a sound, remove a sound. `PATCH /api/emoticons/items/{id}` takes only the slots that changed — an absent `audioKey` keeps the sound, an explicit `null` removes it — and a replaced image must arrive with its new measurements, since § 8.3. reserves the bubble's box from them. The objects the edit detaches are deleted from R2 **immediately**, and that argument survives verbatim: a deferred delete cannot survive § 13.3.'s multi-day cache. What changed is only the mechanism — "immediately" is an inline `await purgeNow` in the route, over rows the same transaction soft-deleted, with § 9.'s reclaim as the durable backstop for a bucket that refuses. **Emoticons are the one scope with zero grace**, and the cache is the whole reason: § 12.'s five-minute window is shorter than a peer's cached 302, where an emoticon's is days and no delay this app could name would outlive it. What pays for it is on the read side — `PreloadImage` retries a failed load once against a cache-busted URL, so the cost is one broken load rather than a permanent one. Pack and item deletion purge on the same terms
- **The sound row auditions what the submit would save**, through the § 13.6. shared player: a newly picked file off its object URL, a kept one off its R2 asset URL. Authoring and editing both get it, since a sound that has been on an item for weeks is the one nobody remembers. Closing the sheet **stops the player before it revokes** the draft's object URLs, or an audition still running is left sourcing a dead blob
- **An item already sent in chat may still be edited**, unlike deleting one. The message references the _item_, not the object, so no foreign key is in the way, and the corrected image showing up in history is the point. The `updated_at` bump is what makes it visible past the cached asset redirect
- **A stored still is re-edited from its thumbnail**, which is a control whenever the item holds a still and no animation — a stored animation is fetched from its own slot into § 13.4.1.'s flow instead, since the canvas editor would flatten it. The sheet fetches `?variant=edit`, which **streams** the object from this origin the way § 12.1.'s does — a 302 into R2 taints the canvas (`CLAUDE.md § 5.3.`) — and hands the `File` to the same 누끼 → 영역 자르기 walk a pick takes, saved lossless. Whether the still was already cut out is not detected; re-running 누끼 is the user's call
- **The stored image and the stored sound each have a 다운로드 control**, shown only while the sheet is showing them rather than a staged replacement. `?download=1` signs `attachment; filename*=…` into the presigned GET (`emoticon-{id}.{ext}` from the row's mime), and the click is a detached-anchor navigation for § 10.'s reason: `download` is dropped once the 302 resolves cross-origin, so the signed disposition is what saves the file
- **A mini carries no words at all.** No search reaches one (§ 13.9.1.), so § 13.4.'s sheet offers no field for a mini, the import preview offers none either, and neither grid puts a line under a mini's cell — there is nothing there to show or to edit. The server keeps `keywords` optional on both write paths and `normalizeKeywords` keeps its per-kind cap, so a mini written before this still reads back exactly as it was stored. § 13.8.1.'s suggester is withheld from minis entirely, on the pack screen and in the import preview alike
- **Deleting an item, mini or not, keeps its row, stamps `deleted_at` (§ 6.), soft-deletes its media and purges the objects.** A mini is never asked whether anything has sent it — that is an unindexable array scan, and the answer would change nothing since the row is kept either way; a normal emoticon is not asked either, for the same reason, now that its delete keeps the row too. **`deleteEmoticonPack` soft-deletes the pack (`emoticon_packs.deleted_at`, `0049`) instead of refusing** when an item has been written into a message's text or `messages.emoticon_item_id` — a hard `DELETE` would otherwise cascade into items a bubble still sizes itself from
- **A soft delete has to do by hand what the hard one got from the schema, and `0050` is the two places that mattered.** `emoticon_keywords` was kept level by its FK's `ON DELETE cascade` alone — the sync trigger fires on `INSERT` and on `UPDATE OF keywords`, neither of which a stamp touches — so a deleted item's index rows outlived it and § 13.8.'s composer went on underlining a word whose search then returned nothing. The trigger now also fires on `UPDATE OF deleted_at` and the function skips its insert for a stamped row. `emoticon_packs.thumbnail_item_id` was the other: its `ON DELETE SET NULL` is what fell a pack back on its first item, so a pack whose 대표 was deleted kept requesting a purged object and R2 404'd the tab icon forever — `deleteEmoticonItem` clears it in the same transaction, and the migration backfills both
- **A deleted item is a 404 to `PATCH`, not an edit.** Its objects are purged, so a replacement registered against it is a `media` row the delete can never come back for while the row goes on drawing its tombstone
- **`deleteEmoticonPack` stamps by `pack_id` inside its transaction rather than by an id list read beforehand.** An item landing between the two reads would never be stamped, and no item-level read joins its pack — so it would stay reachable from 이모티콘 검색 and 즐겨찾기 with its objects already gone
- **`isChoosable` is the one spelling of not-deleted**, which the picker, the search, 최근 사용, the tab-icon subquery and the item count each need. Mirroring it is what turned up that jandh-emoticons' tab-icon and item-count subqueries had never filtered deleted items where this repository's did — two apps on one database answering differently, fixed by the sharing rather than by finding it
- **Adding a pile of images at once**, one item per image. The pack screen's `+` opens a sheet offering 하나씩 추가 (the authoring sheet above) and 여러 장 한 번에 추가, which opens the picker with `multiple`. Several files go straight to registration with neither editor nor sound; a single file picked there still opens the authoring sheet
  - Files upload through a **bounded pool** (`mapPooled`), `UPLOAD_CONCURRENCY` wide **and** under a `MAX_UPLOAD_INFLIGHT_BYTES` ceiling. **Both halves are load-bearing and this is where that argument lives**, since § 9. and § 10. share the pool: a bare concurrency count cannot tell twenty 8MB emoticons from nine 500MB videos, and firing the latter four abreast is the timeout the original one-at-a-time loop existed to avoid. The byte budget always admits **one** task even when it alone exceeds the budget, or an upload larger than the ceiling would wait forever
  - **Registration is not pooled** — `POST` runs strictly in pick order behind the uploads, because `emoticon_items.sort_order` is assigned from `max(sort_order) + 1` at insert and racing the POSTs would shuffle the pack against the order the user picked in
  - The progress line counts what is **left** (`n개를 더 올리는 중이에요`), decremented per settled file, because it sits beside a grid that is already filling in and a fixed total would contradict the rows next to it
- **The `+` is disabled for as long as a pile is landing**, and it is the only route into the bulk path. Two batches would each run their own in-order registration chain and interleave two picks in one `sort_order` sequence, and `setAddingCount(files.length)` is an absolute write the overlap would corrupt. § 10.'s library deliberately does the opposite — its grid is ordered by capture date, so an interleave there is invisible
- **A failed bulk add names the files it dropped**, in a `Modal` (`추가하지 못한 이모티콘`) rather than a count-only toast. A pile fails per file and for different reasons — `지원하지 않는 형식이에요`, `8MB를 넘어요`, `파일을 읽지 못했어요`, `업로드하지 못했어요`, `등록하지 못했어요` — and a bare count leaves the user re-picking twenty images to find the three that did not land. The list scrolls at a fixed height. **The read and the upload need separate `try` blocks** to be told apart: under one block the uploaded key is only ever assigned by the call that would have thrown, so a timed-out PUT reports the user's perfectly good file as `파일을 읽지 못했어요`

#### 13.4.1. An emoticon from a video ✅

**A clip becomes an emoticon**: pick, cut to `MAX_EMOTICON_VIDEO_DURATION` (6s), frame, and the authoring sheet's image field fills with both slots while its 소리 row fills with the clip's own.

- **영상에서 sits in that sheet's image row, beside 편집** — not in the pack screen's add sheet, where it would put 개수 (하나씩 / 여러 장) and 출처 (이미지 / 영상) on one list and offer 하나씩 추가 and 영상으로 만들기 as if they made different things. A clip is another way to fill the one image field, which is also what makes it available when **editing** an item. The sheet closes for the flow's duration, exactly as it does for `MediaEditor` and for the same portal reason

- **The trimmer takes a ceiling rather than § 12.1.'s window.** A background loops, so its length is decided and only its position is chosen; an emoticon's length is part of the gesture, so both handles move and only their spread is bounded. `VideoTrimmer` now reports the range beside the file, because the sound is cut from the source rather than from its output
- **The crop is a rectangle handed to `cropVideo`, never a crop of the finished animation** — a canvas crop decodes one frame (§ 13.4.). `EMOTICON_MAX_EDGE` on that call is also what keeps the encode affordable: what reaches ffmpeg is a 420px clip rather than the 4K one that was picked
- **`animateVideo` throws where `optimizeAnimation` falls back**, since falling back to the file it was given would put an MP4 in the animated slot. It walks an fps/quality ladder until an encode lands under `MAX_EMOTICON_IMAGE_SIZE`, and reports exhausting it as 더 짧게 잘라 보세요 — the only move left. Lossy, unlike § 13.4.'s hand-drawn animations: footage has no alpha edges to fray and every frame of it is noise. **A cut-out clip (§ 13.4.2.) stays lossy too**: lossless was measured at 17MB for 72 frames of 420px footage, which no rung brings under the cap, and libwebp codes the alpha plane losslessly at every quality, so the matte's edge survives where only the chroma beside it is approximated
- **The sound is never asked about.** It is extracted from the **source** at the kept range — `trimVideo` and `cropVideo` both discard the track — and staged into the sheet's 소리 row, which already auditions, replaces and removes. A modal at 완료 would be answered a second time in that row a moment later
- **Both screens play the clip, and both play it out loud.** The trimmer auditions the range between the handles from ▶ and loops back at the end handle, because a cut is a length and a sound and neither of those is a frame; the 영역 자르기 screen — retitled, since it and the trimmer were both 영상 자르기 — loops the clip under the stencil through `backgroundComponent`, over the poster it is still measured against, and plays it **out loud from the start** — the trim that runs in between spends the gesture it was reached by, so playback is attempted unmuted and falls back to muted where the engine refuses, with the bar's own control either way. That control had nothing to unmute until `trimVideo` took `keepsAudio`: it discards the track by default (§ 12.1.), so what the cropper was handed had never carried one. It falls back to a silent cut where the browser cannot copy the track, which is the same Safari the default was chosen for
- **The two screens are a sequence, and their bars say so.** The trimmer ends in → rather than 완료 (`hasNextStep`), since a screen follows it, and reports the cut in its title the way 영역 자르기 reports its re-encode; 영역 자르기's ← returns to it on the same clip and at the same cut (`initialRange`). 취소 stays on the trimmer, which is the flow's only exit
- **An animation is re-edited from the thumbnail whatever it is.** The source clip is preferred where the sheet still holds it — kept beside the slots, dropped on every other pick — since it alone can also be cut shorter; every other animation, a picked GIF or a stored item's animated slot at `?variant=edit&slot=animated-image`, walks 누끼 → 영역 자르기 over its own frames through `animateImage`, which folds the crop, the rotation and the ladder into one encode. **`ImageDecoder` reads those frames** because the bundled ffmpeg is 5.1.4, whose WebP decoder ignores `ANMF`, and because transcoding to h264 first would drop the alpha the emoticon is. The thumbnail is a control only when there is something to edit, and carries a ✎ over it when it is
- **ffmpeg only runs from an **unbundled** worker, and this is why the animation path had never worked at all.** `@ffmpeg/ffmpeg`'s worker ends in `import(coreURL)`, a specifier Turbopack cannot resolve — it replaces it with a stub throwing `Cannot find module as expression is too dynamic`, so every encode since the animation path shipped failed and `optimizeAnimation`, which answers a failure by uploading the original, hid all of it. `pnpm ffmpeg-core` copies that worker out of the package beside the core, and `loadFfmpeg` points `classWorkerURL` at it **absolute against `location.origin`**: `FFmpeg.load` resolves the URL against an `import.meta.url` that Turbopack leaves as a `file://` path, so a root-relative one becomes `file:///ffmpeg/worker.js` and the `Worker` constructor refuses it
- **취소 during the encode tears the core down, and the core is torn down after every encode anyway.** `ffmpeg.exec` cannot be interrupted any other way, and the core is no longer kept across calls (§ 13.4.2.), so `releaseFfmpeg(true)` is the cancel; the counter still tells the matte and the audio that settle behind it apart from a live run

#### 13.4.2. Taking the background off ✅

**A picked image loses its background before it is framed**, on a step of its own between the pick and § 13.4.'s crop. The order is deliberate: the matte's own bounding box is what the crop opens on, and an emoticon is cut tight to its art.

- **The step is a screen with a switch, not an automatic pass.** 누끼 sits beside 다음 in the top bar, off until asked, and 다음 hands the crop either picture. The switch is what _runs_ the model — the matte was eager once, and that made every pick pay for a cutout the user may not have wanted
- **It is off by default because of one measurement.** RMBG-1.4 at its fixed 1024² is ~2.5s on a desktop and **11.8s on an iPhone 18.7** (4 cores, wasm). An opt-in 12s is a wait the user chose; the same 12s on every pick is not
- **iOS has no WebGPU, and that is the whole performance story.** Safari 26.6 reports `navigator.gpu` absent, so the phone — the only device this app is built for (§ 4.1.) — runs the model on wasm and gets no acceleration at all. WebGPU is worth ~1.6× where it exists (2.5s → 1.6s on desktop Chrome) and is taken opportunistically, never depended on
- **The model is RMBG-1.4, self-hosted, fetched lazily.** `pnpm cutout-model` copies onnxruntime-web's four wasm variants and downloads the 96MB fp16 export into `public/cutout/`, which is gitignored and cached against the hub's `x-linked-etag`. It is **not** in `offline-precache.json` — 96MB at install time for a step most picks skip
- **Nothing is fetched off-origin at runtime**, which is why the weights are self-hosted rather than pulled from the hub. `AGENTS.md § 4.2.1.` admits two off-origin fetches and this is neither; `allowRemoteModels = false` and an explicit `wasmPaths` are what enforce it, and a missing `wasmPaths` is a **hard failure** rather than a skipped assignment, since its default is a jsDelivr URL that works and would ship
- **The four onnxruntime variants all ship.** The runtime picks among `jsep` (WebGPU), `jspi`, `asyncify` (the WebKit with neither) and the plain build at load; a missing one surfaces as `no available backend found`, which names nothing
- **Their glue is patched at copy time to take the largest shared memory the engine allows.** Every variant is a pthread build and asks for a shared `WebAssembly.Memory` with a 4GB maximum; WebKit reserves a shared memory's whole maximum up front, and iOS — a home-screen app in particular — refuses it with `RangeError: Out of memory`, reported as the same `no available backend found` (microsoft/onnxruntime#22086). `pnpm cutout-model` rewrites that one call into a 4GB → 2GB → 1GB → 512MB probe, which the wasm accepts since an imported memory may carry a smaller maximum than the module declares. The build fails if the glue no longer matches, so an upgrade cannot silently restore the reservation
- **This export ends in a Sigmoid and the code must not add a second one.** It was added, and the cost was not an exception — a doubled sigmoid maps the whole matte into [0.5, 0.731], reaching alpha as [127, 186], so every cutout came out uniformly half-transparent and read as a _soft model_. It is what made RMBG-1.4 look worse than BiRefNet in the comparison that chose between them; corrected, its matte is 254/0 where BiRefNet's is 255/0
- **BiRefNet was measured and is out of reach**, on grounds a newer export does not fix by itself. `BiRefNet_lite` is frozen at 1024² and exhausts the wasm heap there (`std::bad_alloc`); every BiRefNet asks 11 storage buffers of a WebGPU stage that allows 10; and the 512² export that does run is 464MB an iPhone never finishes loading
- **The worker lives for the session, and `terminate()` is off the table on the phone.** A wasm heap never shrinks, so one still leaves the worker holding ~1GB, and an iPhone 13 mini then reaches iOS's memory limit at the next clip's crop step — a silent full reload, no error — and sometimes inside the RMBG pass itself. Dropping the worker on entering the crop step was tried and made that reload happen **every time**, with RMBG's heap and with MODNet's alike, while the iOS 18.5 and 26.1 simulators survived the same terminate; whatever the phone does on it is unknown until its crash log says. The two video screens detach their `<video>`'s source on unmount rather than leaving the decoder to GC
- **The ffmpeg core is released the same way, once its queue is idle.** One encode passes a clip and its matted frames through the core's virtual FS and leaves the heap that size; reloading the ~32MB core from Cache Storage is a second or two against an encode of tens, and a burst of GIFs queued together still shares one load
- **The alpha is multiplied into whatever the source had**, never assigned over it — a picked PNG can arrive with transparency of its own
- **A picked animation never enters this step**, for § 13.4.'s reason: one matted frame is a picture, not an animation. A clip does, through the subsection below
- **`MediaEditor`'s 편집 button is gone from the image row.** A pick now walks 누끼 → 영역 자르기 by itself, so the only edit left is the one the thumbnail opens — and a conditional button between the two sources shifted them every time an image was replaced

##### An emoticon from a video ✅

**§ 13.4.1.'s clip gets the step too, on a different model.** 누끼 sits between the trimmer and 영역 자르기, and its switch mattes the clip's **poster alone** — a preview of what the encode will do, costing one inference. The clip itself is matted **frame by frame inside `animateVideo`, after the crop**, where every frame is already 420px, and that pass is folded into `VideoEncodingOverlay`'s bar (80% matte, 20% encode). The cut-out ladder starts at 12fps and drops frames from the one matte rather than matting again at each rung.

- **RMBG-1.4 cannot do it, and the reason is arithmetic that no input size fixes.** 90 frames at 11.8s is 17.8 min on the phone. A dynamic-axes re-export from torch **works** (identical output at 1024², `Resize` ops carry the size) but buys less than its pixels promise: 768² is 0.59× the time, 640² 0.43×, 512² 0.32× — and below 768² the saliency widens, taking the twigs behind a cat and the branch under a bird (IoU 0.86 and 0.35 against the 1024² matte). The q8 export is **no faster** on wasm (6.37s against 6.28s). **Tiling frames into one 1024² input was measured and rejected**: with four copies of one subject RMBG mattes two tiles and floods the rest, with four different subjects it drops whichever is least salient (bird 0.44, a horse 0.00), and a gap between tiles makes it worse. Flipping and rotating the tiles held for a cat and nothing else. 3×3 fails in every arrangement
- **MODNet is the video model** (`Xenova/modnet`, 13MB fp16, `shortest_edge` 384 written by `pnpm cutout-model` rather than the hub's 512). It is **14.5× cheaper than RMBG on wasm** — 434ms against 6,280ms on a desktop, so ~0.8s a frame on the phone and under a minute for a 6s clip at 12fps. It is a portrait matting model: on people it is RMBG's equal or better (it leaves the logos behind a person that RMBG keeps), on a cat or a dog within 1–2% of RMBG's matte, and on a bird it takes the branch. A still keeps RMBG, which is the better matte whenever one inference is all there is
- **Frames are decoded with `mediabunny`'s `VideoSampleSink` at the rung's timestamps**, matted through the same worker the still uses, and handed to ffmpeg as two image sequences joined by `alphamerge` — the colour as JPEG and the matte as grey PNG. One RGBA PNG sequence was tried first and measured **22MB** for 72 frames: footage is noise, which PNG cannot compress, where the same frames are ~3MB of JPEG beside ~1MB of alpha. The two sequences come from the same decoded frames, never the clip plus a matte track, because ffmpeg's `fps` and mediabunny's `samplesAtTimestamps` pick a frame per slot by different rules
- **Per-frame matting flickers at the edges** and nothing smooths it yet; a temporal blend would lag a moving subject, and the cost was judged the lesser one

### 13.5. Management (Settings) ✅

- A settings row (§ 12.) opens the emoticon management screen, and it is **two tabs rather than one flat list**: 사용중, the packs this user has enabled in their own order, and 이모티콘 묶음 검색, the whole library. It still matches KakaoTalk, which is split the same way into 내 이모티콘 and the 이모티콘 샵 — what was wrong in the sentence this replaces was the flatness, never the reference
- **The split dissolves a conflict rather than expressing a taste in navigation.** A library holds up to **10,000 packs** and a user enables **thirty or forty**; those are different problems and one list answers neither well. Ten thousand rows cannot be found by scrolling, so the browse list has to be windowed and cursor-paged — and windowing is precisely what **`@dnd-kit` cannot survive**, because `SortableContext` is handed the whole ordered array and a drag cannot cross a window that was never rendered. Splitting the screen is what makes both true at once instead of trading one failure for the other: reordering stays on the small whole list, and windowing goes where nothing reorders
- **미니이모티콘 gets a settings row and a screen of its own at `/settings/minis`** — a sibling route rendering the same pair of components with the kind as a prop, rather than a query parameter or a `[kind]` segment. Three pieces of state keyed to a location are what decided it: `lastTab` and `lastQuery` are module memories and the back target is a static push, so a query parameter would have made both kinds share one remembered tab and one remembered search word. A segment would additionally have collided with the `/settings/devices` and `/settings/server` siblings, and two routes are what give each kind a `loading.tsx` whose fallback draws the right column count. `EMOTICON_PACK_ROW_HEIGHT` did **not** decide it — that measures the pack _row_, which is one shape for both kinds
- **The item grid is 6 columns for minis against 4 for emoticons, written out as literal classes in all three places that must agree** — the grid, its streaming fallback and the cell. Tailwind scans source text, so a computed `grid-cols-${n}` produces no class at all. § 13.6.'s own column constants are deliberately **not** imported here: those are an arrow-key step (§ 8.14.), and these grids are not arrow-navigated
- **Every noun on the two screens comes from one map and every interpolated particle goes through `josa`** (`AGENTS.md § 0.4.`), so the pair cannot drift in wording. Writing one particle by hand was a compile error, which is that rule paying for itself
- **A pack is `이모티콘 묶음` in the UI and an item is `이모티콘`.** Both were called `이모티콘`, which made "이모티콘에 이모티콘을 추가한다" the only way to describe authoring. Screen titles, the create sheet, the picker's empty state and the pack's delete button all follow this split; `pack` / `item` stay as they are in code
- The screen creates, **renames** and deletes packs; the pack's own screen creates and deletes items
- **The `+` opens an `ActionSheet` (`이모티콘 묶음 추가`) with two rows, not the create form directly.** 직접 만들기 is the form it always opened; URL로 추가 hands off to jandh-emoticons' import screen at `/emoticons/import` (§ 13.7.), or at `/emoticons/import/mini` from the 미니 screen. The sheet is the same component and the same copy jandh-emoticons renders over its own list — a row added to either belongs in both
- **사용중 is fetched whole by the server component** — `listEmoticonPacks(userId, { enabledOnly: true })`, no paging and no virtualization, and neither is wanted at thirty rows. The row is thumbnail · name over its item count · chevron · `⋮` · grip. With everything hidden it is empty rather than absent, and its empty state names the way back: `사용 중인 이모티콘 묶음이 없어요. 이모티콘 묶음 검색에서 켜거나 오른쪽 위 + 로 만든 뒤 켤 수 있어요` — 만든 뒤 켤, because a new pack is hidden (§ 13.1.)
- **The `⋮` replaced a gear, and it must never become a `☰`.** In a reorderable list a hamburger _is_ the drag-handle idiom, so it would sit one slot from a real grip with nothing to tell the two apart by
- **The sheet behind it is three rows — `이름 바꾸기` / `숨기기` / `이모티콘 묶음 삭제` — and 수정 is deliberately absent.** Tapping the row already opens the pack, so a 수정 in the sheet would be a second name for what the row does, and two controls called 수정 cannot be told apart. Rename and delete hang off this sheet rather than off the pack's own screen, where delete sat one thumb-width from 이모티콘 추가. The row also carries a chevron, since nothing else said the name was a link
- **숨기기 moved off the row's switch into that sheet, and it took an 실행 취소 rather than a confirmation.** Hiding destroys nothing — it writes `enabled` and touches the position not at all — so a confirmation would tax every intentional hide in order to protect the occasional slip, where an undo charges only the person who slipped. **The undo is honest because `PATCH` never writes `position`**: restoring `enabled` returns the row to exactly the place it left, with no order to record and none to guess. 삭제 keeps its `Modal` beside it, and 숨기기 is why it can afford to — that one takes the pack from the other user as well
- **The row collapses its height on the way out and is dropped when that transition lands**, rather than vanishing on the tap: an instant removal leaves the eye nothing to follow to the toast at the other end of the screen. The commit is gated on the collapse — the same `transitionend` fires for the _expansion_ a failed write puts back, and read as a collapse it would drop the row the screen has just restored
- **Long-press then drag the grip to reorder**, on 사용중 alone, vertical axis only, writing `user_emoticon_prefs.position`
- **While one app is on `position` and the other still on `sort_order`, pack order is per-app rather than shared.** `0027` leaves the column nullable so both can write without erroring (§ 6.), so nothing breaks — the two screens simply remember different orders until `0028` closes the gap. `enabled` is unaffected, since both read and write that same column, and `emoticon_items.sort_order` is a different column entirely
- **The order is a sparse key, not an index, and the null is what the design rests on.** `position` is `numeric` and nullable; a pack that has never been moved has no row and falls back to the **milliseconds reconstructed from its own id** (§ 6.) — **the same number line**, which is what makes moved and untouched packs interleave by value instead of forming two blocks. A move reads its two new neighbours' effective positions and writes the midpoint into **one row**. Bisecting a `numeric` never runs out of room — **as long as the midpoint is `* 0.5` rather than `/ 2`**, since multiplication is exact and widens the scale by a digit where division rounds to the operands' own — so there is deliberately no renumbering and no defence against exhaustion. One expression, `effectivePackPosition`, is both the list's `ORDER BY` and the space a midpoint is computed in — two spellings would put a pack at a position it does not then sort to
- **The fallback reconstructs milliseconds rather than using the id itself, and that was measured.** `0027` backfilled `position` from `sort_order`, so the stored values are small ordinals against a fallback at ~1.7e12 — the fallback's magnitude is therefore what decides where a never-moved pack sorts among the moved ones. Run against production, the millisecond form left all 58 (user, pack) pairs in the order they were already in while `id::numeric` reordered four, so no rebalance was needed. The epoch and shift are imported into the SQL from `identity/id.ts` rather than written out, which is the only reason reading the id here is not a third copy of the format
- **`PUT /api/emoticons/prefs` takes one move, `{ packId, after }`, with `after: null` for the front.** It used to take the whole `packIds` array and renumber every row, which made a drag cost an upload and a write per pack in the library. Both ids are checked before the write: `pack_id` is a foreign key, so an unknown one would surface as a 500 rather than a 400, and an unknown neighbour would silently read as the front of the list. **That check names the two ids and nothing else** (`findKnownPackIds`), which it did not at first — validating against `listEmoticonPacks` left the request O(library) after the write had been made O(1), and that list is the expensive one: a `LEFT JOIN` fanning out every item of every pack to count them, a lateral join for the thumbnail and a `GROUP BY` over the result, run to answer whether two uuids exist. `PATCH` validates the same way for the same reason, and so do `POST` and `PUT` on `packs/{id}/items` — they asked `getEmoticonPack`, which is that same list narrowed to one pack **plus a second query reading every item in it**, to establish that an id names a row
- **Moves are serialized in the browser, one at a time, and the one-row write is what made that necessary.** The server bisects the neighbours **as its request finds them**, so two drags in flight at once both compute a midpoint against a list the other has not landed in: the screen is correct and a reload shows an order the user never asked for. The whole-list `PUT` above survived that race for free — every request carried a complete order, so the last one to arrive was self-contained — and a one-row write does not, which is a property the smaller contract gave up rather than one it kept. **A failure ends the chain rather than pausing it**: the rollback restores the list as it stood before the move that failed, so every write still queued behind it names a neighbour out of a list that no longer exists, and sending it would persist an order off a screen nobody is looking at. A drag made after the rollback is against the restored list and starts a fresh chain
- **이모티콘 묶음 검색 is the whole library — window-virtualized, cursor-paged, a name field above it, a switch per row and a chevron into the pack — and it reorders nothing at all**, per the bullet the split rests on. **A blank query is the browse state**, the library entire, and never a filter that matches nothing; the tab opens on it. The switches are held in the tab's own state rather than written into the query cache, because the cache key carries the word that was asked and an edit would reach the one page list on screen while every other word's cached answer kept the switch as it was before the tap
- **The open tab and the word in its field outlive a trip into a pack**, held in module scope as `ScrollMemory`'s offsets are: a pack is a route, so the chevron unmounted this screen and returning from it landed the reader on 사용중 — with the library's restored scroll offset under it. A reload starts on 사용중 again, which is the state the fallback is drawn for
- **The name match is `ILIKE` containment**, for § 8.6.'s reason — Korean rules out the morphological analysis a full-text index wants. **`%`, `_` and the backslash are escaped** (`toLikeLiteral`, the same helper § 13.9.1.'s keyword arms use): unescaped, a query of a single `%` answers with the entire library, which is the payload the whole split exists to avoid
- **The keyset cursor is the ordering key itself, `(effectivePackPosition(), packs.id)`, compared as one row value.** One expression for the `ORDER BY` and for the cursor, for the same reason the midpoint above is written from one expression: two spellings page past a row that sorts somewhere else. An offset would not survive this screen in any case — the other tab's drag rewrites those positions while the list is open. A page reads `limit + 1` rows and drops the extra, and whether it arrived is the whole of what `nextCursor` reports
- **The window is the _document's_, because the document is this screen's scroller** (`DESIGN.md § 3.3.`): `useWindowVirtualizer`, offset by a `scrollMargin` measured from the list's own top, since the header clearance, the tab strip and the field sit above it. Pointed at the `(main)` slot instead it would read a `scrollTop` that is permanently `0` off a plain flow container and window nothing at all — silently off rather than visibly broken. Nothing is measured: a row is `EMOTICON_PACK_ROW_HEIGHT` exactly, which is why the two rows share that constant and why neither may size to its content
- **Paging is keyed on `q`, `cursor` or `limit` being _present_, not on any of them holding a filter**, which is why the browse tab sends `q` even when it is blank. A request carrying none of the three answers `{ packs }` and the whole list, character for character what this route answered before the parameters existed; a paged one answers `{ packs, nextCursor }`. `?enabled=1` alone is **not** paging — that is 사용중's request, thirty-odd rows fetched whole to be dragged
- **The picker is deliberately not paged** (§ 13.6.), and that has to be said next to these parameters or it reads as an oversight. The panel asks whole-set **membership** questions no page can answer: 최근 사용 tests its stored ids against `visiblePackIds`, so a pack that was never fetched reads as _hidden_ rather than as absent, and the remembered-tab restore falls back to 최근 사용 whenever `findPack` misses. A bare `GET /api/emoticons/packs` therefore still returns the entire library
- **A switch thrown on 이모티콘 묶음 검색 is a write 사용중's server seed predates**, and the answer is a refresh on the way back rather than a broadcast: the flag is raised by the write and spent the next time that tab is looked at. The restored row arrives in its own remembered place — again because `enabled` was written and `position` was not
- **Hide** per pack, writing `user_emoticon_prefs.enabled` — hidden from that user's picker **tabs** and from 최근 사용, never deleted, the other user unaffected, and **still reachable by § 13.8.'s search** (§ 13.9.) and by 이모티콘 묶음 검색, which is where it is turned back on. It writes `enabled` and touches the position not at all. **"Hiding writes the whole list's current order first" is gone**, and the sparse key is what removed it: that workaround existed only because a positional `sort_order` put every row-having pack ahead of every row-less one, so writing the hidden pack's row alone reshuffled the list around it. With moved and untouched packs on one number line there is no order to record in order to avoid inventing one
- The management screen **owns the order**; the list component holds none of its own. Two copies meant a persisted drag could be rolled back by the next rename
- Reordering and hiding are **per-user by definition**, so neither broadcasts over `user_changed` — that channel carries `users` changes, and this writes a different table
- Drag-and-drop uses **`@dnd-kit`** (`core` + `sortable` + `modifiers`). Its `TouchSensor` `activationConstraint` (`delay` / `tolerance`) is what makes long-press-to-drag the activation gesture rather than a plain drag, which on a scrolling list would fight the scroller

### 13.6. Picker and Bubble ✅

**Picking and staging**

- The composer's emoticon toggle opens a panel of **three regions, stacked top to bottom**: a menu bar of `검색` / `이모티콘` / `미니`; then that menu's second region — 검색's field, or a one-row strip holding 최근 사용 and the **enabled packs of that kind in the user's order**, each showing the item § 13.2. resolved for it; then a vertically scrolling grid of cells. The grid is **4 columns for 검색 and 이모티콘 and 6 for 미니** (`EMOTICON_GRID_COLUMNS`, `MINI_GRID_COLUMNS`), and each count is written twice — the constant the arrows step by and the `grid-cols-*` class — which § 8.14. requires to agree
- **The strip moved above the grid to make room for the menu bar**, where it used to sit along the bottom by the thumb. What moved down in its place is the grid, which is the region actually being tapped; the two navigation rows now stack at the top and the cells take the rest of the panel
- **A menu is not stored. It is derived from the open tab** — a pack id already says which kind it is — so `ACTIVE_TAB_KEY` still holds one value and `useEmoticonPreload` still reads it unchanged. 미니's 최근 사용 is a third value under that key (`recents:mini`) rather than a second key, and a preload that knows nothing of menus falls through to the recents branch it already had. **Within one panel session each menu also remembers its own last tab**, in component state, so stepping across the bar and back returns to the pack rather than to 최근 사용
- **최근 사용 is one stored list per kind**, and 이모티콘's keeps the key it has always had so a device already holding recents goes on reading them. One list read through the open menu's kind is what this was: a run of minis then filled all `MAX_RECENTS` slots and 이모티콘's 최근 사용 came back empty, evicted by pictures it cannot draw at its own column count. **The kind is taken from the payload the send carried**, never from the menu the picture was picked in — § 6. puts a mini in `messages.text` as a fragment and an emoticon in `messages.emoticon_item_id`, and neither column can hold the other. **The picker applies no kind filter of its own on top of it**, or the cut lands twice: `packTypes` is empty until the pack summaries arrive, so the second one dropped every recent on the frame the panel opens
- **The panel takes pack summaries, and the open tab's items are a request of their own.** `GET /api/emoticons/packs` answers summaries and nothing else — `?items=1` is gone, and with it the 1.7MB that every room entry and every panel open used to pull. A tab is filled by `GET /api/emoticons/packs/{id}/items` — one pack, in § 13.4.'s shared authoring order — 최근 사용 resolves its stored ids through `GET /api/emoticons/items?ids=`, and 검색 is answered by the server (§ 13.9.1.). Nothing in the panel holds the library any more, which is what every rule below about the warm and the cap is now written against
- **The tab thumbnails are `loading="lazy"`, and the strip is why.** Each one is a § 13.3. asset request — a session check, a row read and a presign — and the 302 is cached for days, so it is one round trip per browser rather than per open. At two hundred packs that one time is two hundred round trips on the frame the panel first opens, for tabs most of which are scrolled off screen. The grid's cells carry it for the same reason
- Selecting an emoticon **stages it as a preview** above the composer, KakaoTalk-style, rather than sending on the tap. The preview autoplays an animated item with its sound, replays both on tap, and survives the panel closing
- **Tapping the same item twice sends it outright**, skipping the preview. The first tap of the pair still stages and still sounds. It is counted off `click` and an `A_SECOND / 3` window rather than `dblclick`, which never arrives on touch: `HapticTap` takes the tap on its own overlay and replays it as a scripted `control.click()`, which starts no double-click sequence
- **A mini is never staged. One tap puts it into the draft at the caret**, and there is no double tap to skip to a send — § 2.2. stores it as a fragment of a `text` message rather than in `messages.emoticon_item_id`, so a staged one would promise a send with no row to land in. The keyboard's `Enter`/`Space` and `⌘Enter` are the same insertion (§ 8.14.). **미니 takes no keyboard exemption for it**: the panel puts the keys away and leaves the composer up, so the draft stays visible and a run of minis costs no round trip — § 13.8.'s tab is exempt because that tab _itself_ asks for typing, which this one does not
- **The staged emoticon and staged attachments are mutually exclusive.** § 6. gives each bubble one payload, so a composer holding both would promise a send that has no row to land in
- **Emoticon and text are two messages, in that order**, when sent together. Nothing is added to the schema — `useSendMessage` delivers on one promise chain, so `messages.id` is assigned in queue order. Allowing `text` on an `emoticon` row was rejected: it would relax the § 6. CHECK for a combination `media` rows still could not express
- A **최근 사용** section, ahead of the first pack. It records at the **send, not the pick** — 최근 "사용" is what was actually sent. Recording at the pick also broke the double tap: `remember` moves the id to the front, the list re-sorts between the two taps, and the cell the second tap was aimed at has already been replaced by its neighbour
- **`POST /api/messages` checks the item exists** before inserting, like its `media` branch. `messages.emoticon_item_id` is a foreign key, and a picker whose list predates the other participant deleting an item would otherwise send its way into a `500` instead of a `400`
- **Every path that serves a message serves its emoticon map with it, and there are nine.** Enumerating the callers rather than trusting a list is what found the last three: `GET /api/messages/changed`, the stream's `change` event — § 8.13.1.'s reconciliation can put an emoticon into a row the window already holds — and the `POST` echo, so a sender's own row draws what the server resolved rather than what the composer happened to be holding. Each composes through `toMessagePayload`, so the way to forget the map is to write a route that does not use it. **The resolver answers a deleted item rather than filtering it**: its row and box outlive its objects precisely so a message holding it still knows how much space to leave (§ 8.3.). An id the page's map does not size draws the tombstone at a one-line square rather than nothing, and the bubble and § 8.3.'s estimate read that box from one function — so a page that ships without its map is visible instead of silent
- **§ 16.2.'s snapshot stores no map.** A mirror draws no emoticon and never did, and § 16.1.'s `(이모티콘)` needs nothing looked up — so the transcript alone is the whole payload
- **Sending validates three things and refuses rather than repairs** — the id count against `MAX_EMOTICON_ID_LOOKUP`, the pairing of placeholders to ids, and that every id names a row, which nothing else would catch since the column carries no foreign key (§ 6.). A deleted item passes: the picker cannot offer one, so that is a race with the other participant rather than a bad body. **`PATCH` takes the same three and had to** — an edit rewrites the text the placeholders sit in, so ids carried over from before the correction would stand at the wrong ones, and absent means none rather than "keep what was there"
- **The optimistic bubble carries the emoticons whole rather than their ids**, because ids alone cannot draw and the bubble has to reserve the box the echoed row will take. `startEdit` seeds a correction with them for the same reason, or the field opens on placeholders paired to nothing — blank boxes to read, and a body the check above refuses on submit. The outbox restore types the field optional, since a queue written by an earlier build carries none
- **The panel reopens on the tab it was last left on**, per device, via `synced-storage`'s `useStorageState`; it falls back to 최근 사용 when the remembered pack has since been deleted or hidden. The 최근 사용 list uses the same hook

**Panel geometry and animation**

- **The sheet rests at the keyboard's height, once one has been measured.** `VisualViewportSync` publishes `--keyboard-height` — the visual viewport's drop below its resting height while a field is focused, remembered in `localStorage` across launches — and the rest spacer is that less `--bottom-inset`, so the composer stands exactly where the keys put it and the toggle between the two moves nothing but what is under the composer. Until a keyboard has been seen the height below is the fallback
- **The fallback is half the shell tall, plus its menu bar and its handle row** (`--emoticon-sheet-handle-height`, outside the `min()` for the bar's reason), measured against `--viewport-height` rather than `vh` (`DESIGN.md § 3.4.`). A fixed `256px` was one and a half rows of a 4-column grid. The bar is added outside that half rather than taken out of it: the half is what the strip and the grid were measured at, and charging § 13.6.'s third region to it cost the grid a whole row on a 393px screen. **The cap widens to `min(560px, …)` from `md`** — a row at 800px holds more than a phone's, and the sheet that was three rows deep on a phone shows one there
- **검색 rests at `--emoticon-panel-height` like the other two menus.** It had a shorter cap of its own, `min(320px, viewport − headroom)`, to fit in what the keyboard left; with the keyboard covering the sheet (§ 13.8.) there is nothing to fit, and one rest height is what keeps the sheet from stepping when the menu bar is crossed
- **It opens and closes over 200ms, animating the `height` of the clipping strip** rather than mounting. The history follows because `--chat-bottom-gap` is measured from the composer's box every frame — one animation drives both, with no second scroll animation to keep in step. The strip is `justify-end`, so the sheet rises from the screen's bottom edge and the composer rides up on top of it. It stays mounted after its first open and is `inert` while closed, since a zero-height clip still holds tab stops
- **Not a `0fr`→`1fr` grid track.** Mid-transition Chrome sizes such a track's container taller than the track it resolved, and that difference is a gap that opens and shuts above the bottom-anchored sheet. One `--emoticon-sheet-height`, set on the strip, is what the strip and the sheet inside it are both drawn at, so the two cannot drift apart
- **The sheet stands under the composer, in the keyboard's slot — KakaoTalk mobile's current emoticon sheet.** It is the last row of the composer's wrapper: full shell width, `rounded-t-xl`, a top hairline, `bg-canvas`, a grab handle row, then the three regions. The composer rides on top of it and stays usable; focusing the field still closes it and the keyboard takes the slot. **The swap between the two moves the composer by nothing.** Both ways, the room holds the chat screen at its resting height (`data-keyboard-overlaid`, `DESIGN.md § 3.4.`) for the length of the swap: the toggle with the keys up opens the sheet under them at once and they slide off it, and the composer's field with the sheet up leaves it standing until the keys have risen over it, then closes it. The screen's 200ms ease and the strip's are the same curve from the same frame, so they cancel; the closing swap is keyed on the keyboard flag, with a timer behind it for a coarse pointer that raises no keys, and a fine pointer closes at once since none are coming Two boxes: an empty in-flow spacer that only ever moves `--bottom-inset` ↔ rest (`--emoticon-sheet-rest-height`) — the one thing `useComposerClearance` measures and `transitionend` listens to — and the drawn card, an `absolute … bottom-0 z-10` clip inside the wrapper that pays the inset back as padding. **The wrapper sits on the screen's edge and the spacer carries the inset**, rather than the wrapper being lifted by it: the keyboard flag steps `--bar-lift` the frame the keys are reported up or down, and with the inset beside the spacer's height that step raced the spacer's 200ms ease and the composer flinched by the inset at the end of every swap. Inside the height, the step changes nothing an open sheet is sized by, and a closed sheet eases it on the same curve the screen does. Above rest the card grows **over** the composer rather than lifting it, as KakaoTalk's expanded sheet does
- **Three sizes — closed, rest, expanded — and the handle moves between them (`useEmoticonSheet`).** Rest is the height above plus the handle. Expanded is measured in JS when the gesture starts and re-measured on resize while held, never a CSS token: the sheet's bottom to the `AppHeader`'s bottom (`APP_HEADER_ID`). The drag is taken on the card from any row that does not scroll vertically — the handle, the menu bar and the pack strip (`touch-none` on the first two; the strip keeps `touch-pan-x`, and a gesture whose larger travel at the slop is sideways is left to it) — while a press inside the grid is the grid's own scroll, **except the pull a grid has no scroll left for — down at `scrollTop === 0`, up at its bottom (or with nothing to scroll) short of expanded — which the sheet takes**, so the grid scrolls first and the sheet moves once it has run out. The hook refuses the browser's pan with a non-passive `touchmove` on the grid from the first move (`touch-pan-y` would otherwise `pointercancel` the drag at once, and React's `onTouchMove` is passive), only for the moves the sheet can take, so a sideways commit, or a vertical one the grid still has scroll for, gives the gesture back to the tab swipe or the scroll, and the grid never rubber-bands under a pull the sheet is following; past `GESTURE_SLOP` the pointer is captured and the height follows the finger with the transition off; a release settles only past a quarter of the height it started from (the hook's constant) — up from rest → expanded, down from expanded → rest, down from rest → closed — and a shorter pull returns the sheet to the size it had; a tap on the handle toggles rest ⇄ expanded, the `click` a drag ends in is swallowed on the card whatever it lands on, and the handle carries `aria-expanded` for the size it is at. **검색's field taking focus opens the sheet to expanded** on the same spring — the reader's own tap on it, or ⌃1, since arriving on the tab focuses nothing (§ 13.8.); leaving the tab afterwards leaves the size alone, and a pull down from expanded blurs whatever the sheet holds focused, so the keyboard leaves with the height. A close forgets the size, so reopening lands at rest. **The move up is a spring and nothing else is**: rest → expanded, while the sheet is open, runs `--ease-sheet` over `--duration-sheet-expand` (450ms) — a generated `linear()` list, ζ 0.74, ω 8.4 rad/s, peaking 3% over with no clamp, so the card overshoots ~14px under the header's glass and settles (`DESIGN.md § 4.7.2.`); expanded → rest, open and close keep the 200ms ease-out, and a drag is `transition-none`
- **The panel is promoted to its own layer** (`will-change: transform`) so the growing clip is a compositor crop; unpromoted, every frame repaints a grid of animated images against a moving clip rect
- **The staged preview is centered above the composer and floats over the history rather than pushing it** — positioned out of the composer's flow, so the strip `--chat-bottom-gap` reserves does not grow. Centered rather than aligned to the send side: against the right edge it read as a stray bubble already in the conversation. Glass with a shadow (§ 6.6.) precisely because it sits over them; the attachment tray keeps pushing, because a row of photos is not one floating object. It rides above the composer, at `bottom-full` of the wrapper — **clamped at the floating header by a `min()`**, because the wrapper is the composer plus the sheet's rest spacer and on a ~600px viewport that puts the unclamped preview under the header's glass. It is `z-20`, over the sheet's `z-10`, so it stays visible while the sheet is expanded over the composer. **Only the card takes taps**, never the full-width row that centers it: floating over the history means the band on either side of a 96px card would otherwise be a strip of history that cannot be touched

**Scroll pinning around the panel** — two cases beyond the per-frame `--chat-bottom-gap` follow, both of them WebKit handing the scroll offset to the compositor:

- **The pin is repeated once the strip's `transitionend` fires**, whichever way the panel was closed — the one moment the strip's height is final and nothing else is moving. Filtered on `propertyName === "height"` and on the strip being the event's own target, since the panel's transitions bubble through it
- **The post-send follow is a pin, not an animation.** `behavior: "smooth"` there was the case `transitionend` could not answer for: WebKit runs a smooth scroll against the content size it started with, so one still in flight 200ms later goes on steering toward the offset the _open_ panel implied and lands the history back where the panel had pushed it — overwriting every pin made underneath, the `transitionend` one included. It bought nothing either. A send at the live edge only has to cover the row it just added, one a § 8.6.1. jump replaced the window for animates over history the sender never asked to see, and every other follow in the room — an arrival, a re-measure, the clearance loop — is already instant. The § 6.7. pill keeps its animation, which is a journey the user asked for

**Panel navigation**

- **Picking from the expanded sheet collapses it to rest, and a stage waits `DOUBLE_TAP_WINDOW` before it does.** A 미니 insert collapses at once — the draft it landed in is under the sheet. A stage is the first tap of § 13.6.'s quick send, so collapsing on it would move the cell out from under the second tap; the collapse is therefore deferred by the window, and a quick send inside it cancels the pending collapse — the sheet stays expanded, since sending never resizes it
- **Sending does not close the panel** — KakaoTalk leaves it up and sending several in a row is the ordinary case. The composer therefore skips its post-send `focus()` while the panel is open, because that focus would raise the keyboard the panel may never share the screen with. The staged preview still clears
- **Tapping the history closes the panel; scrolling it does not.** The toggle and the field still close it too — and the toggle's close puts the caret in the field, so the keyboard rises into the slot the sheet leaves. **This reverses an earlier decision in part** — the panel used to survive both. What overturned it is the plain report: with the panel filling half the shell, touching the conversation reads as leaving the panel, and having it stay up reads as stuck. What survives of the old decision is its actual objection, which was never about the tap: dismissing runs the whole collapse — strip, clearance and re-pin — underneath a finger, and a finger that was only reaching for the history must not pay that. **So the two gestures are told apart rather than traded off**
- **The press is armed on `pointerdown` and settled on `pointerup`.** A gesture that travelled past `GESTURE_SLOP`, or that moved the scroller by any amount at all, was a scroll and closes nothing; only a press that did neither is a tap. `pointercancel` disarms, since that is how a touch that becomes a scroll usually ends — the scroll test then covers the engines that send a `pointerup` anyway. A finger laid on a coasting scroller arrests it and moves the offset a few pixels, which is why the offset is compared exactly rather than within a tolerance: stopping a scroll is not a tap either
- **There is no `wheel` listener, and the `scroll` event cannot be the signal.** A wheel _is_ the scroll, so it dismisses nothing. And opening the panel scrolls the history itself — the strip's growth re-pins it every frame — so a `scroll` listener would close the panel on the frame it opened
- **The listeners are on the room, not on the scroller**, and mounted only while the panel is on screen. An empty room renders no scroller at all, and that is the room where the panel covers the most nothing — bound to the scroller the rule simply would not hold there. The composer's own subtree is excluded by hand, since the panel is inside it and every tap on a cell reaches the room too
- **Swiping the grid left or right moves to the next or previous tab**, over the same order the tabs show — **within the open menu, never across menus**. The larger axis of the drag wins, so a mostly-vertical flick still scrolls the grid; the ends do not wrap; and the `click` the browser fires on whatever the drag started on is swallowed, or a swipe begun on a cell would also stage that emoticon. The incoming pack slides in from the side it came from — for a tab tap too, whose direction is the index it moved by, and for a menu step, whose direction is the menu bar's own order. 검색 has no tabs at all, so the swipe is not attached there: crossing menus by accident would lose the word the user is holding
- **Every tab change reads the arriving tab from its top**, the grid's offset dropped to `0` on each one — by the strip, the swipe, the menu bar or § 8.14.'s page turn alike. The scroller is one element across every tab, so left alone it carried whichever offset the tab before it was read at, and a pack entered halfway down is one whose first row was never seen
- **The tab strip scrolls the selected tab back into view**, by hand rather than with `scrollIntoView`, which walks _every_ scrollable ancestor: the clipping strip is `overflow: hidden`, and mid-collapse that counts as one, so a partially clipped panel would be scrolled inside its own clip and never come back. A small margin past the revealed tab leaves a sliver of its neighbour visible
- **The strip's horizontal inset is the first and last tab's margin, and must never be the container's `padding-inline`** (`DESIGN.md § 3.6.`). WebKit leaves `padding-right` out of `scrollWidth` until the content already overflows without it, so a padded strip has a dead band exactly that wide in which it is over-full, reports no overflow and cannot be scrolled at all — by hand, by the scroll-into-view above, or by the finger
- **The strip is also dragged by hand**, which needs `keepsScroll` on each tab's `HapticTarget` (`DESIGN.md § 7.15.1.`). The tabs tile the strip, so a finger reaching for it lands on an overlay and not on the scroller — and a native switch keeps a drag of its own before the scroller is ever consulted, which left the strip reachable only by tapping a tab or swiping the grid. Unlike the grid cells the tabs pass **no `overlayClassName`**: the axis an overlay must leave to the browser is the one its own scroller runs on, and here that is the horizontal one
- **The panel is never on screen with the keyboard**, with § 13.8.'s search tab as the one exemption. Reaching for the field closes it (`onFieldFocus`), and the render is additionally gated on `useIsVirtualKeyboardOpen` — **derived rather than an effect**, because Android reopens the keyboard on an already-focused field and fires no `focus` for the picker to hear
- **The composer stays in the stack while that exemption holds.** It used to stand down — hidden for as long as 검색's field held the keyboard, because the viewport the keys left was short enough that the preview's clamp drew it over the results — and the screen no longer shrinking for that keyboard (§ 13.8.) is what took the reason away. A draft is visible and sendable throughout, which is § 13.8.'s two-bubble send back
- **The toggle puts the keyboard away itself.** That gate means a press made while the field is focused only sets a flag the panel may not act on, and iOS lowers the keyboard for a blur alone — a tap on a `<button>` is not one — so the press read as doing nothing. The composer blurs the field before it reports the toggle. The toggle is also inverted against **what is on screen** rather than the flag behind it: while the keyboard suppresses the panel the flag can already be `true`, and inverting that closes a panel the user is asking to open

**The tab strip does not survive a large library, and virtualizing it is not the answer.** The payload above was cut for a few hundred packs and holds at ten thousand; the strip does not, and the reason is not rendering cost. A horizontal row of icons is a browsing surface that works because the eye can cross it — at two hundred tabs it is a scroller nobody reaches the end of, and at ten thousand it is one nobody starts. Adding a windowed renderer would make that strip cheap to draw and no more usable, so **this is deliberately unbuilt**: the answer at that scale is a search-first navigation model, which is a redesign of § 13.6. rather than an optimisation of it, and § 13.8.'s field is the piece of it that already exists.

**Bubble and sound**

- The bubble renders **the image alone** — no bubble, no border, no background, max 140×140 (`DESIGN.md § 6.5.`). The box is reserved from the stored `width`/`height` before the asset loads (§ 8.3.)
- **A `text` message's own emoticons (§ 6.) draw two ways, decided by its content**: one emoticon and no words draws large and bubble-less, anything else draws inline at text size. Read off the segments, so whitespace either side of a lone emoticon does not make it inline. **The lone case takes `toSoloEmoticonBox`, `toEmoticonBox`'s own arithmetic at a smaller 100px ceiling** — the scale is still capped at 1, so a picture authored small draws at its own pixels, and the lower ceiling is what keeps a solo mini from drawing at the same size as a real emoticon message despite sharing its bubble-less layout. A mini is authored to stand one line tall and upscaling one is guaranteed blur, and a rule that upscaled only minis would render the same asset at two sizes depending on which pack it came from
- **An inline one is `1lh` tall with the ratio doing the width, and cannot grow the line box it sits in.** `InlineEmoticon` is in `shared/ui`, so the composer's draft and the bubble it is sent as are one declaration of that box. Nothing measures the line and nothing waits for the asset, so the box is identical before and after the image lands — which is what keeps a load from re-wrapping the text around it, and what lets § 8.3. price a line without knowing whether an emoticon is on it
- **A deleted item keeps its row and therefore its box**, so what stands in its place occupies exactly what the picture did and § 8.3. re-measures nothing when one appears — `MediaTombstone`'s own principle, and § 6.'s "a deleted row keeps its geometry on purpose" one table over. Inline it is **icon only**: one line is a box no sentence fits in
- **An animated item plays because the browser plays it, and replays because `useViewportReplay` (`shared/lib`) does.** With one image slot nothing swaps sources — a GIF or animated WebP just runs its own loop from load. The virtualizer's own unmount at the window's edge used to be the only thing that ever restarted one, and overscan keeps a row mounted well past the edge of what is actually visible, so a bubble scrolled a screen away and back kept whatever frame it had stopped on. `useViewportReplay` wraps `react-intersection-observer`'s `useInView` on the bubble's own box, independent of the virtualizer's mount boundary, and bumps a replay token — the same remount a tap already does — every time the box re-enters the viewport, silently. It does not bump on the mount that is already mid-first-loop from load, only on a later re-entry. **The token also rides the asset URL (`toReplaySrc`), not only the `<img>`'s React key** — iOS Safari, including an installed PWA, ties a GIF/WebP/APNG's running loop to the request URL rather than to the element, so a fresh element pointed at an unchanged `src` restarts nothing there. The reused URL still answers from the browser's own cache (§ 13.3.'s year-long immutable presign), so a replay costs only the asset route's redirect, not a re-download
- **A sound emoticon sounds at KakaoTalk's four moments, and at no other**: selecting it in the picker, sending it, receiving it live while the room is on screen, and tapping a bubble. Everything else — scrolling past a bubble, the reconnect replay, the catch-up fetch, a page load into existing history — is **silent**, because the sound announces the emoticon _arriving_, not being on screen
- **The live arrival is told apart on the wire**, not guessed from timestamps: § 8.4. emits a replay as `event: backfill`. Deduplication is the second guard — a message the window already holds is not an arrival however it was delivered
- **A message with no emoticon sound gets a 전송음 instead** — a soft sine blip, gliding up on a send and down on a live arrival, from `public/sounds/` through the same shared player and under the same four-moment rule, so the replay, the catch-up and a backgrounded tab (already announced by § 16.1.'s push) stay silent. It is rendered to peak at about −28 dBFS on purpose, since `audio.volume` is ignored on iOS and the file is the only place its level can be set — it has to sit under a max media volume without grating. Each file is read once into an object URL by the shared player's own cache, which every sound now goes through. **An emoticon's own sound wins**: one submit sounds once, and an emoticon that sounded in it is that sound — and the 전송음 is `"secondary"` at the player, so it also stays silent while an _earlier_ message's emoticon is still sounding, which is a case no per-submit rule could reach. Per device and on by default, from Settings' 전송음 row (`useMessageSound`, `localStorage`); silent mode cannot be detected on either platform, so a switch is the whole of the control
- **My own emoticon sounds at the send, not at the echo.** A sound arriving on the round trip lands a beat late and can land twice. It is played from the optimistic row rather than inside the tap, through the same one-row flag a live arrival takes (below), keyed by `client_msg_id`; the shared player is already unlocked by the room's first gesture, which is what lets that play start outside one
- **One shared `AudioContext` plays every sound**, minted once in `shared/lib`. A per-bubble `<audio>` cannot be started by an arrival — iOS refuses to start audio outside a user gesture, and a freshly mounted element has never been in one — whereas a single context resumed by any gesture in the room (`useSoundUnlock`, armed on **every** gesture, since a phone call or a spell in the background leaves it `interrupted`) plays for the rest of the session. It also makes two emoticons in a row cut over rather than overlap. **Web Audio rather than an `<audio>` element because of the clock**: an element reports that it started, a frame or more after it did and with the output device's own delay still ahead, where a context takes a start time on its own clock and states that delay (`baseLatency` + `outputLatency`, `getSoundLatency`) — which is what the bullet two below is built on
- **A sound is warmed like a picture is, and that is what lines the two up.** An emoticon's image is fetched and decoded tabs ahead of being drawn (below), where its sound was fetched at the tap that played it — so the picture was instant and the sound landed a round trip later, on the same tap. The shared player keeps its sources as decoded `AudioBuffer`s under one byte budget (`MAX_CACHED_BYTES`, counted in PCM), evicting least-recently-**played** so a tab's warm cannot throw out a sound the reader keeps tapping, and fetching through the same four-at-a-time ceiling the image warm holds itself to. `playSound` on a miss decodes first and starts when it can, so that one play lands late; the warm ahead is what keeps a miss rare, and `warmSound` is called ahead by the three screens that hold a sounding emoticon: a bubble in the room, a tappable solo mini, and **the open picker tab only** (`warmEmoticonSounds`) — never the tabs either side of it, since a swipe reveals pictures and only a tap asks for a sound
- **A picture and its sound start on one frame, and both are placed rather than observed.** An animated image's clock starts on the first paint after a renderer takes it — WebKit on attach, Blink and Gecko on first draw, none on `decode()` — which a caller can place; and the sound is scheduled on the audio clock (`playSound`'s `delayMs`), whose distance to the speaker the context states (`getSoundLatency`). `useSyncedEmoticonPlayback` is that for every moment that sounds: it decodes the picture off-DOM from a Blob it keeps beside the sound cache (`warmAnimatedImage`), on a **fresh object URL per play** since WebKit keeps the clock on the URL shared by every element showing it, and inside a frame callback schedules the sound to be heard on that frame's paint (`A_FRAME` less the latency), mounts that very element and commits the reveal through `flushSync`. Where the output is slower than a frame — Bluetooth — the sound starts at once and the picture waits the frames it takes to catch up.
