A single `WHERE (keyword ILIKE '%term%' OR keyword = ANY(…))` is planned as one predicate over one relation, so the arm the planner cannot index takes the other arm's index down with it — the btree the reverse direction exists to use went unused on every search, which is the whole of what made that direction cheap. Two selects unioned are two plan nodes, and each gets the index it was written for
- **The forward direction is one arm _per term_, and the same rule is why.** The second cut split the two directions and then left every term `OR`ed together inside the forward one, which is the identical failure one level down: a single two-character term in the field took the trigram index away from `고민` and from every other well-formed term beside it. `MIN_KEYWORD_LENGTH` is two, so such a term is an ordinary query rather than an edge case, and § 13.9.'s 따라하기 fills the field with up to `MAX_EMOTICON_KEYWORDS` words at once. **The reverse arm stays single** and is not split per term: a btree equality is exact at any length, so one shared `= ANY` over the union of every term's substrings costs no term its index and reads the btree once instead of twelve times
- **The reverse direction is inverted rather than approximated, and the bounds are what make it exact.** Read the other way it asks whether any stored keyword _equals_ a substring of the term, and the term is a word somebody typed. So its substrings are enumerated from `MIN_KEYWORD_LENGTH` to `MAX_EMOTICON_KEYWORD_LENGTH` and probed with one equality (`keyword = ANY(…)`, which Postgres answers off the btree in a single scan whatever the list's length). **Both bounds are enforced by the trigger and the backfill**, not by `normalizeKeywords` — that function guards only the two write paths in `write-emoticon-item.ts`, so a row older than the rule is exactly the one that can sit outside either end, and the upper bound was missing from the index for the same reason the lower one had to be added to it. A keyword contained in the term is therefore **necessarily** one of the substrings enumerated. This is an identity, not a heuristic — and **widening either bound breaks it silently rather than breaking the query**: a one-character keyword would be missed in the direction that matches most widely, and one past twenty would be missed for any term long enough to hold it
- **The term is bounded too, and that is where the identity stops.** Nothing caps a single term: `splitKeywordQuery` splits on commas, so a paste with none in it arrives as one 264-character term (`MAX_KEYWORD_QUERY_LENGTH`) and enumerating it whole is some 4,800 bind parameters per debounced keystroke — 따라하기's twelve twenty-character words are already 2,280. So the probe reads at most `MAX_EMOTICON_KEYWORD_LENGTH` characters of a term, which puts the worst case at `MAX_EMOTICON_KEYWORDS` × 190 whatever is pasted. **What that gives up is stated rather than implied**: a keyword sitting past the twentieth character of such a term is not reached by this direction. A term that long is not a word with a particle on it, which is the case the direction exists for, and the forward arm is unaffected
- **`%`, `_` and the backslash are escaped, exactly as § 8.6.'s message search escapes them.** Not hygiene — unescaped, a query of `%` matches every keyword in the library and one of `_` matches every keyword of that length. The user typed characters, not a pattern
- **The ladder is unchanged and is still the single definition of relevance**: `6` a keyword equal to the term, `4` a keyword containing it (`민` → `고민`), `3` the term containing a keyword — the particle direction, which matches far more widely. Summed per term, so sharing several of the tapped emoticon's words beats sharing one very well. **It is deliberately not reimplemented in SQL.** Two definitions of "related" that drift is the expensive failure; a JS pass over a bounded page is not. Its zero is also the check that the SQL candidate set and the ladder still agree — a candidate the ladder scores at zero is dropped rather than ranked last
- **The candidate cap is applied _before_ the ranking, and that is a known compromise.** `EMOTICON_SEARCH_CANDIDATE_LIMIT` (5000) is what the server reads out of the database, in `item_id` order — which has nothing to do with relevance, so a query matching more than that can lose a highly relevant item to one that merely sorts earlier. The client-side pass this replaced could not, because it held the whole library — losing that property is the price of not sending it. The cap is unreachable at the few hundred packs this ships with and is left standing as the ceiling that keeps the query bounded at ten thousand, where the answer is an approximate sort in SQL rather than a larger number here. The ordering is there at all so two identical searches answer identically, which an unordered `LIMIT` does not
- **The cap sits on the candidate select and not on the items read from it**, which is the second thing the first cut got wrong. Applied downstream it bounded the rows the candidates produced while the candidate set itself stayed unbounded, so the number named a page size and bounded nothing the query had to do. On the `UNION` it bounds what the semi-join and the ranking are handed. **What it does not bound is the scan below it**: a `UNION` must read both arms out before it can deduplicate, so the cap limits the rows that leave the candidate select rather than the rows it reads to produce them — which is the bullet below
- **`EMOTICON_SEARCH_PAGE_SIZE` (30) is what leaves the server, and the two numbers must not be confused.** § 13.8. draws the results as one row that scrolls sideways, so thirty is a scroll's worth rather than a page anybody reaches the end of. Sending candidates to the browser instead would restore the payload § 13.6. exists to have removed
- **The field is debounced, and Korean is why the interval is what it is.** A Hangul field commits a jamo at a time — `고민` is typed as `ㄱ`, `고`, `곰`, `고미`, `고민` — so an undebounced field asks five times for one word. The row keeps the previous word's answer while the next is fetched, or it flickers once per character on the app's narrowest surface
- **The Hangul-shape and QWERTY rungs are gone.** 초성 검색 (`ㄱㅁ` → `고민`), the half-typed syllable (`고ㅁ` → `고민`) and the IME slip (`rhals` → `고민`) were decompositions of the **keyword**, which is a thing only an in-memory pass over the whole library can afford. None of them survives translation into an indexable predicate, and running them over a candidate page instead ranks on a rung the page was not selected by — items the aid would have found are simply not in the page to rank. Restoring any of them means an index that stores the decomposition, which is a schema change and a second trigger, not a function. `es-hangul` stays a dependency for `josa` alone (`CLAUDE.md § 0.4.`); `convertQwertyToHangul`, `disassemble` and `getChoseong` are no longer imported anywhere
- **A term under three characters cannot use the trigram index, and a two-syllable Korean word is the modal query.** `pg_trgm` extracts no trigram from a `LIKE '%..%'` pattern that short, so that term's arm becomes a full pass over `emoticon_keywords` with the `ILIKE` rechecked on every row — whether the planner reaches that as a sequential scan or as a GIN scan matching everything is a plan detail, not a difference in what is read. **It is that term's arm and no other's**, which is what splitting the forward direction per term bought; before it, one short word made a full scan of the whole field. The reverse arm is untouched, since a btree equality is exact at any length. **The cap does not help here** (bullet above): it bounds the rows the candidate select emits, not the rows it reads
- **A full scan is reachable for a well-formed long term as well, and the planner is the reason rather than the pattern.** Measured on 60,003 keyword rows: a term matching 2,484 of them was planned as a sequential scan because the GIN scan plus the recheck costed higher than reading the table. So the split above bounds the _blast radius_ of a short term and does not promise that every long term is an index scan — the index is available to it, and whether it is used is a cost decision that moves with selectivity and table size. What the split guarantees is that one term's plan is no longer every term's plan
- **At today's size that is accepted, and the scale at which it stops being acceptable is stated rather than left implied.** A few hundred packs is on the order of ten thousand items and tens of thousands of keyword rows — short text, well under a megabyte, a low single-digit millisecond pass once per debounced keystroke, and § 8.6. already accepts the same limit in message search over a larger table. It stops being acceptable in the low hundreds of thousands of rows, and the ten-thousand-pack target this search was built for (≈400,000 items, ≈1.6M keyword rows) is an order of magnitude past that: a two-character term there is a full scan of the index per keystroke, per term. The answer at that point is an index that can answer a two-character pattern — `pg_bigm`, or a stored decomposition of the keyword — and **not** a larger `EMOTICON_SEARCH_CANDIDATE_LIMIT`, which bounds the wrong half
- **A mini's keyword never enters `emoticon_keywords`, and it is kept out at the index rather than at the readers.** `sync_emoticon_keywords()` carries a pack-type condition (`0045`), so a name a mini is managed under is not a word this search can answer. Both repositories query that table, so a filter written into the queries instead would be exactly the two copies `0026` chose a trigger to avoid. **The migration rebuilds the table in full** rather than deleting the mini rows, so it is provably level with the function as written whatever it held before — and the condition rests on a pack's kind being immutable (§ 6.), since nothing on `emoticon_packs` fires this trigger and a statement that changed one would strand these rows
- **The freshly built table is `ANALYZE`d by the migration that fills it.** A table created and backfilled inside one migration has `relpages = 0` until autovacuum reaches it, so the planner costs both arms off its defaults — a stretch of wrong plans beginning at the moment of deploy, on the search's hottest path. It was reached in practice: `EXPLAIN` was meaningless against the backfilled table until it had been analyzed. `ANALYZE` is transactional, unlike `VACUUM`, so it is a last statement rather than a runbook step
- **The ladder folds each candidate's keywords once, not once per term.** `toKeywordRelevance` scores up to `EMOTICON_SEARCH_CANDIDATE_LIMIT` items against up to `MAX_EMOTICON_KEYWORDS` terms, so folding inside the per-term loop repeated the same `toLowerCase` twelve times over every keyword of every candidate — the same answer for eleven twelfths more work

#### 13.9.2. User preference ranking boosts frequently used emoticons ✅

- **Up to `MAX_EMOTICON_SEARCH_USER_MATCHES` (3) of the user's most frequently sent emoticons are boosted to the top of search results.** `user_emoticon_usage` records `(user_id, item_id, use_count, last_used_at)` maintained by a trigger on `messages` (`0046`).
- **A minimum relevance threshold (`MIN_USER_BOOST_RELEVANCE = 4`) is required for the boost.** Only exact matches (`6`) or strong keyword containment (`4`) are eligible for the top-3 boost. Loosely matching items (e.g. term holding keyword `3`) cannot hijack the top ranks merely due to high `use_count` and are ranked naturally in the general pool instead.
- **Within the boosted tier, items are sorted by `relevance DESC`, then `use_count DESC`, then `last_used_at DESC`.** The remaining results are filled from the general candidate pool up to `EMOTICON_SEARCH_PAGE_SIZE` (30).

---

## 14. Security and Index Blocking ✅

**Landed:** `app/robots.ts` (`Disallow: /` for every agent), root layout `robots: { index: false, follow: false, nocache: true }`, `X-Robots-Tag: noindex, nofollow, noarchive` on every path, and `Strict-Transport-Security` / `X-Content-Type-Options` / `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` / `X-Frame-Options: DENY`. **Do not generate a sitemap.** Proxy-matcher exclusions are listed in § 7.

- Upload MIME/extension allow-list and a size cap, enforced at registration against **both** objects of the § 9. pair. **§ 9.1. widens the type rule deliberately and only for a file attachment**: anything shaped like a mime is stored, and the compensating control is that such a row is served only as a `Content-Disposition: attachment` download, from R2's own origin, with the name sanitized into the header. The `_thumb` sibling is checked as strictly as the original (`image/jpeg`, `MAX_THUMBNAIL_SIZE`): it is what every chat cell, grid tile and video poster loads, so leaving it unchecked is the same hole by another name
- `POST /api/messages` verifies every upload it is handed against the key prefix — **the sender's own id and the `chat` scope** (§ 9.) — and registers it in the transaction that attaches it. Nothing else stopped one user hanging the other's objects off their own bubble, and the scope half is what keeps an avatar out of a message (§ 12.)
- **Registration is still the only place the type and size limits hold**, and § 9.'s reservation does not move that: a claim is about a key, so a reserved key says nothing about what R2 was handed. It is a new **row-creating** surface, though — an authenticated `upload-url` call writes a `storage_reservations` row whether or not any bytes follow — and the argument below is what covers it: two admitted users, and the rows expire on their own
- **No endpoint is rate-limited, and none needs to be while `ALLOWED_EMAILS` admits two people.** Widening it is what puts limiting back on the table

**The audited sweep**

- Every API route validates the session (401 when unauthenticated). Audited across every exported method, not every file — a route with two verbs can check one and not the other. The four **auth** routes are the deliberate exceptions and the only ones: `login/google`, `callback/google` and `logout` cannot require what they exist to establish or clear, and `login/dev` answers a **bodiless 404** before anything else unless `IS_DEV_LOGIN_ENABLED`, which is `NODE_ENV` and therefore compile-time. That 404 is deliberately not `apiError("not_found")` — a body would confirm the endpoint exists
- Error responses must not leak internal details — no stack, no driver message, no key or path. One helper the routes answer through, applied over the same sweep as the item above: `apiError(code)` in `shared/api`, the sole owner of both the shape (`{ error: code }`, nothing else) and the code→status table. All 109 error responses across the 27 routes that raise one go through it. **A new error is a case added to that table**, never a `NextResponse.json({ error })` inlined beside one — an assembled message is where a driver string or a storage key gets out, and Next's own Route Handler guide reaches for `reason.message` at exactly that spot. `unsupported_type` and `unsupported_media` collapsed into one: two names for a single 415, and no client reads the code at all — every caller branches on the status

---

## 15. Deployment ✅

**Landed:** `maxDuration = 300` on the SSE route segment (§ 8.4.), and the **R2 bucket setup** — private with public access disabled, plus a CORS policy allowing `PUT` and `GET` from the app origin. Without `PUT` every § 9. upload fails preflight; without `GET` the § 10. share route cannot buffer an original; no code change can fix either. `AllowedHeaders` is `Content-Type` only, so no upload may send a second header without this policy changing first (§ 9.).

- The Vercel project is connected to the GitHub repository, the custom domain `jandh.jeheecheon.com` resolves over automatic HTTPS, and every environment variable below is set on it — `DATABASE_URL`, `DATABASE_URL_UNPOOLED`, `GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID`, `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET`, `ALLOWED_EMAILS`, `RELATIONSHIP_START_DATE` (ISO `YYYY-MM-DD`), `R2_ACCOUNT_ID`, `R2_ACCESS_KEY_ID`, `R2_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`, `R2_BUCKET`, `APP_URLS`, `NEXT_PUBLIC_EMOTICONS_ORIGIN` (`https://jandh-emoticons.jeheecheon.com`, **production only** — bare origin with no `/emoticons`, a trailing slash being stripped for it; unset it falls back to that repo's dev server, § 13.7. **Renamed from `EMOTICONS_ORIGIN`**, which must be deleted from the project rather than left beside it: the old name is read by nothing and would sit there looking authoritative), `SESSION_COOKIE_DOMAIN` (`.jeheecheon.com`, **production only**, and the same value on jandh-emoticons — § 5.2.1.), `NEXT_PUBLIC_VAPID_PUBLIC_KEY`, `VAPID_PRIVATE_KEY`, `VAPID_SUBJECT`, `OPS_GITHUB_TOKEN` (§ 12.4. — a fine-grained PAT with `Actions: Read and write` on this repository, and the only thing the three dispatching controls need; unset, they are hidden), `DATA_GO_KR_SERVICE_KEY` (§ 11.7., and the bullet below). This list is the one to check against when a new variable is introduced
- **`DATA_GO_KR_SERVICE_KEY` joined that list when § 11.7. moved to the API**, and it is the one variable there that expires on its own: data.go.kr grants a key a two-year **활용기간**, after which the gateway refuses every call. Missing or lapsed, the calendar keeps drawing `holiday-table.ts` and says so in the log (§ 11.7.) — so nothing breaks, and nothing announces it either. It is no longer a GitHub repository secret and there is no workflow to hold one
- **§ 9.'s reclaim adds no variable here and one on jandh-ops.** `STORAGE_RESERVATION_TTL` and `MEDIA_DELETE_GRACE` are constants in `shared/config`, deliberately not environment: they are invariants two deployments have to agree on (§ 13.7.1.), not a knob. jandh-ops gains **`PURGE_GRACE`** (default `5m`) beside its `ORPHAN_*` pair — and it is that service's availability, not a value in this project, that now decides when deleted bytes actually go. A `/api/purge` cron that is never attached leaves every soft-deleted object in the bucket forever, with nothing in this app failing
- **One variable lives on the other project and is this app's to check anyway:** jandh-emoticons sets `CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGIN=https://jandh.jeheecheon.com`, which is the origin its keyword route names in `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` (§ 13.8.1.). Unset there, it defaults to `http://localhost:3000` and every suggestion request from this deployment is refused by the browser. **jandh-emoticons deploys first** — until it does, the direct call has no CORS to answer it
- `pnpm build` runs `lint:steiger` ahead of `next build`, so an architecture violation fails the deploy
- **One deployed database.** Local work runs against the § 1. docker compose Postgres, so it has a single writer

### 15.1. Refreshing a client across a deploy ✅

An installed iOS PWA is not reloaded when the user reopens it — the system restores the suspended process, so a phone last opened before a deploy keeps running the old bundle indefinitely. This is **not a caching problem**: `sw.js` registers no `fetch` handler and caches nothing (§ 16.1.), so there is no stale cache to invalidate, only a stale process to replace.

**Landed:**

- The server identifies its deployment with `BUILD_ID` — `VERCEL_DEPLOYMENT_ID`, falling back to `VERCEL_GIT_COMMIT_SHA`, then to `development`
- `/api/chat/stream` emits `event: build` once per connection, ahead of the replay, carrying no `id:`
- The client compares each `build` against the **first one it saw**, never against a value baked into the bundle: a browser bundle cannot read a non-`NEXT_PUBLIC_` variable, so the two sides would always disagree
- The stream already reconnects on every iOS resume (§ 8.4.), so the signal arrives exactly when a suspended client wakes. No poll, no second connection
- A detected deploy reloads immediately **unless** the screen holds unsent work — composer text, staged attachments, a staged emoticon, a send in flight, or a bulk emoticon add still landing. Those register through `useUnsentWork`, and the reload retries every `APP_REFRESH_RETRY_DELAY` and on every backgrounding until they clear
- **A bulk add counts even though nothing on screen would be lost.** The uploads are a plain promise chain, so routing away leaves them running — but a document reload kills them mid-pile, and unlike a draft there is no staged state to resume from. Because the batch outlives the screen, the hold is taken by `addEmoticonsFromFiles` itself through `holdUnsentWork` and released in a `finally`: `useUnsentWork` drops its probe at unmount, so a screen-scoped guard would expire at exactly the moment it was written to cover
- **A _failed_ send is not unsent work.** It stays in the pending list until the user retries or cancels, and never resolves on its own — counting it pins a user who sent a photo on a dead connection to a stale bundle indefinitely. Only `sending` holds the reload
- Settings carries a manual `강제 새로고침` row on **development builds only**, since `BUILD_ID` is constant locally
- **Vercel Skew Protection is part of this section and belongs to the project, not to `src/`.** It keeps a superseded deployment's chunks reachable, so a client that has not refreshed yet fails softly instead of 404-ing on a missing chunk — the gap the signal above cannot close on its own, since a suspended client can request a chunk before it has read a `build` event. Nothing in the repository can assert it, so it is re-checked against the project settings alongside § 15.'s environment list whenever the Vercel project is recreated

---

## 16. Deferred at the Start, Landed Since ✅

Everything filed here was scoped out of the first pass and has since shipped. The heading is kept rather than merged into the sections above because `src/` comments cite these numbers (§ 1.).

- Dark theme — the `.dark` block in `theme.css` carries the full hand-tuned palette (DESIGN.md § 5.4.), `forcedTheme` is gone, and § 12.'s row is rendered. **`system` is the default**, not `light`. No `dark:` utility exists anywhere in `src/` and none may be added — the tokens swap underneath the same utility, which is the whole point of § 5.1.'s five years of discipline
- Offline caching (the caching role of a service worker — separate from push). **Two kinds of thing may enter the cache and nothing else**: build output whose URL carries a content hash (`/_next/static/` — and **only** that prefix), and a document that holds no user data — `/offline`, and § 16.2.'s `/offline/shell` beside it. `/icons/` is deliberately **not** cached, despite looking like a sibling: `pnpm icons` regenerates `icon-192.png` and the splash set **in place**, under fixed names, so a cache-first entry would serve last year's artwork to every installed client until `CACHE_NAME` happened to move — and nothing ties the two together. **The rule is not freshness, it is that `caches` is scoped to the origin rather than the session** — § 16.1. already has two accounts sharing one browser, and logging out clears the cookie, not the Cache Storage. Every `(main)` document is rendered through `requireUserOrRedirect()` (§ 6.4.), so **the HTML _is_ user data** and no route inside that group can ever be cached; `/offline` and § 16.2.'s mirror are separate routes that hold none, which is the whole of what makes them cacheable. **The test is the document, not the screen it grows into** — a mirror is admissible because its HTML is empty of the user and fills itself from IndexedDB after mount, which is storage the session can key and clear where `caches` is not. A hashed URL is safe for the opposite reason: changed bytes get a new URL, so a hit can never be the previous deploy's — this is the answer to the "why am I looking at last week's build" objection that kept § 7. worker-free. `/api/*` is never cached and never intercepted at all: `resolveHandler` returns `null` rather than passing through to `fetch()`, because `/api/chat/stream` is an SSE body held open for the life of the tab (§ 8.4.) and routing one through the worker is how it gets buffered or cut. Cross-origin GETs are dropped the same way — the worker controls the whole origin, so R2's presigned, expiring URLs (§ 9.) reach it too. **A worker's own script is dropped before every other test** (`request.destination` is `worker` or `sharedworker`): a `Response` taken from `caches` carries the _cached entry's_ URL and the browser makes that the worker's `location`, which drops the `#params=…` fragment Turbopack hangs a bundled worker's chunk list on — the worker aborts with `Missing worker bootstrap config`, and § 9.'s AVIF and video encodes fall back to the main thread, silently, which is a console error on desktop and a killed tab on iOS. **The precache is one `cache.add` with its rejection swallowed, never `cache.addAll`**: a rejected `waitUntil` fails the install, an unactivated worker rejects `register()`, and § 16.1. loses the push subscription with it — surfacing only as the Settings toggle sitting empty, which is the failure this section must not manufacture. `/offline` is `force-static` and its one control is a bare `<a>` rather than a reload handler or a client `Link`, since the screen is shown exactly when a chunk fetch can fail. **The declaration came out while § 1.1.'s flag was on — which rejects it — and went back when the flag did**; either way the guarantee is visible in the build's own summary, where this route must read `○`. It is excluded from `proxy.ts`'s matcher alongside `sw.js`, or the fallback would redirect to a `/login` that is equally unreachable. `CACHE_NAME` carries the version and `activate` deletes every other one, which is the only thing that evicts a stale fallback page. **Outside production the worker caches nothing at all** — `?nocache=1` on the registration URL turns the whole of it off, `resolveHandler` then answers no `fetch`, and `activate` clears `CACHE_NAME` too so a browser holding a stale dev bundle heals itself. The reason is the hashed-URL premise above: `next dev` reuses stable chunk names, so the worker pins the first bundle it ever saw and serves it over every edit, which presents as HMR having silently stopped. The flag rides on the URL because a worker is served raw from `public/` and can read no environment of its own — `sw.js` and `push-registration.ts` have to move together — and it makes the dev and production script URLs differ, so their registrations can never share a state. Push is unaffected either way, since it never touches the cache

### 16.1. Push Notifications ✅

**Landed.** This reverses the "no service worker" decision in § 7.

- **Every push produces a banner. No exceptions.** `userVisibleOnly` is a promise the platform audits: WebKit revokes the subscription after **three** pushes whose worker showed nothing, and Chrome substitutes its own "site updated in the background" banner before doing the same. The symptom is not an error anywhere — it is the Settings toggle found empty on the next launch, push dead until the user re-subscribes, and a fresh `push_subscriptions` row each time they do. `sw.js` therefore **MUST** call `showNotification` on every `push` event, and **MUST NOT** test window visibility to decide whether to. **The count never resets** — `webpushd`'s `PushDatabase` only ever increments it — so three misses across the whole life of a subscription retire it, however many banners landed in between. A miss is a push the worker fetched that reached no successful `showNotification` within 30 s, or a `push` event whose `waitUntil` promise rejected; so `handlePush` shows the banner **first, with nothing awaited before it**, and the badge and unread message that follow are wrapped so they can neither delay nor reject it
- **One alerting channel, not two.** The obvious way to keep the banner from duplicating an on-screen message is to give the app its own in-app notice and let the worker stand down while a window is visible. That is the shape that lost the subscription, and **no variant of it is safe** — closing the banner from inside the push event reads to the platform as never having shown one. So the in-app channel is gone instead: no toast, no chime, no `shared/sound` alert. SSE (§ 8.4.) delivers the message and moves the badge; alerting is the OS notification's job alone. **A message that arrives while the recipient is looking at the conversation still raises a banner** — that is the cost of keeping push alive
- A consequence worth stating: with push off, unsupported, or blocked, an open app **alerts nothing at all**. The message still arrives and the badge still moves. The Settings toggle is the only alerting switch in the app
- **Sent at INSERT time, from the request that inserted.** `POST /api/messages` dispatches inside `after()`, so the fan-out never sits between the sender and their 201. This is why push costs Neon's autosuspend **nothing** — the compute is already awake. Nothing polls, nothing is scheduled, no process is resident
- **Two triggers, and no third.** "New message arrived" is this section's; § 16.3.'s reminders are the other. A morning digest is still **excluded**. Reminders were excluded here too and the record of why is worth keeping, because half of it expired rather than being argued away: the compute is no longer Neon's and § 12.4.'s jobs already reach it every ten minutes, so the polling objection is a cost this deployment pays regardless, and the second deployment target it would have needed was unwound when jandh-ops was. The two that stood were paid — duplicate-send state is § 16.3.'s claim table, and a cron that stops is § 12.4.'s 60-day problem, which is answered outside this app
- **iOS.** Web Push works from 16.4 but **only for a home-screen PWA**, never a Safari tab. No UA sniffing is needed — iOS exposes `PushManager` only in the installed case. Deleting the app from the home screen destroys the subscription without telling the server, which is why `PushSync` re-saves on every launch
- `push_subscriptions` is keyed on the **installation**: `endpoint` is unique table-wide and the upsert moves `user_id`, so two accounts sharing one browser cannot leave the row pushing to the previous account. `ON DELETE cascade` from `users`, plus a `user_id` index for the fan-out
- Env: `NEXT_PUBLIC_VAPID_PUBLIC_KEY`, `VAPID_PRIVATE_KEY`, `VAPID_SUBJECT`. The public key is `NEXT_PUBLIC_` because the browser needs it to subscribe; `ensureEnv` cannot reach it in the client bundle, so a missing key surfaces as the disabled Settings toggle rather than a throw (`AGENTS.md § 6.2.`)
- A `404` or `410` from the push service is **final** — delete the row rather than retrying. `sendPush` never throws; a dead device must not fail the send that triggered it
- **`sent` means the push service accepted the payload, and nothing more.** It does not mean the device received it, showed it, or exists. A phone in a drawer answers `201` for as long as its subscription stands, so `last_success_at` goes on advancing for an installation nobody has opened in a year — which is why an **abandoned device is invisible to the delivery result** and why the prune below keys off launches instead. The other two results are worth stating for the same reason: `gone` is the subscription's own retirement (permission revoked, PWA deleted, endpoint rotated, FCM discarding a registration), and `failed` is everything else — a `401`/`403` VAPID mismatch, a `413` oversized payload, a `429`, a push-service `5xx`, a network error, and, since `configure()` sits inside `sendPush`'s `try`, a missing VAPID variable
- **Devices are retired by disuse, never by accumulated failure.** `pushToUser` partitions its targets on `last_seen_at` and deletes anything older than `ABANDONED_AFTER` (90 days) alongside that send's `410`s, in the same statement — the two mean one thing to this table, an endpoint that will never raise a banner again. **A failure-counting prune was considered and rejected**: the states that produce a sustained run of `failed` are global rather than per-device — a rotated VAPID key, a `.env` deployed without one, a long push-service outage — so a threshold long enough to be meaningful is a threshold that empties the whole table on the day the configuration breaks, and leaves it empty, since the browsers still hold subscriptions and nothing prompts them to re-subscribe. Disuse has no such failure mode: it asks only whether the app was opened
- **The lease is renewed by the launch upsert and by nothing else**, which is what makes `last_seen_at` mean "this installation was opened" rather than "this row was written". That also makes the prune **self-healing** — opening the app on a pruned device re-registers it through the same `PushSync` that re-registers after a `410`, so the cost of retiring one too early is one launch. 90 days is deliberately far beyond any plausible gap: a device opened four times a year is precisely the case push exists for. **No cron and no scheduler** — the prune runs inside the fan-out, which is the only place the waste it removes is actually incurred
- The permission prompt MUST fire inside a user gesture, so `subscribeToPush` awaits nothing before `Notification.requestPermission()`
- **A line with room for words only reads each emoticon as `(이모티콘)`** — this banner, § 8.10.'s quote and § 16.2.'s mirror all take `toMessageSummary`, which is that substitution and nothing else, so a message made of nothing but pictures still says what it is without any of them being looked up. § 8.6.'s excerpt takes `toPlainMessageText` instead, which drops the placeholders outright and then collapses and trims, since a removed one leaves the spaces that surrounded it against each other; text carrying none is returned untouched, so nothing written before the format (§ 6.) is re-spaced
- The banner carries the sender's name **resolved at send time**. This does not violate § 8.7.: a notification is a point-in-time artifact and the worker holds no session to re-resolve one
- One conversation (§ 6.), so every banner collapses onto one `tag` with `renotify`. The tag lives in `sw.js` **alone** — a worker is served raw from `public/` and cannot import `@/shared/config`, so a mirrored constant would be a second source of truth nothing checks. This is why the page sweeps notifications **unfiltered** rather than by tag
- **Entering the app clears Notification Center.** `PushSync` sweeps every delivered banner through `registration.getNotifications()` and `close()`, on mount and on each return to `visible` — the mount alone is not enough, since an installed iOS PWA is resumed rather than reloaded (§ 15.1.) and would sweep exactly once per process. This does not contradict the bullet above: what the platform audits is a `push` event that showed nothing, and a page closing a banner it is not handling a push for is the ordinary use the API exists for. `visibilitychange`, `focus` and `pageshow` are all observed on § 8.4.'s terms — `focus` is the only one a desktop window raised from behind another fires, since it never stopped being `visible` — and collapsed through `SSE_SYNC_COALESCE_WINDOW`. **Every path including the mount is gated on `visibilityState`**: those events fire on the way out too, and a tab restored in the background starts `hidden`, so an unguarded mount would clear the banners of a user who has not arrived. **Not gated on § 8.4.1.'s dormancy** — a departure sleeps, so nearly every return is a dormant one, and the banners belong to a reader who has arrived whatever the gate is doing. Nothing else gates on it either now that it is scoped to 채팅. `getRegistration`, never `register`: a browser that never subscribed holds no banners. **A banner younger than 30 s is left alone** (`BANNER_SETTLE_WINDOW`): that is WebKit's silent-push window, and since bug 309940 also the minimum age at which it honours a `close()` on a persistent notification at all — so the sweep skips them rather than have the refusal logged as a security error on every entry that follows a fresh push
- **Clearing a banner at any moment other than entering the app was built twice and abandoned twice — do not rebuild either.** A close from inside the worker's own `push` event (to stop banners stacking while the app is shut) and a second push retracting a deleted message's banner both shipped and neither worked on macOS or iOS: `getNotifications()` answered `0` against banners plainly present in Notification Center, so nothing reachable from the worker ever identified them. The tag is no fallback — WebKit's tag-coalescing bug is [258922](https://bugs.webkit.org/show_bug.cgi?id=258922), open since 2023 — and the `webpushd` notification implementation that would back `getNotifications` sits behind `HAVE(FULL_FEATURED_USER_NOTIFICATIONS)`, which is iOS-only, so macOS never had that path at all. **The consequence is accepted:** banners accumulate for as long as the app stays shut, a deleted message's banner keeps its text, and the sweep above is what clears the pile — all at once, on the next entry
- `navigator.setAppBadge` carries the recipient's unread count (§ 8.8.), set from both the worker and the shell (`shared/badge`). Absent outside installed PWAs, so every call is optional. The shell **must** rewrite it on resume rather than leaving it to a count change — the worker moves the badge while the page is frozen, and a resume into a count that is already `0` renders no effect
- A subscription the server failed to store reports the toggle as **off**
- **The launch sync and the toggle write the same status, and the later write is not the newer intent.** `serviceWorker.register()` can take seconds, so a toggle can grant permission, subscribe and store the row while the launch sync is still resolving against a permission that was `default` when it sampled it. The hook settles this **by request id** — only the newest claim may write — because the losing order is silent: a stored subscription with the switch showing off and no failure to report
- A refused or dismissed permission prompt **resolves** (`blocked` / `off`) instead of throwing, so turning the toggle on and landing anywhere but `on` raises the `알림을 켜지 못했어요` toast. The toast states only the failure; the recovery step (`브라우저 설정에서 이 사이트의 알림을 허용해 주세요`) belongs to the row description for `blocked` and MUST NOT be duplicated into the toast
- `user_agent` and `last_success_at` are stored for diagnostics and nothing reads them — `last_seen_at` is the one the prune above reads, and the three must not be confused. § 12.'s device list is still the `sessions` one and not this set, but the two are now joined by `session_id` (§ 12.), so a 로그아웃 there retires this row too. The prune stays automatic all the same: it is what covers an installation whose session simply expired, which no screen ever revokes
- **알림 소리 is per installation, and it lives on `push_subscriptions.sound_enabled`** (`NOT NULL DEFAULT true`), never on `users`. The same person carries a phone that should sound and a laptop that should not, which is the same argument the table itself was keyed on. The consequence is deliberate: a preference with **no subscription row has nowhere to live**, so the 알림 소리 switch is disabled whenever 알림 is not `on`, reads `이 기기에서 알림을 켜면 설정할 수 있어요` in that state, and a **relaunch** keeps the value it had — the launch upsert refreshes the keys, `user_agent` and `user_id` and **MUST NOT list `sound_enabled` in its `onConflictDoUpdate` set**, or every launch would silently restore the default. **Turning 알림 off and on again does not keep it**, and that is not the same case: unsubscribing deletes the row and invalidates the endpoint, so the browser mints a new one and the insert lands on the default. Preserving it across that would mean keeping a row for a device that has revoked its subscription, which is the state the `410` prune exists to remove
- **The fan-out serializes per row rather than once.** `pushToUser` selects `sound_enabled` beside the keys and sends each device `silent: !row.soundEnabled`, so one message can sound on the phone and land quietly on the laptop. The row is the only thing that decides it — `PushPayload` carries no `silent` of its own, since a caller that could override the switch is a caller that can silence a device the user asked to hear. **Two bodies at most**, however many devices answer, so they are serialized once and picked per row rather than inside the fan-out
- **`sw.js` sends `silent` to `showNotification` as an explicit boolean on every push, and never omits it.** WebKit's `platformShouldPlaySound` reads an absent value opposite ways per platform — `silent == nullopt || !*silent` on iOS against `silent != nullopt && !*silent` on macOS — so an omitted `silent` means _sound_ on the phone and _silence_ on the desktop. Stating it is what makes one toggle mean one thing on both. Safe beside the collapse above: the spec throws only for `silent: true` **with** `vibrate`, or `renotify: true` **with an empty tag**, and neither is set
- **`POST /api/push/subscription` answers `200 { soundEnabled }`, not the `204` it used to.** The settings screen is a Server Component and cannot know which installation is reading it, so the launch re-registration — already sent on every launch, already identifying the device by endpoint — is where the stored preference comes back. Writing it is a `PATCH` on the same route, `{ endpoint, soundEnabled }`, scoped to the caller's own `user_id` for `DELETE`'s reason and answering `404` when no row of theirs matches. Both Settings rows read one `usePushNotifications` through `PushSettingsProvider`: a hook each would re-register the device twice per mount and let the two switches settle from different responses
- **The last `PushState` is cached in a `push-state` cookie, and the `(main)` layout hands it to `synced-storage` as `ssrCookies`.** Both Settings rows used to open disabled behind 알림 설정을 확인하고 있어요 until the launch sync answered; now they render server-side in their last known position, and the sync — which still runs — corrects a revoked permission or a retired row after the fact. A cookie rather than `localStorage` because that group is already dynamic and `localStorage` is unreadable until hydration, which paints off → on. The layout passes **only** that cookie: `getAll()` would serialize the `httpOnly` session cookie into the RSC payload. A second provider under `GlobalProvider`'s, because the root one also covers `/offline`, which must stay static
- **조용히 보내기 is a per-device toggle on the chat header, `jandh:silent-send`, default off, seeded through the same cookie mechanism as `jandh:side-panel`.** On, `POST /api/messages` withholds the recipient's banner for every send it dispatches for (text, media, emoticon, voice — one route) while the sender's own pushes (a 단축어 공유's 완료 banner) are unaffected. It narrows an `assistant_reply`'s push to the asker alone rather than to nobody (§ 8.15.) — asking is what earns the answer
- **On, the composer says so** (`DESIGN.md § 6.6.`) — a one-line `BellOff` + 조용히 보내기 notice above the pill's header-row slot, coexisting with the staged quote, the edit bar or `AiSelectionBar` rather than taking their place

### 16.2. Offline Mirrors ✅

**Landed.** § 16. made the app survive a cold launch with no network; this makes it _readable_ there. 채팅, 캘린더, 보관함's three shelves and 설정 render from a local snapshot instead of the `/offline` dead end.

- **One URL-inert document, `/offline/shell`, answers all seven paths.** `serveNavigation` matches the pathname against the mirrored set and serves the cached mirror; everything else — `/settings/devices`, `/settings/emoticons`, `/settings/server`, `/login`, anything unrecognised — still gets `/offline`, which keeps its job unchanged. Seven paths for six screens: `/archive` is a prefix with no screen of its own (§ 7.), and online it redirects to the 갤러리 shelf — offline there is no redirect to run, so it is mirrored to that shelf directly rather than left to the dead end. **The address bar is left on the real URL**, and that is the decision rather than a side effect: redirecting to `/offline/shell` would lose which screen the reader asked for, break the back stack, and make 새로고침 land somewhere they never navigated to. The document therefore renders a neutral shell and reads `location.pathname` **in an effect** to decide what to draw. Matched **exactly, never by prefix** — `/settings` mirrors while the three screens nested under it do not, and a `startsWith` test would hand all three a screen with no snapshot behind it
- **The user data lives in IndexedDB, and that is what makes this admissible where § 16. forbids Cache Storage.** The objection there is not freshness, it is that `caches` is keyed by origin and survives a logout — two accounts on one browser. IndexedDB is keyed by `UserId` and cleared on logout, and a write for one user deletes the other's records first. So the _document_ is cached and holds nothing; the _content_ is stored per user and is not in the cache at all. **Nothing may ever be baked into the mirror's HTML.** The last-signed-in `UserId` sits in `localStorage` — § 5.2.'s ban is on auth state, and a snowflake opens nothing
- **The precache is build-time, because on-demand caching cannot boot a route nobody has opened.** `serveImmutable` caches what has been requested, and a never-visited screen has never requested its own chunk — it would serve HTML that cannot hydrate, which for a snapshot-driven mirror is a permanently blank screen. `scripts/generate-offline-precache.ts` runs after `next build` and writes every file under `.next/static/{chunks,media}`. **The whole set, not the subset a prerendered document names**: a chunk reached through `import()` appears in no HTML, so a scraped list boots the mirror and then fails at the first lazy boundary, offline, with nothing reporting it. ~4.3 MB per deploy. The list is data fetched with `cache: "reload"` and never an `importScripts`ed script, for the reason in the bullet below; its `offline-` prefix is load-bearing, being what keeps the path out of `proxy.ts`'s matcher. **A 200 is not a manifest** — a captive portal or a CDN error body parses as JSON too, so the shape is checked, and the two fallback documents are cached before the list is even read rather than beside it, where a malformed body decided by ordering whether they landed. `CACHE_NAME` moves with any of it — **and it carries the build stamp as well, read off the worker's own script URL**, because the hand-bumped literal alone made this an accumulation rather than a replacement. `install` runs on every deploy by design and every build's chunks are new hashed names, so under one fixed name they piled up with nothing removing them, ~4.3 MB a deploy until the origin quota refused a write — swallowed, as every add here is, and first visible months later as a mirror the reader had never opened coming up blank. Stamped, `activate` collects the previous deploy's cache the way it collects any other version
- **A deploy reaches an installed client only because the worker's script URL carries the build.** `sw.js` is served raw from `public/`, so its bytes are identical every deploy and the browser's byte-for-byte check finds nothing to install — and `install` is the only place the precache above runs. Left at that, the online app goes on updating (navigations are network-first) while the mirror stays pinned to whatever build was live when the worker first installed, and no fix to it ever arrives. `push-registration.ts` therefore registers `/sw.js?v={SERVICE_WORKER_VERSION}`, which `next.config.ts` inlines from `VERCEL_DEPLOYMENT_ID`, then `VERCEL_GIT_COMMIT_SHA`, then `BUILD_SHA` — the `Dockerfile` passes that into the **builder** stage for this, the runner's copy being § 15.1.'s and unrelated — so a rebuild of the same commit carries the same stamp and reinstalls nothing. Absent all three it falls back to a timestamp, which reinstalls on every build — the reason the `Dockerfile` line exists rather than a cost worth avoiding. **It cannot ride an `importScripts`ed manifest instead**: WebKit alone skips imported scripts in that byte check, so importing the list would update the worker on Chrome and Firefox and silently never on iOS, which is the platform this app is installed on. **Dev carries no version at all**, only `?nocache=1`, because a registration minted per dev-server start takes § 16.1.'s push subscription with it
- **Two Next 16 constraints, both hard.** Nothing URL-derived may render during hydration — the document was prerendered for `/offline/shell` and is being served at `/chat`, so reading the path at render time is a mismatch for the whole tree. And **no `history.pushState`** to switch screens inside the mirror: the App Router patches both it and `replaceState` to dispatch `ACTION_RESTORE`, which spawns an RSC fetch, fails offline and hard-navigates anyway. Each offline tab switch is therefore a plain `<a href>` and a cache-only reload, exactly as `/offline`'s 다시 시도 already is — which also means `TabBar`, `NavRail` and the 보관함 segments cannot be reused, all reading `usePathname()` at render — the mirror carries `OfflineNavRail` beside `OfflineTabBar` so the `md` geometry (`AGENTS.md § 4.1.`) holds offline too
- **Media bytes are deliberately not cached, and this is not a gap to be closed later.** `/api/media/{id}` 302s into R2 behind a presigned URL that expires, so a cached response is a cached redirect that is dead on arrival; the bytes are also unbounded where the chunk set is fixed. Offline, a photo renders the `media.blurhash` it already carries (§ 9., and `AGENTS.md § 5.3.`) rather than a failing `<img>`, so the grid keeps its shape and its colour. **The colour alone was not enough** — a wash of tinted boxes is indistinguishable from a screen still loading, so the box says `사진을 불러올 수 없어요` under the 오프라인 모드 pill's own `WifiOff`, on the pattern § 8.13.'s tombstone already set. The average is composited under `surface-soft` at 80% rather than worn bare, because a dark photo takes that copy below the contrast floor and there is no second value to fall back to. **A grid tile keeps the icon and drops the sentence**, which survives as its `aria-label`: 보관함 draws nine at once, and one sentence nine times is a wall of type where nine photos were. Voice shows its waveform and cannot play. **A failed `<img>` never retries itself**, so the reconnect bumps a `key` to re-request
- **The mirrored transcript reads § 13.6.'s emoticon map for names and draws no emoticon at all.** An emoticon's asset belongs to the § 13.7. deployment and carries no `blurhash` to fall back through, so unlike a photo there is nothing offline to draw — which is already why an emoticon _message_ renders here as a sized box with an icon. What the map buys is the sentence: a bubble that was nothing but emoticons reads the item's own name rather than 이모티콘 (§ 16.1.), and a mixed one keeps its words with the placeholders removed. **The restore deliberately does not republish into the store the live room reads.** That store has no reader in this tree — the mirror draws its own row rather than `MessageRow` — and it is a module singleton the server shares between requests, so a page render may not write it. **A stored entry may name an item since renamed or deleted** (§ 13.4.), and the cost is bounded to that name: the screen requests no asset, so there is no failed load and no tombstone, and a deleted item still reads what it was called — which is what a picture of the past should say
- **The calendar's D-day is hidden offline rather than recomputed.** § 11.1. resolves it on the server precisely so both users see one number whatever their device clock says; a mirror computing it from a cached `todayKey` would show a number that is wrong and looks authoritative. The 기준 stale label carries the honesty instead
- **Otherwise the mirrored calendar is the live one, row for row.** 다가오는 일정 above the grid with every row the snapshot holds and no 더 보기, the agenda under it with the same event rows (colour dot, scope, memo, author) and static 공휴일/기념일 rows, and a tap opening `EventDetailDialog` with `isReadOnly` — the memo in full, no 일정 관리. The rows live in `widgets/calendar-month` (`AgendaEventRow`, `UpcomingEventRow`, …) precisely so both screens draw one component; a page may not import a sibling page, and the copy the mirror used to carry fell three commits behind. Relative dates and the today cell read the **device** day; a 다가오는 row pointing outside the snapshot's month toasts `OFFLINE_MESSAGES.view` rather than selecting a day the grid cannot show
- **A mirror takes itself off screen when the network comes back; a live screen never does.** The two look alike behind the pill and are opposites underneath. A screen that stayed mounted through the outage recovers on its own — § 8.4.'s reconnect runs `catchUp` and `reconcile`, and the query cache refetches — so reloading it would throw away the composer's draft, the queued outbox and the reader's place for nothing. A mirror recovers from none of that: it holds no stream, and the pill leaving is its only signal, which says the opposite of what the screen is. It is also read-only by construction, so there is nothing to lose and nothing to ask — it reloads. **On a probe rather than on the `online` event**, which is the direction the bullet below distrusts: a `HEAD` of the reader's own path, which `resolveHandler` passes to the network untouched because the worker answers `GET` alone. **No navigation is involved** — the worker left the address bar on the real path, so the live screen is already at this URL. `ok` alone reloads, since a server that is up and failing leaves the reader better off on a snapshot than on its error page, and a `sessionStorage` mark bounds it to one reload per 10s so a network that drops again between the probe and the document cannot loop
- **Detection is `navigator.onLine`, and it is a hint.** The asymmetry is the whole contract: `false` is trustworthy, `true` is not — a LAN with no route out reports online. So **nothing may gate a feature on it**; it may only inform, and every offline refusal is a control saying why rather than a capability withdrawn. Shown after a **1s settle** so a blip cannot flash the pill, and hidden **immediately** on return, because a stale "you are offline" is the worse failure. The `false` is also **corroborated against this app's own requests**, since the stacks that report it wrongly are the ones whose requests keep working: a failure newer than the last success settles it at that 1s, and with neither — a screen nobody has asked anything of — it takes a 30s window of silence instead. A failure may only ever _shorten_ a verdict the flag has already reached and never reach one of its own, because `fetch` rejects with the same `TypeError` for a CORS denial as for a dead network. **The evidence is asked for rather than waited out**: `/api/health` answers 204 and nothing else, and the flag going `false` sends exactly one request to it — a transition, never a poll. That is what settles a reader who was doing something a moment ago, where the window would otherwise run its full 30s with nothing able to shorten it: the stream is not a request, and a query paused by the outage is not a failure. **That route may never grow a database check**, however much it looks like the place for a container probe — it would wake the compute § 8.4.1. exists to let sleep, on a request that fires whenever a connection wobbles, and would report a database outage as a network one. **The SSE stream is not the signal and must not become one**: § 8.4.1. closes it on dormancy, § 8.4. closes it on `visibilitychange` and `blur`, and it is only ever mounted on 채팅 — a closed stream means "backgrounded, dormant, or on another tab" far more often than it means offline, and reading it would pin 오프라인 모드 on the three tabs that never hold one

---

### 16.3. Event Reminders ✅

**Landed.** `ops-remind.yml` runs `scripts/ops/remind.ts` every ten minutes on § 12.4.'s shared runner and raises a banner ahead of an upcoming calendar event.

- **Three thresholds for a timed event — 7일, 1일 and 2시간 before it starts. An all-day event takes the first two only, and takes them at 09:00 `TIME_ZONE` rather than as an offset.** An all-day row stores 00:00 as its start (§ 11.), so subtracting an offset lands at midnight for 1일 and at 22:00 the night before for 2시간 — an hour nobody is being reminded for. `yearly` anniversaries are all-day rows and follow that same rule
- **The state is one column on `events` — `notified_at` — and no table.** The three thresholds only ever fire in order, so the instant itself says how far the sequence has got: a threshold is sent when the stamp `IS NULL OR < that threshold's instant`. **The same comparison is the whole of the recurrence handling** — last year's stamp is earlier than this year's first threshold, so a `yearly` anniversary notifies again with nothing resetting anything and nothing to delete. A threshold is taken by an UPDATE whose own predicate is that guard, so two overlapping runs race on one row and Postgres lets exactly one match; read-then-write would let both read NULL and both send. **Stamped before the push, not after**: a process dying in between loses one banner, where the other order duplicates every banner whenever two runs overlap, which the scheduler does routinely (§ 12.4.)
- **At most one banner per event per run, and it is the latest due threshold.** A backlog — the runner down for a day — would otherwise raise 7일 전 and 1일 전 back to back for an event starting tomorrow. Taking the most recent one collapses that into the only one of them that is still true, and the single column then suppresses the rest for free
- **A threshold already behind when the event was written never fires.** Without it an event added an hour before it starts raises all three banners at once, every one of them being both past and before the start. The creation instant is the id itself (§ 6.), so this costs no column
- **The banner names a clock time, never a countdown — except the 2시간 one, which counts the real remaining minutes.** A scheduled run arrives late and is sometimes skipped (§ 12.4.), so that gap is routinely `2시간 10분` or `1시간 54분` and a fixed figure would simply be wrong. Everything else reads as a date because a notification is a point-in-time artifact (§ 16.1.) read whenever the reader gets to it, and `7일 뒤` baked into one still says `7일 뒤` a week later. The event's title is the notification title and the time phrase is its body, which is also why no `josa` is needed here — nothing is interpolated into a sentence
- **`shared` reaches both participants; `mine` reaches only its author** (§ 11.5.). **There is no per-user switch and none may be added** — § 16.1.'s 알림 toggle is the only alerting control in the app, and a second one that silences a subset of banners is a state in which push is on and a reminder never arrives, with nothing on the screen saying so
- **A reminder is audible**, unlike § 12.4.'s ops banners: it is something the reader asked to be told, so it honours the per-device 알림 소리 rather than overriding it. It links to the day it is about, through `CALENDAR_DAY_PARAM`
- **The reminder is posted into the conversation as well as pushed, at the LAST threshold only** — a `type = 'system'` row carrying `system_action = 'event_reminder'`, drawn by § 11.5.'s pill and linking to the same day. A banner is read once and gone; this is the record of it, and it is what the reader finds when the phone was face-down. **It names the date and never the remaining time**, unlike the banner: the row is scrolled past weeks later, where `2시간 뒤` goes on saying so forever. **It is rendered with no actor** — `sender_id` is the event's author only because the column is `NOT NULL`, and `composeEventNotice` drops the `~님이` prefix for this action, since naming them would say they did something they did not. **Only 2시간 (1일 for an all-day row) posts one**: all three thresholds fire for one event and the row names a date rather than a countdown, so posting each would leave three byte-identical rows a week apart, the first of them saying 다가왔어요 seven days out
- **The claim and the notice are one transaction; the push is outside it.** A stamp that outlived a failed INSERT would be a threshold marked sent with nothing written, and no later run retries it — so the two commit together. The push is not in that transaction: a device that took the banner cannot be un-taken, and rolling back on a recipient who has **no subscription at all** would destroy the record on every run for exactly the reader with no banner to read instead

## 17. Implementation Order ✅

The order the work was taken in, kept as a record. **Every step has landed** — nothing here is a queue any more, and § 18. is the only place with anything still open.

1. ✅ Project setup + FSD scaffolding + design tokens + base components (§ 1.–§ 4.)
2. ✅ Auth + session (§ 5.) — highest-risk area, so it went first
3. ✅ Database schema + migrations (§ 6.)
4. ✅ Layout + tab bar + PWA manifest (§ 7.)
5. ✅ Chat tab (§ 8.) — text and media bubbles, paging, virtualization, optimistic send, SSE, read cursor, replies, the jump machinery, the typing indicator (§ 8.12.), search (§ 8.6.), the date dividers and scroll-to-bottom marking read (§ 8.1.), viewer pinch zoom, and the expired-thumbnail rule (§ 8.9.)
6. ✅ R2 media pipeline + sending photos and videos in chat (§ 9.)
7. ✅ Library tab (§ 10.), all three shelves — the jump-to-message link included
8. ✅ Emoticons (§ 13.)
9. ✅ Calendar tab (§ 11.)
10. ✅ Settings tab (§ 12.) — profile editor, tap-to-enlarge avatar, the 입력 중 표시 switch (§ 8.12.), 로그아웃, both backgrounds (§ 12.1., § 12.2.), the profile screen (§ 12.3.) with its focus trap, the device list, and 화면 테마
11. ✅ Security hardening + deployment (§ 14., § 15.) — the session-check and error-detail sweep, and the deploy-skew signal (§ 15.1.)
12. ✅ Dark theme and offline caching (§ 16.), push notifications (§ 16.1.)

Deliberately left open. When work reaches the feature, **confirm with the user**, then update this document.

**Still open**
